,1);

let

t1

=

sum
(
n1
);

n

+

t1

}

}

\square

4.2
Types and Terms

\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
extends the syntax of
\mathsf{\lambda}

with a few extensions summarized in
fig.

4.1
.

Types

The syntax of types is extended with
the basic type
bool
.

Terms

The syntax of terms is extended in two ways.
First,
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
introduces a
conditional

expression
\texttt{{if}}\;x\;\texttt{{then}}\;{e_{1}}\;\texttt{{else}}\;e_{2}
, that
evaluates to
e_{1}
when
x
evaluates to

\mathit{true}
and to
e_{2}
otherwise.
We require that the condition be a variable

x
instead of an expression for reasons
similar to that of the ANF conversion required
for
Syn-App
from §
3.3.3
.
Second, we introduce a
recursive binder

expression
\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{t_{1}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}
,
where the binder
x
may appear free in
e_{1}
.
Unlike plain let-binders, we require that the types
of such recursive binders be annotated with their
type
t_{1}
.

Basic Types

b

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

…

from
\mathsf{\lambda}
(§
3.1
)

\ \mid\

bool

booleans

Terms

e

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

\ldots

from

\mathsf{\lambda}

\ \mid\

\texttt{{if}}\;x\;\texttt{{then}}\;{e}\;\texttt{{else}}\;e

branches

\ \mid\

\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e}\!:\!{t}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e

recursion

Figure 4.1:
Syntax of Types and Terms

4.3
Declarative Typing

Next, let us consider the rules used to determine whether a term
e

has a given type
t
. As before, we have four kinds of judgments.
The rules for entailment and subtyping are exactly the same
as for
\mathsf{\lambda}
.
However, to support branching, path-sensitivity and recursion, we must
extend the rules that establish the checking and synthesis judgments.

\textrm{{Type Checking}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t$}

\frac{y\ \mbox{is fresh}\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash x\Leftarrow\texttt{bool}\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};{{y}\!:\!{{{\{{{\texttt{{int}}}\!:\!{x}}\}}}}}\vdash e_{1}\Leftarrow t\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};{{y}\!:\!{{{\{{{\texttt{{int}}}\!:\!{\neg x}}\}}}}}\vdash e_{2}\Leftarrow t}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{{if}}\;x\;\texttt{{then}}\;{e_{1}}\;\texttt{{else}}\;e_{2}\Leftarrow t}{{{\textsc{Chk-If}}}}\par\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t_{1}:k\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{t_{1}}}\vdash e_{1}\Leftarrow t_{1}\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{t_{1}}}\vdash e_{2}\Leftarrow t_{2}}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{t_{1}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}\Leftarrow t_{2}}{{{\textsc{Chk-Rec}}}}

Figure 4.2:
Bidirectional Checking: Other rules from
\mathsf{\lambda}
(
fig.

3.6
)

4.3.1
Checking

Conditionals and recursive binders are handled
by the checking rules summarized in
fig.

4.2
.

Conditionals

\texttt{{if}}\;x\;\texttt{{then}}\;{e_{1}}\;\texttt{{else}}\;e_{2}

can be checked to have the type
t
in a context

\mathrm{\Gamma}
when
x
is a
bool
and
both

branches
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
can be checked to
have type
t
(
Chk-If
).
Unlike with classical type checking, we want to
check
e_{1}
(resp.
e_{2}
) in a context
that is extended with the fact that
x

evaluated to
true
(resp.
false
).
Without this extra information, we cannot,
e.g.
, establish
that the body of
not
(
section

4.1
) returns
a boolean
b
that is the logical negation of the input
x
.
The rule
Chk-If
incorporates branch condition
by binding a
fresh
variable
y
to a refinement
that captures the value of the condition
x
.
That is we check the “then” branch
e_{1}
by extending
the context with a binding
{y}\!:\!{{{\{{{\texttt{{int}}}\!:\!{x}}\}}}}

that says that
x
is
\mathit{true}
.
On the other hand, we check the “else” branch
e_{2}
by extending
the context with
{y}\!:\!{{{\{{{\texttt{{int}}}\!:\!{\neg x}}\}}}}
which
records the fact that
e_{2}
is evaluated when
x

is
false
.
The binder
y
is used only to capture the value of the condition
and could be of any type.

Example: Checking
not

Lets see how this method of
branch strengthening

allows us to check the implementation of
not
from

section

4.1
against its specification.
First, rule
Chk-Lam
gives us the following
obligation where the context has only
{x}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}}

from the specification for the input parameter
x
:

{{x}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}}}\vdash{\texttt{{if}}\;x\;\texttt{{then}}\;{\texttt{false}}\;\texttt{{else}}\;\texttt{true}}\Leftarrow{{\texttt{bool}}{{{\{{{b}\!:\!{b\Leftrightarrow\neg x}}\}}}}}

Chk-If
splits the above
into two obligations, for the then- and
else- branch respectively:

\displaystyle{x}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}};\ {y}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{x}\}}}}

\displaystyle\vdash{\texttt{false}}\Leftarrow{{\texttt{bool}}{{{\{{{b}\!:\!{b\Leftrightarrow\neg x}}\}}}}}

\displaystyle{x}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}};\ {y}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\neg x}\}}}}

\displaystyle\vdash{\texttt{true}}\Leftarrow{{\texttt{bool}}{{{\{{{b}\!:\!{b\Leftrightarrow\neg x}}\}}}}}

Chk-Syn
and
Syn-Con
,
using the constant types for
true
and
false
,
reduce the above to the respective subtyping obligations:

\displaystyle{x}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}};\ {y}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{x}\}}}}

\displaystyle\vdash{{\texttt{bool}}{{{\{{{b}\!:\!{\neg b}}\}}}}}\prec:{{\texttt{bool}}{{{\{{{b}\!:\!{b\Leftrightarrow\neg x}}\}}}}}

\displaystyle{x}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}};\ {y}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\neg x}\}}}}

\displaystyle\vdash{{\texttt{bool}}{{{\{{{b}\!:\!{b}}\}}}}}\prec:{{\texttt{bool}}{{{\{{{b}\!:\!{b\Leftrightarrow\neg x}}\}}}}}

Sub-Base
boils the above down
to the subsumption checks:

\displaystyle{x}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}};\ {y}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{x}\}}}}

\displaystyle\vdash\forall{{b}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}}}.\ {\neg b}\Rightarrow{b\Leftrightarrow\neg x}

\displaystyle{x}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}};\ {y}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\neg x}\}}}}

\displaystyle\vdash\forall{{b}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}}}.\ {b}\Rightarrow{b\Leftrightarrow\neg x}

Ent-Ext
turns these into the VCs that are proved valid by SMT:

\displaystyle\forall{{x}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}}}.\ {\mathit{true}}\Rightarrow{}\forall{{y}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}.\ {x}\Rightarrow{}

\displaystyle\forall{{b}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}}}.\ {\neg b}\Rightarrow{b\Leftrightarrow\neg x}

(4.3)

\displaystyle\forall{{x}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}}}.\ {\mathit{true}}\Rightarrow{}\forall{{y}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}.\ {\neg x}\Rightarrow{}

\displaystyle\forall{{b}\!:\!{\texttt{bool}}}.\ {\neg b}\Rightarrow{b\Leftrightarrow\neg x}

(4.4)

Note that validity depends crucially on the
hypotheses
x
and
\neg x
introduced by branch
strengthening. Without those, the VCs would be invalid
and hence
not
would fail to typecheck.

\square

Recursive binders

\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{t_{1}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}

have type
t_{2}
in a context
\mathrm{\Gamma}
if in the context where

x
is also
assumed
to have type
t_{1}
,
(1) the recursive term
e_{1}
can be
guaranteed

to have type
t_{1}
and
(2) the body
e_{2}
can be checked to have type
t_{2}
.
Note that
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
requires explicit type annotations for
recursive binders to facilitate bidirectional checking, so
the rule
Chk-Rec
additionally checks that the
annotation
t_{1}
is well-formed in
\mathrm{\Gamma}
.
(While top-level signatures are invaluable for design and
documentation, we will see how they may be elided via
refinement inference in
chapter

5
.)

Example: Checking
sum

Lets see how the assume-guarantee method allows us to verify
the implementation of
sum
. Let us introduce a few abbreviations:

\displaystyle t_{s}

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0\leq\nu\wedge n\leq\nu}}\}}}}

(4.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{s}

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ {{sum}}\!:\!{{{n}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{t_{s}}};\ {n}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}

(4.6)

\displaystyle e_{s}

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ \mbox{the body of $\texttt{sum}$ }

(4.7)

Chk-Rec
and then
Chk-Lam
get the ball rolling
by yielding the checking obligation:

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{s}\vdash e_{s}\Leftarrow t_{s}

Chk-Let
and then
Chk-If
split the above into two obligations:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{s};{c}\!:\!{{\{{c\Leftrightarrow n\leq 0}\}}};{y}\!:\!{{\{{c}\}}}

\displaystyle\vdash 0\Leftarrow t_{s}

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{s};{c}\!:\!{{\{{c\Leftrightarrow n\leq 0}\}}};{y}\!:\!{{\{{\neg c}\}}}

\displaystyle\vdash e_{2}\Leftarrow t_{s}

(4.9)

where
e_{2}
is the
else
-branch of the body of
sum
.

Chk-Syn
,
Syn-Con
,
Sub-Base
and

Ent-Ext
turn (
4.8
) to the valid VC:

\forall n,c,y,\nu.\ (c\Leftrightarrow n\leq 0)\Rightarrow c\Rightarrow(\nu=0)\Rightarrow(0\leq\nu\wedge n\leq\nu)

(4.10)

Chk-Let
reduces the judgment (
4.9
)
for the
else
branch to the following subtyping obligation
that checks that the value of
n

+

t1
is indeed a subtype of
the return
t_{s}
:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{\Gamma}_{s}^{\prime}}\ \vdash\

\displaystyle{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\nu=n+t_{1}}}\}}}}}\prec:t_{s}

(4.11)

where the context
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{s}^{\prime}
is
\mathrm{\Gamma}
extended
with the
else
-branch condition and bindings for
n_{1}

and
t_{1}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{s}^{\prime}\ \doteq\

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{s};{c}\!:\!{{\{{c\Leftrightarrow n\leq 0}\}}};{y}\!:\!{{\{{\neg c}\}}};

\displaystyle{n_{1}}\!:\!{{\{{n_{1}=n-1}\}}};{t_{1}}\!:\!{{\{{0\leq t_{1}\wedge n_{1}\leq t_{1}}\}}}

In
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{s}^{\prime}
, the binding for
n_{1}
is the output type of
sub

with formals replaced with
n
and
1
. Similarly, the binding for
t_{1}

is the (assumed) output type of
sum
, with the formal
n
replaced
with the actual parameter
t_{1}
.

Sub-Base
and
Ent-Ext
reduce the
above subtyping (
4.11
) to the VC:

\displaystyle\forall

\displaystyle n,c,y,n_{1},t_{1},\nu.

\displaystyle(c\Leftrightarrow n\leq 0)\Rightarrow(\neg c)\Rightarrow(n_{1}=n-1)\Rightarrow(0\leq t_{1}\wedge n_{1}\leq t_{1})\Rightarrow(\nu=n+t_{1})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow(0\leq\nu\wedge n\leq\nu)

(4.12)

which the SMT solver proves valid, guaranteeing that
sum

indeed meets its given specification.

\square

4.3.2
Synthesis

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{self}&:&(X\times T)\rightarrow T\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{self}(x,{b}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{p}}\}}}})&\doteq&{b}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{p\wedge\nu=x}}\}}}}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{self}(x,t)&\doteq&t\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 4.3:
Selfification: Singleton Type Strengthening

Both the new language constructs in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
,

i.e.
branches and recursive binders have checking
judgments.
However, to precisely use the information gleaned
from branch conditions, to enable path-sensitive
verification, we will need to modify the rule for

variables
.

Example: Path Sensitivity

The function
abs
returns the
absolute value of its input
x
.

⬇

val

abs

:

x
:
int

=>

nat
[
v
|
x

\leq

v
]

let

abs

=

(
x
)

=>

{

let

c

=

leq
(0,

x
);

if

(
c
)

{

x

}

else

{

sub
(0,

x
)

};

};

However, note that the
then
branch is simply
x
.
If we directly applied
Syn-Var
from

fig.

3.5
, then the synthesized type
would be
int
, the type that
x
is bound to in
the context, which is clearly not a subtype of
nat
!
Hence, we require another way to type
the variable lookup
x
in a manner that
is precise enough to let us use the branch
condition to prove that the result is a
nat
.

\square

Variables and Selfification

This problem can be solved by the so-called

selfification
rule introduced by
[
Ou2004
]

where the variable
x
is given a singleton type

{b}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\nu=x}}\}}}}
,
i.e.
where
the refinement says the value is equal to
x
.
We formalize this idea in the updated
Syn-Var

in
fig.

4.4
. In context
\mathrm{\Gamma}
, the type
synthesized for
x
is
\mathsf{self}(x,t)
, where

t
is what
x
is bound to in
\mathrm{\Gamma}
.
Figure
4.3
summarizes definition
of
\mathsf{self}
. When invoked on a base type
b

– for which equality is defined in the refinement
logic – the function strengthens the refinement

p
with the singleton conjunct
\nu=x
.
When invoked on other (
e.g.
function) types,
the input is returned as is.

Example: Selfification in
abs

Lets use selfification to verify
abs
. Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{a}

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ {x}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}};\ {c}\!:\!{{\{{c\Leftrightarrow 0\leq x}\}}}

As we’ve seen before with
not
and
sum
,
Chk-Lam
,

Chk-Let
and
Chk-If
produce the two obligations
for the
then
and
else
branches:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{a};\ {y}\!:\!{{\{{c}\}}}

\displaystyle\vdash x\Leftarrow{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0\leq\nu\wedge x\leq\nu}}\}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{a};\ {y}\!:\!{{\{{\neg c}\}}}

\displaystyle\vdash({sub}\ \texttt{0}\ x)\Leftarrow{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0\leq\nu\wedge x\leq\nu}}\}}}}

Chk-Syn
,
Syn-Var
and
Syn-App
reduce
the above to, respectively:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{a};\ {y}\!:\!{{\{{c}\}}}

\displaystyle\vdash{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\nu=x}}\}}}}\prec:{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0\leq\nu\wedge x\leq\nu}}\}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{a};\ {y}\!:\!{{\{{\neg c}\}}}

\displaystyle\vdash{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\nu=0-x}}\}}}}\prec:{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0\leq\nu\wedge x\leq\nu}}\}}}}

Finally,
Sub-Base
and
Ent-Ext
establish the above
by verifying the validity of the VCs:

\displaystyle\forall x,c,y,\nu.\ (c\Leftrightarrow 0\leq x)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow c\Rightarrow(\nu=x)\Rightarrow(0\leq\nu\wedge x\leq\nu)

(4.13)

\displaystyle\forall x,c,y,\nu.\ (c\Leftrightarrow 0\leq x)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\neg c\Rightarrow(\nu=0-x)\Rightarrow(0\leq\nu\wedge x\leq\nu)

(4.14)

Notice that the hypothesis
\nu=x
obtained from
selfification is essential for the validity of the first (
then
)
VC; without it,
i.e.
if we simply gave the term
x
its type from
the context
int
, we would get the
invalid
VC

\displaystyle\forall x,c,y,\nu.\ (c\Leftrightarrow 0\leq x)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow c\Rightarrow\mathit{true}\Rightarrow(0\leq\nu\wedge x\leq\nu)

which would make us foolishly reject
abs
.

\square

\textrm{{Type Synthesis}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Rightarrow t$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma}(x)=t\quad}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash x\Rightarrow\mathsf{self}(x,t)}{{{\textsc{Syn-Var}}}}

Figure 4.4:
Bidirectional Synthesis: Other rules from
\mathsf{\lambda}
(
fig.

3.6
)

4.4
Verification Conditions

Next, lets implement the checking and synthesis rules as a pair
of VC generation functions
\mathsf{check}
and
\mathsf{synth}
.

Checking
Recall that
\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e,\ t)

returns the VC
c
whose validity implies that

\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t
holds (
proposition

3.4.3
).
Figure
4.5
summarizes the new cases
of
\mathsf{check}
for
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
.

For conditional expressions
\texttt{{if}}\;x\;\texttt{{then}}\;{e_{1}}\;\texttt{{else}}\;e_{2}
the VC is
the conjunction of the VCs
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
that are generated
by invoking
\mathsf{check}
on the branches
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
respectively,
and then conditioning the result to track whether the branch condition
was true (
{\{{x}\}}
) or false (
{\{{\neg x}\}}
).
For example,
\mathsf{check}(\emptyset,\ e_{n},\ t_{n})
where

e_{n}
and
t_{n}
are respectively the implementation
and specification of
not
(from
section

4.1
)
returns the VC constraint which is the conjunction of the two
VCs (
4.3
,
4.4
):

\forall x,y,b.\ (x\Rightarrow\neg b\Rightarrow(b\Leftrightarrow\neg x))\ \wedge\ (\neg x\Rightarrow b\Rightarrow(b\Leftrightarrow\neg x))

For recursive binders
\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{t_{1}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}
the VC
is the conjunction of the VCs obtained for
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, both
generated using the environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}
extended by binding
x
to
its specified type
t_{1}
.
For example,
\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma}_{s},\ e_{s},\ t_{s})
, where
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{s}
,

e_{s}
and
t_{s}
are the environment, body and
output type of
sum
as shown in (
4.6
), (
4.7
),
(
4.5
), yields the following VC, which is
the conjunction of the two VCs (
4.10
,
4.12
)

\displaystyle\forall n

\displaystyle,c,y,n_{1},t_{1},\nu.\ (c\Leftrightarrow n\leq 0)\Rightarrow

\displaystyle(c\Rightarrow\nu=0\Rightarrow(0\leq\nu\wedge n\leq v))\ \wedge

\displaystyle(\neg c\Rightarrow n_{1}=n-1\Rightarrow(0\leq t_{1}\wedge n_{1}\leq t_{1})\Rightarrow\nu=n+t_{1}\Rightarrow(0\leq\nu\wedge n\leq\nu))

Synthesis
The synthesis function
\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e)
returns
a pair of a VC
c
and type
t
such that the validity of
c

implies that
\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Rightarrow t
holds (
proposition

3.4.2
).
Figure
4.5
summarizes the updated case for variable lookup
using selfification.
Here, the generated VC is trivial
i.e.

\mathit{true}
, but the synthesized type
is
\mathsf{self}(x,t_{x})
where
t_{x}
is the type that
x

is bound to in the context
\mathrm{\Gamma}
.
For example, using the updated selfified version of
\mathsf{synth}
, the invocation
of
\mathsf{check}(\emptyset,\ e_{a},\ t_{a})
— where
e_{a}
and
t_{a}
are the
implementation and specification of
abs
— yields the following VC which
is the conjunction of the
then
and
else
VCs (
4.13
,
4.14
)

\displaystyle\forall x,c,y,\nu.\ (c\Leftrightarrow 0\leq x)\Rightarrow\

\displaystyle(c\Rightarrow\nu=x\Rightarrow(0\leq\nu\wedge x\leq\nu))\ \wedge

\displaystyle(\neg c\Rightarrow\nu=0-x\Rightarrow(0\leq\nu\wedge x\leq\nu))

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{check}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E\times T)\rightarrow C\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ \texttt{{if}}\;x\;\texttt{{then}}\;{e_{1}}\;\texttt{{else}}\;e_{2},\ t)&\doteq&c_{1}\wedge c_{2}\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad c_{1}&=&({{y::{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{x}\}}}}})\Rightarrow\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e_{1},\ t)\\
\quad\quad c_{2}&=&({{y::{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\neg x}\}}}}})\Rightarrow\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e_{2},\ t)\\
\quad\quad y&=&\mbox{fresh binder}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ \texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{t_{1}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2},\ t)&\doteq&c_{1}\wedge c_{2}\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad c_{1}&=&\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma}_{1},\ e_{1},\ t)\\
\quad\quad c_{2}&=&\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma}_{1},\ e_{2},\ t)\\
\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}_{1}&=&\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{t_{1}}}\\[3.61371pt]
\mbox{\emph{\ldots plus cases from}}\ \mathsf{\lambda}\ (Fig.\penalty\ \ref{fig:one:algo:chk})&&\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{synth}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E)\rightarrow(C\times T)\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ x)&\doteq&(\mathit{true},\ \mathsf{self}(x,t_{x}))\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad t_{x}&=&\mathrm{\Gamma}(x)\\[3.61371pt]
\mbox{\emph{\ldots plus cases from}}\ \mathsf{\lambda}\ (Fig.\penalty\ \ref{fig:one:algo:syn})&&\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 4.5:
Algorithmic Checking for
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}

4.5
Discussion

At this point, we have seen enough to write refinement type
checkers for interesting languages, with functions, branching
and recursion. Lets glance back at the mechanisms that
make verification tick in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
.

Recursion via Assume-Guarantee Reasoning

First, we account for recursion using the
classic
assume-guarantee
method where,
to check
\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{t_{1}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}

we
assume
that the recursive binder
x

has the type
t_{1}
, and then,
guarantee

that fact by checking its implementation
e_{1}

against
t_{1}
.
As in classical Floyd-Hoare logic, this only gives
us a so-called
partial
correctness guarantee;
we will look at verifying
total
correctness
later in
chapter

9
.

Path-Sensitivity via Branch Strengthening

We incorporate path-sensitive reasoning in conditional
expressions
\texttt{{if}}\;x\;\texttt{{then}}\;{e_{1}}\;\texttt{{else}}\;e_{2}
by introducing
a fresh variable (
i.e.

y
in
Chk-If
) and
binding it to a refinement that states that the condition

x
is true (resp. false) when check the
then

branch
e_{1}
(resp.
else
branch
e_{2}
).
We will generalize this strategy to account for user-defined
data-types and pattern matching in
chapter

7
.

Occurence Typing via Selfification

Finally, the presence of branches allows binders to have
strong or more precise types under branches (as in
abs
).
We account for this form of path-sensitive strengthening
by updating the variable lookup rule
Syn-Var

with
selfification
which says the type of
x

is a singleton whose value equals
x

[
Ou2004
]
.
This method, dubbed “occurrence typing” by
[
Komondoor05
]

and
[
Tob08
]
, allows us to then use the rest of the refinement
typing machinery (
e.g.
branch strengthening) to precisely type
each
occurrence
of a variable
x
under different
branches.

Chapter 5
Refinement Inference

Bidirectional typing’s separate checking and synthesis modes
ensure that the programmer need only write type signatures
for functions, after which the refinement checker can
synthesize the types of intermediate sub-expressions
to produce verification conditions for the SMT solver
to validate.
However, as
[
pierce-turner
]
observe,
to make higher-order programming pleasant,
we will want to spare the programmer the
tedium of having to type local function
definitions like those passed as arguments
to
map
or
fold
.
Similarly, to make type polymorphism
(§
6
) usable, we
want to avoid cluttering the code
with explicit (refinement) type
annotations at polymorphic
instantiation sites.
Thus, lets study
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
which
extends
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
with a mechanism
for
inferring
refinements via
the following strategy.

•

Step 1: Types to Templates

First, we generalize refinement type signatures
to allow them to contain
holes
denoting
unknown refinements. Type checking begins by
replacing these holes with
Horn Variables

that represent the unknown refinements.

•

Step 2: VCs to Horn Constraints

Second, we run the VC generation procedures as
described in the preceding chapters.
Now, however, these procedures yield

Horn Constraints
which are VCs
containing Horn (Variable) applications
in addition to predicates.

•

Step 3: VC Validity to Horn Solving

Third, instead of asking an SMT solver to determine
the validity of a VC, we will invoke a Horn Solver
that will perform a
fixpoint
computation to
determine whether there are refinements that can
be substituted for the Horn variables that make
the resulting VCs valid.

5.1
Examples

Before plunging into the formal
details of
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
, lets build
up some intuition by studying how
the three-step strategy plays out
on an example.

Encoding Assertions

Many languages have some form of
assertion

statement which allows the programmer to test,
typically at run-time, that some condition holds
and to halt execution otherwise.
The following
assert
function allows
the programmer to write such
assertions

but the refined (input) type ensures that
any client that calls
assert
(
cond
)
only
typechecks if the refinement type checker
can verify that
cond

always

evaluates to
true
at run-time.

⬇

val

assert

:

bool
[
b
|
b
]

=>

int

let

assert

=

(
b
)

=>

{

0

};

Recall the
abs
function from §
4.3.2

whose type signature has been deliberately elided

⬇

let

abs

=

(
x
)

=>

{

let

c

=

leq
(0,

x
);

if

(
leq
(0,

x
))

{

x

}

else

{

sub
(0,

x
)

};

};

Finally, consider
main
that calls

abs
and
assert
s that the returned
value is non-negative:

⬇

val

main

:

int

=>

int

let

main

=

(
y
)

=>

{

let

z

=

abs
(
y
);

let

c

=

leq
(0,

z
);

assert
(
c
)

}

Recap: Verification Conditions

Suppose that we are given a type signature
for
abs
, for example

⬇

val

abs

:

x
:
int

=>

int
[
v
|0

\leq

v
]

Then the type checker from
chapter

4

would produce the VC:

\begin{array}[]{rrllllr}&\forall{x},{c},\nu.&({c}\Leftrightarrow 0\leq{x})&\Rightarrow{c}&\Rightarrow\nu={x}&\Rightarrow 0\leq\nu&\quad\quad{(a)}\\
&&&\wedge\neg{c}&\Rightarrow\nu=0-{x}&\Rightarrow 0\leq\nu&\quad\quad{(b)}\\
\wedge&\forall{y},{z},{c},{b}.&\lx@intercol 0\leq{z}\Rightarrow({c}\Leftrightarrow 0\leq{z})\Rightarrow({b}\Leftrightarrow{c})\hfil\lx@intercol&\Rightarrow{b}&\quad\quad{(c)}\\
\end{array}

The first two conjuncts of the VC arise from verifying
that the implementation of
abs
satisfies the output
the above signature,
i.e.
its specified post-condition.
The conjuncts respectively state that in the
then

(conjunct
(a)
) and
else
(conjunct
(b)
) branches,
the output value
\nu
must be non-negative.
The last conjunct comes from checking the call to

assert

i.e.
verifying that the boolean value

c
that
assert
is invoked on, is indeed
always
true
.
Note that the third conjunct
uses

(1) the output type of
abs
to assume
that
z
is non-negative,
(2) the output type of the primitive
leq

which we typed as (§
4.1
)

\mathsf{prim}({leq})\ \doteq\ {{{x}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{{y}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{bool}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\nu\Leftrightarrow x\leq y}}\}}}}}}}

to assume that
c
is
true
if and only if
z
is non-negative.
The above assumptions suffice to prove that
the
assert
’s input
b
(which at this
call-site is
c
) is indeed always
true
.

5.1.1
Step 1: Holes and Templates

Suppose that we wrote the
following specification where
\star
denotes a
refinement-hole
:
an unknown refinement that we want
to
infer

⬇

val

abs

:

x
:
int

=>

int
[
\star
]

Readers may be reminded of Haskell’s
notion of a
type-hole
which
allows programmers to partially
specify type signatures that can
then be automatically filled by
type inference
[
padl2014
]
.
The key trick to filling refinement
holes is to generalize VCs to constraints
containing Horn applications that
represent unknown refinements.
To do so, every type signature with
a hole is transformed into a
template

containing (distinct) Horn variables
that represent the unknown refinements.

Example: Template for
abs

For example, the signature for
abs
yields the template:

\texttt{abs}\ :\ {{x}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({x,\nu})}}\}}}}}

(5.1)

where
\kappa
is a
Horn variable

such that
\kappa({z_{1},z_{2}})
denotes
an (unkown) refinement (relation) over the
Horn variable’s
parameters

z_{1}
and
z_{2}
.

\square

5.1.2
Step 2: Horn Constraints

Next, we use the templates to run exactly the
same VC generation procedure as before. However,
instead of producing a VC we get a
Horn

constraint which is a VC with Horn variable
applications appearing at various positions.

Example: Constraints for
abs

For example, if we run
\mathsf{check}
on the above
code with
abs
and
main
, but using the
template (
5.1
) as the specification
for
abs
, we get the Horn constraint:

\begin{array}[]{rrllllr}&\forall{x},{c},\nu.&({c}\Leftrightarrow 0\leq{x})&\Rightarrow{c}&\Rightarrow\nu={x}&\Rightarrow\kappa({{x},{\nu}})&\quad\quad{(a^{\prime})}\\
&&&\wedge\neg{c}&\Rightarrow\nu=0-{x}&\Rightarrow\kappa({{x},{\nu}})&\quad\quad{(b^{\prime})}\\
\wedge&\forall{y},{z},{c},{b}.&\lx@intercol\kappa({{y},{z}})\Rightarrow({c}\Leftrightarrow 0\leq{z})\Rightarrow({b}\Leftrightarrow{c})\hfil\lx@intercol&\Rightarrow{b}&\quad\quad{(c^{\prime})}\end{array}

(5.2)

Notice that this constraint is mostly identical
to the VC shown above, with three conjuncts
(a)
,

(b)
and
(c)
, except that instead of:
(1) the consequent
0\leq\nu
that appears
in the conjuncts
(a)
and
(b)
stipulating
that the output value
\nu
is non-negative,
we have the Horn application
\kappa({{x},\nu})

representing the output
\nu
is related to
the input
{x}
via an (as yet not known)
refinement
\kappa
, and
(2) the assumption
0\leq{z}
that appears
as a hypothesis in
(c)
stating that
{z}

is non-negative, we have the Horn application

\kappa({{y},{z}})
that says
that the value of
{z}
is related
to that of (the argument)
{y}
by
the as yet unknown refinement
\kappa
.

\square

5.1.3
Step 3: Horn Solving

At this point, we cannot ask an SMT solver
to simply check the validity of a VC, as the
constraints contain unknown Horn relations.
Instead we invoke a
Horn solver

to determine whether
there exist

a satisfying assignment for the Horn variables.
A Horn
assignment
is a mapping of Horn
variables to refinement predicates over the
Horn variables’ parameters.
An assignment
satisfies
a Horn
constraint if the result of substituting
the horn variables with their assignments
yields a
valid
(Horn-variable free)
formula.

Example: Solution for
abs

For example
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma_{3}

are three possible assignments for
\kappa
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(\kappa)({z_{1},z_{2}})

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ z_{1}\leq z_{2}

(5.3)

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}(\kappa)({z_{1},z_{2}})

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ 0<z_{2}

(5.4)

\displaystyle\sigma_{3}(\kappa)({z_{1},z_{2}})

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ 0\leq z_{2}

(5.5)

The assignment
\sigma_{1}
(
5.3
)
does not satisfy the Horn constraint (
5.2
)
as substitution yields the following VC
whose last conjunct is invalid:

\begin{array}[]{rrllllr}&\forall{x},{c},\nu.&({c}\Leftrightarrow 0\leq{x})&\Rightarrow{c}&\Rightarrow\nu={x}&\Rightarrow{{x}\leq{\nu}}&\quad\quad(\mbox{✓})\\
&&&\wedge\neg{c}&\Rightarrow\nu=0-{x}&\Rightarrow{{x}\leq{\nu}}&\quad\quad(\mbox{✓})\\
\wedge&\forall{y},{z},{c},{b}.&\lx@intercol{{y}\leq{z}}\Rightarrow({c}\Leftrightarrow 0\leq{z})\Rightarrow({b}\Leftrightarrow{c})\hfil\lx@intercol&\Rightarrow{b}&\quad\quad(\mbox{✗})\\
\end{array}

Assignment
\sigma_{2}
(
5.4
) also
fails to satisfy the constraint (
5.2
)
as substituting it yields the following VC
whose first conjunct is invalid:

\begin{array}[]{rrllllr}&\forall{x},{c},\nu.&({c}\Leftrightarrow 0\leq{x})&\Rightarrow{c}&\Rightarrow\nu={x}&\Rightarrow{0<{\nu}}&\quad\quad(\mbox{✗})\\
&&&\wedge\neg{c}&\Rightarrow\nu=0-{x}&\Rightarrow{0<{\nu}}&\quad\quad(\mbox{✓})\\
\wedge&\forall{y},{z},{c},{b}.&\lx@intercol{0<{z}}\Rightarrow({c}\Leftrightarrow 0\leq{z})\Rightarrow({b}\Leftrightarrow{c})\hfil\lx@intercol&\Rightarrow{b}&\quad\quad(\mbox{✓})\\
\end{array}

However, assignment
\sigma_{3}
(
5.5
)

does
satisfy the Horn constraint (
5.2
)
as substitution produces the
valid
VC

\begin{array}[]{rrllllr}&\forall{x},{c},\nu.&({c}\Leftrightarrow 0\leq{x})&\Rightarrow{c}&\Rightarrow\nu={x}&\Rightarrow{0\leq{\nu}}&\quad\quad(\mbox{✓})\\
&&&\wedge\neg{c}&\Rightarrow\nu=0-{x}&\Rightarrow{0\leq{\nu}}&\quad\quad(\mbox{✓})\\
\wedge&\forall{y},{z},{c},{b}.&\lx@intercol{0\leq{z}}\Rightarrow({c}\Leftrightarrow 0\leq{z})\Rightarrow({b}\Leftrightarrow{c})\hfil\lx@intercol&\Rightarrow{b}&\quad\quad(\mbox{✓})\\
\end{array}

Thus, we can fill the refinement-holes with the
satisfying assignment to infer signatures that
yield a well-typed program. For example, plugging

\sigma_{3}
into the template for
abs
yields
the “hand-written” signature

\texttt{abs}\ :\ {{x}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0\leq\nu}}\}}}}}

(5.6)

that let us verify
main
.

\square

5.2
Types and Terms

Next, we formalize our three-step strategy in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
whose
syntax is summarized in
fig.

5.1
.

Predicates

We extend the grammar of refinement predicates
(
fig.

2.1
) to include Horn applications
of the form
\kappa({\overline{x}})
where

\overline{x}
abbreviates a sequence of
variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.
A Horn application
\kappa({\overline{x}})
denotes
an
unknown
predicate (or relation) over the
variables
\overline{x}
.

Refinements

Thus,
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
has two kinds of refinements.
The first are
known
refinements (or just, refinements)

{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{p}}\}}}
, made up of predicates
p
as before.
The second are refinement
holes
(or just, holes)

{\{{\star}\}}
which can appear in type annotations, and which
denote an unknown refinement that the programmer has chosen
to elide.

Holes vs. Horn applications

In
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
, Horn-applications do not appear
in the
external
surface syntax,
i.e.
in
type annotations. Instead, the programmer elides
refinements in annotations using holes.
During type checking we will replace all holes
with Horn applications. That is, dually, holes
do not appear in the
internal
typing
derivations.

Predicates

p

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

\ldots

from
fig.

2.1

\ \mid\

\kappa({\overline{x}})

horn application

Refinements

r

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{p}}\}}

known

\ \mid\

\{{\star}\}

hole

Figure 5.1:
Syntax of Predicates and Refinements

5.3
Declarative Typing

Next, lets see how a term
e
that may be annotated
with refinement holes
{\{{\star}\}}
can be verified
to have a type
t
.
The vast majority of the rules, in particular,
the rules for well-formedness, subtyping and
entailment, remain unchanged from
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
.
However, we will introduce a new
instantiation

judgment that stipulates how holes can be filled by
refinements. We will then use the instantiation
judgment to eliminate holes in the two rules
that pertain to type annotations.

5.3.1
Instantiation

The instantiation judgment
s\triangleright t
states
that a type
s
can be
instantiated to
a type

t
by replacing the refinement holes in
s
with
suitable concrete refinements.
This intuition is formalized by the three rules summarized
in
fig.

5.2
:

Ins-Hole
, which describes how a single hole is instantiated,

Ins-Conc
, which states the concrete refinements are left unmodified, and

Ins-Fun
, which describes the component-wise instantiation of function types.

Lets focus on two important aspects of the instantiation judgment.
First, the rules ensure that if
s\triangleright t

then there are no holes left in
t
.
Second, the judgment is
declarative
, it does not
tell us
how
to find suitable concrete refinements.
Instead the rules tell us
what
valid concrete
refinements should look like for the program to be
well-typed.

Example: Instantiating holes in
abs

The above rules establish that

{{x}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\star}\}}}}\quad\triangleright\quad{{x}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0\leq\nu}}\}}}}}

i.e.
the partial type signature for
abs
from (
5.1
) can
be instantiated to the concrete type in (
5.6
).

\square

\textrm{{Hole Instantiation}}\hfill\framebox{$s\triangleright t$}

\par\frac{\quad}{{b}{{\{{\star}\}}}\triangleright{b}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}}{{{\textsc{Ins-Hole}}}}\par\par\par\frac{\quad}{{b}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}\triangleright{b}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}}{{{\textsc{Ins-Conc}}}}\par\par\frac{s_{1}\triangleright s_{2}\quad t_{1}\triangleright t_{2}}{{{x}\!:\!{s_{1}}}\rightarrow{t_{1}}\triangleright{{x}\!:\!{s_{2}}}\rightarrow{t_{2}}}{{{\textsc{Ins-Fun}}}}\par

Figure 5.2:
Hole Instantiation

5.3.2
Checking

We need only alter the
checking
rules
in one place: the
Chk-Rec
judgment
which deals with the annotations for recursive
binders
\texttt{{rec}}\;x_{1}={e_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}
.
The updated
Chk-Rec
rule is shown
in
fig.

5.3
.
(All the other checking rules from
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
,
shown in
fig.

4.2
, carry over to

\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
.)
Instead of using the user-specified annotation

s_{1}
which may contain holes, we use
t_{1}
,
which is an instantiation of
s_{1}
that is
guaranteed to be free of holes.

5.3.3
Synthesis

Similarly, we need only modify the one
synthesis
rule
that deals with type annotations, namely, rule
Syn-Ann

which types the annotation terms
{e}\!:\!{s}
.
The new rule is shown in
fig.

5.3
.
(All the other synthesis rules from
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
,
summarized in
fig.

4.2
, apply unchanged,
to
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
.)
Again, as the annotated type
s
may have holes,
we first instantiate it to
t
and then proceed,
as in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
, pretending that the annotation was

t
all along.

\textrm{{Type Checking}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t$}

\frac{s_{1}\triangleright t_{1}\and\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t_{1}:k_{1}\and\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{t_{1}}}\vdash e_{1}\Leftarrow t_{1}\and\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{t_{1}}}\vdash e_{2}\Leftarrow t_{2}}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}\Leftarrow t_{2}}{{{\textsc{Chk-Rec}}}}

\textrm{{Type Synthesis}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Rightarrow t$}

\frac{s\triangleright t\and\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t:k\and\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash{e}\!:\!{s}\Rightarrow t}{{{\textsc{Syn-Ann}}}}

Figure 5.3:
Bidirectional Checking and Synthesis: other rules from
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
(
fig.

4.2
)

5.4
Verification Conditions

The declarative typing rules require
an oracle to magically
guess

refinements for the holes, and then

verify
those guesses.
Next, lets see how Horn constraints
let us
automate
the process
of guessing suitable instantiations.
This approach, introduced by

[
LiquidPLDI08
]
, has three steps.

1.

Templates:
First, we generate
templates

with
Horn applications

\kappa({x})

that represent the unknown refinements;

2.

Horn Constraints:
When the type annotations
contain templates, the VC generation procedure returns

Horn constraints
that circumscribe the possible
concrete refinements that would make the program well-typed;

3.

Horn Solving
Finally, we solve the Horn constraints
to find either a suitable instantiation for the holes that
demonstrates the program is well-typed, or otherwise reject
the program as ill-typed, if no such solution can be found.

Next, lets formalize each of these steps.

5.4.1
Instantiating Holes with Templates

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{fresh}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times T)\rightarrow T\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ {b}{{\{{\star}\}}})&\doteq&{b}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu,\overline{x}})}}\}}}}\\[3.61371pt]
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad\kappa&=&\mbox{fresh horn variable of sort}\ b\times\overline{t}\\
\quad\quad\nu&=&\mbox{fresh binder}\\
\quad\quad\overline{{x}\!:\!{t}}&=&\mathrm{\Gamma}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ {b}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{p}}\}}}})&\doteq&{b}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ {{x}\!:\!{s}}\rightarrow{t})&\doteq&{{x}\!:\!{s^{\prime}}}\rightarrow{t^{\prime}}\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad s^{\prime}&=&\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ s)\\
\quad\quad t^{\prime}&=&\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{s}},\ t)\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 5.4:
Generating fresh templates

Recall from
fig.

5.1
, that in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
the
language of predicates includes Horn applications
\kappa({\overline{x}})
.

Well-formedness
A predicate
p
is
well-formed if
p
has no horn-applications,
or is of the form
p^{\prime}\wedge\kappa({\overline{x}})

where
p^{\prime}
is well-formed.
This particular syntactic requirement
on predicates ensures that the resulting
constraints are indeed Horn constraints,
and hence can be solved to determine
typeability.

Templates
A
template

is a type where all the refinements
are well-formed predicates,
i.e.
whose
refinements are all of the form

p\wedge_{i}\kappa_{i}({x_{i}})

where
p
has no Horn applications.

Instantiation

Instantiation arises in two crucial rules:

Chk-Rec
and
Syn-Ann
.
In both cases, we require that if
s\triangleright t

then the instantiated
t
be well-formed in the
environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}
.
The procedure
\mathsf{fresh}
summarized
in
fig.

5.4
captures
this requirement in an algorithmic
manner:

\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ s)
returns
a
template

t
such that
for every
assignment
for the
Horn variables, the type obtained by
applying the assignment to
t

is guaranteed to be well-formed under

\mathrm{\Gamma}
.

The first case (corresponding to
Ins-Hole
)
instantiates a hole
{\{{\star}\}}
with a Horn application

\kappa({\nu,\overline{x}})
where
\nu
is a
fresh symbol denoting the value being refined, and

\kappa
is a fresh Horn variable denoting an (unknown)
relation over the variables in the the environment

\mathrm{\Gamma}
and
\nu
.
The second case (corresponding to
Ins-Conc
)
returns the concrete refinement unmodified, and
the third case (corresponding to
Ins-Fun
)
recurses on the function’s input and output types,
adding the input binder to the environment used to
instantiate the output, to let the output refinement

depend upon
the input.
For example, to generate a template from the partial
type annotation for
abs
, we would invoke:

\mathsf{fresh}(\emptyset,\ {{x}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\star}\}}}})

which would then return the template from (
5.1
)

{{x}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({x,\nu})}}\}}}}}

5.4.2
Horn Constraints

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{check}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E\times T)\rightarrow C\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ \texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2},\ t)&\doteq&c_{1}\wedge c_{2}\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad c_{1}&=&\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma}_{1},\ e_{1},\ t)\\
\quad\quad c_{2}&=&\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma}_{1},\ e_{2},\ t)\\
\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}_{1}&=&\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{t_{1}}}\\
\quad\quad t_{1}&=&\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ s_{1})\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{synth}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E)\rightarrow(C\times T)\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ {e}\!:\!{s})&\doteq&(c,\ t)\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad c&=&\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e,\ t)\\
\quad\quad t&=&\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ s)\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 5.5:
Horn Verification Condition Generation for
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
, extends cases of
fig.

4.5

In
fig.

5.5
we extend the VC generation procedure
to use
\mathsf{fresh}
to generate templates from partial types.

Checking

Procedure
\mathsf{check}
is modified only for the case where it
handles annotated terms
\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}
.
Now, we use
\mathsf{fresh}
to generate a template
t_{1}

for the annotation
s_{1}
, after which we generate
constraints as in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
(
fig.

4.2
)
assuming the annotation was
t_{1}
.

Synthesis

Similarly, procedure
\mathsf{synth}
is modified only for the case
where it handles annotated terms
{e}\!:\!{s}
.
Again, we use
\mathsf{fresh}
to get a template
t

for the annotation
s
, and then proceed as in

\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
(
fig.

4.4
).

We encourage the reader to confirm that when run
on the code with
abs
and
main
and with
the annotation
{{x}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\star}\}}}}

for
abs
, the VC generation procedure
\mathsf{check}

returns the Horn constraint
eq.

5.2
.

5.5
Solving Horn Constraints

Finally, to determine whether the program is typable,
we need to
solve
the Horn constraints produced
by VC generation.

5.5.1
Constraint Satisfaction

Assignments

Recall that a Horn
assignment

\sigma

is a mapping of Horn variables
\kappa
to
SMT predicates (relations) over the Horn
variables’ parameters.
We
apply
an assignment
\sigma

to a predicate and constraint by
replacing

each Horn application
\kappa({y})
with its solution

{\sigma(\kappa)}[\overline{x}:=\overline{y}]

where
\overline{x}
are the parameters
of the Horn variable
\kappa
.

Satisfaction

An assignment
\sigma

satisfies
a Horn
constraint
c
if applying the assignment
to the constraint yields a
valid

Horn-variable free formula,
i.e.
if

\mathsf{SmtValid}(\sigma(c))
.
A Horn constraint
c
is
satisfiable

if there
exists
an assignment
\sigma

that satisfies
c
.

If the VC generation procedure yields a satisfiable
constraint, then the program is well-typed.

Proposition 5.5.1
.

If
\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e,\ t)
is Horn satisfiable,
then
\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t
.

5.5.2
Computing Satisfiability via Predicate Abstraction

The
LiquidHaskell
verifier uses its own Horn constraint
solver that is based on predicate abstraction

[
GrafSaidi97
]
, in particular, the Houdini
algorithm
[
flanagan01houdini
]
, extended
with optimizations that enable precise

local
type inference
[
LiquidPLDI08
,
CosmanICFP17
]
.
This technique is summarized as the procedure

\mathsf{solve}
shown in
section

5.5.2
.

\mathsf{solve}(c,\mathbb{Q})
takes as input
a Horn constraint
c
and set of
candidate atomic predicates or

qualifiers

\mathbb{Q}
.
The procedure returns SAT iff there
exists an satisfying assignment for

c
that maps each
\kappa
to a

conjunction
of atomic predicates
from
\mathbb{Q}
, and satisfies
c
.
The procedure has two essential elements.

1. Flatten

First, we convert the Horn constraint
c
into
a set of
flat
constraints
{cs}
each of
which is of the form

\forall{\overline{{x}\!:\!{t}}}.\ {p}\Rightarrow{p^{\prime}}

where the
head

p^{\prime}
is either:
(1) a single Horn
application

\kappa({\overline{y}})
, or,
(2) a Horn-variable free
concrete
predicate.
The subset of
{cs}
that have (resp. do not have)
Horn applications in the head are gathered into the
set
{cs}_{\kappa}
(resp.
{cs}_{p}
).
We then invoke a the procedure
\mathsf{fixpoint}

to find a solution
\sigma
for the application
constraints
{cs}_{\kappa}
, and
\mathsf{solve}

returns
Sat
iff
\sigma
also satisfies
the concrete constraints
{cs}_{p}
.

2. Fixpoint

The assignment that maps each
\kappa

to the relation
\mathit{true}
suffices to satisfy
the application constraints, but of course,
this assignment may not satisfy the
concrete constraints.
Instead, we start with an
initial

solution
\sigma_{0}
that maps each Horn
variable
\kappa
to the conjunction of

all
the candidate predicates in

\mathbb{Q}
.

Any
solution that that maps Horn
variables to conjunctions over
\mathbb{Q}

is trivially
weaker
than
\sigma_{0}
.
Next,
\mathsf{fixpoint}
iteratively

weakens
the candidate solution

\sigma
by
(1)
choosing
some constraint
c
not satisfied
by
\sigma
,
i.e.
where
\sigma(c)
is
is
not valid
, and
(2)
removing
qualifiers from the
\kappa

at the head
c
, and
(3)
iterating
the above process until all
the application constraints
{cs}_{\kappa}
are
satisfied.

The
\sigma
computed by
\mathsf{fixpoint}

is guaranteed to be the
strongest

conjunction of candidate qualifiers that
satisfies the application constraints

{cs}_{\kappa}
.
Hence, if this
\sigma

also
satisfies
the concrete constraints
{cs}_{p}
then
it satisfies
c
and
\mathsf{solve}

returns
Sat
.
Instead, if
\sigma
does not satisfy

{cs}_{p}
, we can be sure there
is no satisfying assignment for
c

over conjunctions of
\mathbb{Q}
, and so

\mathsf{solve}
returns
Unsat

[
LiquidPLDI08
]
.

Proposition 5.5.2
.

If
\mathsf{solve}(\mathbb{Q},\ \mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e,\ t))=\textsc{SAT}
,
then
\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{solve}&:&(C\times[P])\rightarrow\textsc{Sat}+\textsc{Unsat}\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{solve}(\mathbb{Q},c)&\doteq&\mbox{if}\ \mathsf{SmtValid}(\sigma({cs}_{p}))\ \mbox{then}\ \textsc{Sat}\ \mbox{else}\ \textsc{Unsat}\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad{cs}&=&\mathsf{flat}(c)\\
\quad\quad{cs}_{\kappa}&=&\{c\ \mid\ c\in{cs},c\equiv\forall{{\overline{x}}\!:\!{\overline{t}}}.\ {p}\Rightarrow{\kappa({y})}\}\\
\quad\quad{cs}_{p}&=&\{c\ \mid\ c\in{cs},c\not\equiv\forall{{\overline{x}}\!:\!{\overline{t}}}.\ {p}\Rightarrow{\kappa({y})}\}\\
\quad\quad\sigma_{0}&=&\lambda\kappa.\wedge\{q\ \mid\ q\in\mathbb{Q}\}\\
\quad\quad\sigma&=&\mathsf{fixpoint}({cs}_{\kappa},\sigma_{0})\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{flat}&:&C\rightarrow[C]\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{flat}(c)&\doteq&\{\mathsf{simpl}(\emptyset,\mathit{true},c^{\prime})\ \mid\ c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{split}(c)\}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{split}(p)&\doteq&\{p\}\\
\mathsf{split}(c_{1}\wedge c_{2})&\doteq&\mathsf{split}(c_{1})\cup\mathsf{split}(c_{2})\\
\mathsf{split}(\forall{{x}\!:\!{t}}.\ {p}\Rightarrow{c})&\doteq&\{\forall{{x}\!:\!{t}}.\ {p}\Rightarrow{c^{\prime}}\ \mid\ c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{split}(c)\}\\[7.22743pt]
\mathsf{simpl}(\overline{{x}\!:\!{t}},p,\forall{{x}\!:\!{t}}.\ {q}\Rightarrow{c})&\doteq&\mathsf{simpl}(\overline{{x}\!:\!{t}};{x}\!:\!{t},p\wedge q,c)\\
\mathsf{simpl}(\overline{{x}\!:\!{t}},p,q)&\doteq&\forall{\overline{{x}\!:\!{t}}}.\ {p\Rightarrow q}\\[7.22743pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{fixpoint}&:&([C]\times\Sigma)\rightarrow\Sigma\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{fixpoint}({cs},\sigma)&\doteq&\mbox{case}\ \{c\mid c\in{cs},\ \mbox{not}\ \mathsf{SmtValid}(\sigma(c))\}\ \mbox{of}\\
&&\quad\emptyset\rightarrow\sigma\\
&&\quad c:\_\rightarrow\mathsf{fixpoint}({cs},\mathsf{weaken}(\sigma,c))\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{weaken}(\sigma,\forall{\overline{{x}\!:\!{t}}}.\ {p\Rightarrow\kappa({y})})&\doteq&{\sigma}[\kappa:={qs^{\prime}}]\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad{qs^{\prime}}&=&\{q\ \mid\ q\in\sigma(\kappa)\ \mbox{s.t.}\ \mathsf{keep}(q)\}\\
\quad\quad\mathsf{keep}(q)&\doteq&\mathsf{SmtValid}(\forall{\overline{{x}\!:\!{t}}}.\ {\sigma(p)\Rightarrow q(y)})\\
\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

5.6
Discussion

[
PierceLICS03
]
observes that “the more interesting
your types get, the less fun it is to write them down.”
In this chapter, we saw how the programmer
can elide refinement annotations and instead,
let the type checker carry out the tedious task
of writing them down.
To do so, we introduced
Horn variables

to represent the unknown refinements, converting
the Verification Conditions into
Horn constraints

whose satisfying assignments show the program is well-typed.

Tools for Horn Constraint Satisfiability

In addition to the simple algorithm based
on predicate abstraction
[
Jhala2018
]

shown above, there is a rich literature on
techniques for solving Horn constraints
that is comprehensively surveyed by

[
BjornerHorn
]
.
Many of these ideas are based on the notion
of iteratively refining solutions using
refutations and are implemented in
different tools including Spacer

[
spacer
]
, Eldarica
[
eldarica
]
,
and even directly within some SMT solvers
like Z3
[
z3PDR
,
z3horn
]
.

[
Jagannathan18
]
describes a Horn constraint
solver that combines the refutation-guided approach
with machine learning over sample data-values that
either belong within or without the constrained
relations.
Many of the above solvers are publicly available
and compete regularly in an open competition

[
ChcComp
]
that aims to benchmark
and improve the solvers.

Finding Candidate Qualifiers

In practice we have found predicate abstraction
to be particularly effective. Though the other
approaches are fully automatic,
i.e.
do not
require qualifiers or templates, the general
problem of inferring solutions is undecidable
of course, and the solvers can easily diverge
when searching for suitable predicates or relations,
thereby making the end-to-end verification
quite brittle
[
jhalamcmillan06
]
.
In contrast, the
\mathsf{solve}
algorithm is
parameterized by the set of qualifiers
\mathbb{Q}

which should be thought of as a set of
candidate

fragments from which refinements should be
synthesized, thereby bounding the space of
candidate refinements, and ensuring that
the solver quickly
terminates
, which
is essential for predictable verification.
One natural source of candidates are all
the atomic predicate fragments that appear
inside type annotations written by the programmer.
Experience shows that this simple heuristic
suffices to automatically infer refinements
in practice
[
Seidel14
]
.

No Principal Types

In some settings, inference
can produce ideal results,
such as the
principal

types of
[
Hindley69
,
Dam82
]
.
Unfortunately (with apologies to Pierce),
the more interesting your types get the
less principal they become.
That is, we do not know of any reasonable
definition of an
ideal
refinement
type for a function.
The reason is that refinements are expressive

contracts
about how functions
should

be used.
One can imagine many different
and
incomparable
contracts
that
e.g.
restrict the space of
possible inputs to provide
more precise guarantees
about the
outputs

of a function.
Thus, really the only
ideal

specification would be an explicit
enumeration of all the inputs and
their respective outputs, which is
both incomputable and entirely
defeats the purpose of logical
specification in the first place!

Intra-Module Inference

Consequently, we advocate moderation
in the use of inference. In particular,
because preconditions of a function are inferred
based on the function’s clients,
it is impossible to deduce
preconditions

describing the all inputs
of library functions,
e.g.
the public or exported
functions of a module.
Instead, inference is best used
judiciously, in an
intra-modular

fashion
[
Lahiri09
]
.
Here, the
programmer
specifies
the types for all exported functions,
and the
verifier
uses those
specifications, and the code of the
module to infer all contracts for
internal (private) functions.

This recipe offers several benefits.
First, specifications on public functions
provide useful documentation.
Second, the method allows modular analysis in
that the verifier can analyze each module in
complete isolation from the others.
Consequently, we have found that intra-modular
inference drastically reduces the overhead of
using “interesting” types
[
LiquidPLDI08
,
Seidel14
]
,
by eliminating the need to provide explicit
annotations during polymorphic instantiation,
as we shall see next.

Chapter 6
Type Polymorphism

Next, lets look at
\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
which
adds support for
type
polymorphism.

6.1
Examples

The main challenge with polymorphic
signatures is to
instantiate

them appropriately at usage.
Consider the following specification
and implementation of the
max
function:

⬇

val

max

:

forall

’
a
:
Base
.

’
a

=>

’
a

=>

’
a

let

=

(
x
,

y
)

=>

{

if

(
x

<

y
)

{

y

}

else

{

x

};

Comparison is permitted for any base type as illustrated below:

⬇

:

forall

’
a
:
Base
.

’

=>

’
a

=>

Bool

How can we verify the following
client
of

?

⬇

client
:

()

=>

int
[

|0

<

]

let

=

{

(0,

r

+

Problem: Instantiation

Intuitively, at its usage in
client

the
max
function behaves
as if it takes and returns
non-negative numbers.
Thus, to verify
client
we must

instantiate
, the type variable

’a
with the equivalent of the
refinement:

{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0\leq\nu}}\}}}}}
.
But how shall we determine appropriate
instance refinements?

1
1

1

Of course, we
could
specify
that
max
returns
one of

its two inputs, via the type:

{{x}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{{y}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\nu=x\vee\nu=y}}\}}}}}}

after which we would be able to verify
the clients as in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\beta}}
.
However, for the sake of exposition,
lets assume this option is unavailable.

Solution: Decouple Type and Refinement Inference

The key idea in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
is to
decouple

the inference of
types
from those of

refinements
.
The former,
i.e.
type instances, can
be determined by classical methods

e.g.
Hindley-Milner style unification

[
Milner82
]
, or its modern variants
as seen in Haskell
[
JonesVWW06
,
SchrijversJSV09
]
,
or local inference methods with support for
subtyping
[
pierce-turner
,
sulzmann97type
]

or higher-rank polymorphism
[
DunfieldK13
]
.
The latter,
i.e.
refinement instances,
can be obtained via Horn constraint
solving as shown in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
.

Phase 1: Type Elaboration

In the first phase,
\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
uses
classical unification
[
piercebook
]

to
elaborate
the source program to

⬇

val

max

:

forall

’
a
:
Base
.

’
a

=>

’
a

=>

’
a

let

max

=

\Lambda

’
a
:
Base
.

(
x
,

y
)

=>

{

if

(
x

<

y
)

{

y

}

else

{

x

}

};

val

client
:

()

=>

int
[
v
|0

<

v
]

let

client

=

()

=>

{

let

=

(

[

[
\star
]])(0,

r

+

1

};

Each instantiation site is elaborated to an
explicit
type application
that annotates
the polymorphic function (
max
) with
the instance (
int[
\star
]
) for each
type variable (
’a
).
Crucially, the instances are just the (unrefined)
types with
holes
for the as yet unknown
refinements.

Phase 2: Refinement Inference

In the second phase, we will generate and solve
Horn constraints to infer suitable refinement
instances. As in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
, we will create
a new Horn variable
\kappa
for each hole, so
the above type application becomes

{\texttt{max}[{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}]}

which has type

{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}}

Next, the VC from
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
yields the Horn constraint

\begin{array}[]{rll}&\forall\nu.\ \nu=0&\Rightarrow\ \kappa({\nu})\\
\wedge&\forall\nu.\ \nu=5&\Rightarrow\ \kappa({\nu})\\
\wedge&\forall{r}.\ \kappa({{r}})&\Rightarrow\ \forall\nu.\ \nu={r}+1\ \Rightarrow\ 0<\nu\end{array}

(6.1)

Which has the satisfying assignment
\sigma
such that

\sigma(\kappa)({z})\ \doteq\ 0\leq z

which verifies that the code is well-typed.

\mathrm{\Gamma}

\ldots

Figure 6.1:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
: Syntax of Types and Terms

6.2
Types and Terms

Lets formalize the above intuition in

\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
, which extends
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}

with polymorphic types, extends the terms
to include type abstraction and application
as summarized in
fig.

6.1
.

Types

We extend the language of types to include
type
variables

\alpha
of a kind
k
which can be
quantified to get
polymorphic
types

\forall{\alpha}\!:\!{k}.{t}
.
The set of
bare
types
\tau
are those
where
all
refinements are holes
{\{{\star}\}}
.

Terms

As the procedure for determining type instances
is classical
[
piercebook
,
bidir-survey
]
, we assume
that the language of terms is already elaborated with
an explicit

type abstraction
form
\char 12\relax{\alpha}{e}

and a

type application
form
e[\tau]
.
Crucially, neither form involves refinements:
the former just has type variables, and the
latter uses bare types where all the refinements
are holes.

6.3
Declarative Typing

To account for type polymorphism, we must
extend well-formedness and subtyping to
quantified types, and then add rules for
checking type abstraction terms and
synthesizing types for the type
application terms.

\textrm{{Well-formedness}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t:k$}

\frac{{\alpha}\!:\!{B}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}\and\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{\alpha}}\vdash p}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash{\alpha}{{{\{{{x}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}:B}{{{\textsc{Wf-Var-Base}}}}\par\par\frac{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}\in\mathrm{\Gamma}}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash{\alpha}{{{\{{{x}\!:\!{\textit{true}}}\}}}}:k}{{{\textsc{Wf-Var}}}}\par\par\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma};{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}\vdash t:k_{t}}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\Lambda{\alpha}\!:\!{k}.{t}:\star}{{{\textsc{Wf-All}}}}

Figure 6.2:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
: Rules for Well-formedness

Well-formedness

We associate each type variable with a
kind
,
and use the well-formedness rules in
fig.

6.2

to ensure that only base-kinded type variables are refined.
The rule
Wf-Var-Base
checks well-formedness of
refined type variables of base kind by checking
that the type variable is bound in the typing environment
and that the refinement predicate is well formed.
The rule
Wf-Var
ensures that type variables
of non-base types are trivially refined with true to
avoid unsoundness.
The rule
Wf-All
ensures that polymorphic types
are well-formed with a star kind, if their body is well-formed
in an environment extended with the bound variable.

Example: Refining Non-base Variables is Unsound

Consider the following polymorphic function
that returns a
\mathit{false}
refined
int
.

⬇

val

dead

:

forall

a
:
Base
.

a
[
v
|
false
]

=>

int
[
v
|
false
]

let

dead

=

(
x
)

=>

{

0

};

For the type of
dead
to be well-formed,
the kind of
a
has to be base as
a
is
refined with a non-trivial predicate.
The precondition ensures that the function

dead
type checks.
However, the precondition also effectively
prohibits the function from being called at
run-time.
Suppose that we allowed a call to
dead

with a non-base argument,
e.g.

id

⬇

val

unsound

:

int
[
v
|
false
]

let

unsound

=

{

let

id

=

(
x
)

=>

{
x
};

deadcode

id

};

If the above call type checked,
our system would
unsoundly

prove that
0
has the type
int
[
v
|
false
]
.
Fortunately, we can ensure that
the above definition is rejected
by restricting how refined type
variables (like
a
) are instantiated.
(We could simply prohibit refinements
on type variables, but this would
preclude many useful specifications §
7
.)

\square

\begin{array}[]{rcll}{(\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{\tau})}[{\alpha}\mapsto{t_{\alpha}}]&\doteq&\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{\tau}&\\
{(\forall{{\alpha^{\prime}}\!:\!{k}}.{\tau})}[{\alpha}\mapsto{t_{\alpha}}]&\doteq&\forall{{\alpha^{\prime}}\!:\!{k}}.{({\tau}[{\alpha}\mapsto{t_{\alpha}}])},&\alpha^{\prime}\not=\alpha\\
{({{x}\!:\!{t_{x}}}\rightarrow{t})}[{\alpha}\mapsto{t_{\alpha}}]&\doteq&{{x}\!:\!{({t_{x}}[{\alpha}\mapsto{t_{\alpha}}])}}\rightarrow{({t}[{\alpha}\mapsto{t_{\alpha}}])}&\\
{({\alpha}{{\{{r}\}}})}[{\alpha}\mapsto{{b}{{\{{r_{\alpha}}\}}}}]&\doteq&{b}{{\{{r\wedge r_{\alpha}}\}}}&\\
{({b}{{\{{r}\}}})}[{\alpha}\mapsto{t_{\alpha}}]&\doteq&{b}{{\{{r}\}}},&b\not=\alpha\\
\end{array}

Figure 6.3:
Type Variable Instantiation

Type Variable Instantiation

We use two functions to instantiate
(substitute) variables in types.
The function
t
[\alpha:=b]

substitutes
the type variable
\alpha
with
the basic type
b
in a standard way.
The function
{t}[{\alpha}\mapsto{t_{\alpha}}]
,
on the other hand,
instantiates
the type variable

\alpha
with the type
t_{\alpha}
by strengthening
refinement predicates, as defined in
fig.

6.3
.
Note that definition of
{s}[{\alpha}\mapsto{t}]
is partial:
it is not defined when
s\equiv{\alpha}{{\{{r}\}}}

for
r
different than
\mathit{true}
and
t
is not a base type,
to prevent unsoundness as described above.

\textrm{{Subtyping}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t_{1}\prec:t_{2}$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma};{{\alpha_{1}}\!:\!{k}}\vdash t_{1}\prec:{t_{2}}[\alpha_{2}:=\alpha_{1}]}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\forall{{\alpha_{1}}\!:\!{k}}.{t_{1}}\prec:\forall{{\alpha_{2}}\!:\!{k}}.{t_{2}}}{{{\textsc{Sub-All}}}}

Figure 6.4:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
: Rules for Subtyping

Subtyping

The rule
Sub-All
shown in
fig.

6.4

formalizes subtyping for quantified types by renaming the
type variables and checking that the types being quantified
over belong to the subtyping relation, in an environment
extended with the type variable.
For example, the rule derives

\emptyset\vdash\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{{{x}\!:\!{\alpha}}\rightarrow{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\nu=x}}\}}}}}}\prec:\forall{{\beta}\!:\!{k}}.{{{x}\!:\!{\beta}}\rightarrow{\beta}}

because, after substituting
\beta
with
\alpha
the above reduces to

\alpha\vdash{{x}\!:\!{\alpha}}\rightarrow{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\nu=x}}\}}}}}\prec:{{x}\!:\!{\alpha}}\rightarrow{\alpha}

which follows from the rules
Sub-Base
and
Sub-Fun
.

Checking

The rule
Chk-TLam
in
fig.

6.5
checks type-abstraction terms

\char 12\relax{\alpha}{e}
against quantified types
\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{t}

by checking the inner expression
e
against the
t

in an environment containing
\alpha
, and checking the
well-formedness of the polymorphic type.

Example: Implementation of
max

The specification and implementation of
the
max
function from
section

6.1

are elaborated to:

\displaystyle t_{\texttt{max}}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \forall{\alpha}\!:\!{B}.{{\alpha}\rightarrow{{\alpha}\rightarrow{\alpha}}}

\displaystyle e_{\texttt{max}}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \Lambda{\alpha}\!:\!{B}.{\lambda{x,y}.\ {\texttt{{if}}\;x<y\;\texttt{{then}}\;{y}\;\texttt{{else}}\;x}}

The checking rules for
\lambda
- and branching terms establish that

\displaystyle{\alpha}\!:\!{B}

\displaystyle\vdash\ \lambda{x,y}.\ {\texttt{{if}}\;x<y\;\texttt{{then}}\;{y}\;\texttt{{else}}\;x}\Leftarrow{\alpha}\rightarrow{{\alpha}\rightarrow{\alpha}}

after which the rule
Chk-TLam
lets us conclude

\emptyset\vdash e_{\texttt{max}}\Leftarrow t_{\texttt{max}}
.

\square

Synthesis

The rule
Syn-TApp
in
fig.

6.5

synthesizes the type for a type-application term

e[\tau]
.
Recall that the elaboration process inserts the
application annotations using one of many standard
approaches, but the applied type is
bare

in that every refinement is a
hole

{\{{\star}\}}

as a standard elaborator is unaware of refinements.
Instead, similar to the
Chk-Rec
and

Syn-Ann
from
fig.

5.3

which handle type annotations with holes,
the rule
Syn-TApp
first guesses
a suitable instantiation
\tau\triangleright t

such that
t
is well-formed in the given
context.
The rule then
substitutes
the
concrete
t
for the type variable

\alpha
quantified over in the signature
for
e
.
To prevent unsoundness, this substitution
is partial: type synthesis fails in cases
that it is not defined.

Example: Uses of
max

The function application term

(\texttt{max}\ 0\ 5)
inside
client
from
section

6.1

is elaborated to
(\texttt{max}[{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\star}\}}}]\ 0\ 5)
.
In the context
\mathrm{\Gamma}
with the signature for
max
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}\doteq\

\displaystyle{\texttt{max}}\!:\!{t_{\texttt{max}}}

we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\

\displaystyle\texttt{max}\Rightarrow\forall{\alpha}\!:\!{B}.{{\alpha}\rightarrow{{\alpha}\rightarrow{\alpha}}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\star}\}}}\triangleright\texttt{{nat}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{{nat}}:B

and so, using
Syn-TApp
we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\

\displaystyle\texttt{max}[{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\star}\}}}]\Rightarrow{\texttt{{nat}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{nat}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{{nat}}}}

after which the application rule
Syn-App

fig.

3.6
yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\

\displaystyle{{\texttt{max}[{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\star}\}}}]}\;{0}}\;{5}\Rightarrow\texttt{{nat}}

\square

\textrm{{Type Checking}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma};{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}\vdash e\Leftarrow t\and\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{t}:\star}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\char 12\relax{\alpha}{e}\Leftarrow\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{t}}{{{\textsc{Chk-TLam}}}}

\textrm{{Type Synthesis}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Rightarrow t$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Rightarrow\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{s}\and\tau\triangleright t\and\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t:k}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e[\tau]\Rightarrow{s}[{\alpha}\mapsto{t}]}{{{\textsc{Syn-TApp}}}}

Figure 6.5:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
: Rules for Checking and Synthesis

6.4
Verification Conditions

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{sub}&:&(T\times T)\rightarrow C\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{sub}(\forall{{\alpha_{1}}\!:\!{k}}.{t_{1}},\ \forall{{\alpha_{2}}\!:\!{k}}.{t_{2}})&\doteq&\mathsf{sub}(t_{1},\ {t_{2}}[\alpha_{2}:=\alpha_{1}])\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{check}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E\times T)\rightarrow C\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ \char 12\relax{\alpha}{e},\ \forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{t})&\doteq&\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma};{\alpha},\ e,\ t)\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{synth}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E)\rightarrow(C\times T)\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e[\tau])&\doteq&(c,{{s}[\alpha:=t]})\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad(c,\ \forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{s})&=&\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e)\\
\quad\quad t&=&\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ \ \tau)\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 6.6:
Verification Conditions for
\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
, extends cases of
fig.

5.5

Figure
6.6
summarizes how we extend the Horn Verification Condition
generation algorithm to account for type polymorphism. In essence, we add new cases
to the procedures
\mathsf{sub}
,
\mathsf{check}
and
\mathsf{synth}
that respectively generate
Horn Constraint for the subtyping, checking and synthesis modes to account for the
new derivation rules shown in
fig.

6.5
.

Subtyping

The subtyping constraint for two polymorphic types is generated
by recursing on the underlying types, after unifying the type
variables.

Checking

Similarly, to check a type abstraction, we recursively
invoke
\mathsf{check}
on the inner expression using a suitably extended
context. We also check well-formedness of the provided type
to ensure the kind given to the abstracted type variable is
correct.

Synthesis

The heavy lifting is done by
\mathsf{synth}
, which
synthesizes a type and a constraint for a type
application term
e[\tau]
.
However, we treat this analogous to synthesizing
the type of a type-annotation.
Instead of “guessing” a type as in the declarative

Syn-TApp
(
fig.

6.5
), we use

\tau
to generate a fresh
template
for the
instantiated
t
and then substitute the template

t
for the type variable
\alpha
to get the
template for the
e[\tau]
.

Example:
client

Let us see how the above works on
client

from
section

6.1
. Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\doteq\ {\texttt{max}}\!:\!{\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{{\alpha}\rightarrow{{\alpha}\rightarrow{\alpha}}}}

\displaystyle e_{0}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \texttt{max}[{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\star}\}}}]

\displaystyle e_{1}

\displaystyle\doteq\ {{e_{0}}\;{0}}\;{5}

\displaystyle e_{2}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \texttt{{let}}\;\texttt{r}=e_{1}\;\texttt{{in}}\;\texttt{r}+1

Now, it it easy to check that as it simply returns the type of
max
in
\mathrm{\Gamma}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ \texttt{max})

\displaystyle\doteq\ (\mathit{true},\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{{\alpha}\rightarrow{{\alpha}\rightarrow{\alpha}}})

Thus, the instance of
max
synthesizes the type

\displaystyle\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e_{0})

\displaystyle\doteq\ (\mathit{true},{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}})

by substituting all occurrences of
\alpha

with the
fresh
template
{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}

generated from
{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\star}\}}}
. Consequently, the subsequent
applications in
e_{1}
synthesize the constraint and type

\displaystyle\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e_{1})

\displaystyle\doteq\ (c_{1}\wedge c_{2},\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}})

where the synthesized type is the (instance) function’s
output

and
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are constrain
0
and
5
to be subtypes of
the function’s
input

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \forall\nu.\ \nu=0\Rightarrow\ \kappa({\nu})

\displaystyle c_{2}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \forall\nu.\ \nu=5\Rightarrow\ \kappa({\nu})

The result, and hence, output type is bound to
r
and so writing

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \mathrm{\Gamma};{{\texttt{r}}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}}

then we get
\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime},\ \texttt{r}+1,\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0<\nu}}\}}}})\doteq\ c_{3}
where

\displaystyle c_{3}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \forall{r}.\ \kappa({{r}})\ \Rightarrow\ \forall\nu.\ \nu={r}+1\ \Rightarrow\ 0<\nu

Hence, checking the body
e_{2}
of
client
against its output type yields

\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e_{2},\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0<\nu}}\}}}})\ \doteq\ c_{1}\wedge c_{2}\wedge c_{3}

which is exactly the constraint (
6.1
).

\square

6.5
Discussion

Thus, the mechanism for refinement inference
introduced for
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}

chapter

5
makes
using polymorphic functions very pleasant.
The main problem here is to figure out how to
instantiate

a polymorphic signature at a particular instantiation site.
The key idea is to
first
use classical methods to instantiate
the
unrefined
(“bare”) part of the type, leaving
holes

for the unknown refinements, after which the Horn constraint based
method from
\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
can be applied to infer suitable refinements.

Other approaches to Polymorphic Instantation

There are several other possible ways to account for type polymorphism.

•

Annotations

One approach is to have the programmer
explicitly
specify
the instance refinements. However, this is
most unpalatable as polymorphism is ubiquitous in
modern code
[
pierce-turner
]
and placing explicit
annotations would get tedious quickly.

•

Defaults

Another approach would be to
default
to some refinement
such as
\mathit{true}
as done in Refined Racket
[
RefinedRacket
]

or the Stainless verifier
[
kuncak-stainless
]
.
Sadly, this method is rather conservative, as it
precludes the verification of
client
as the
type checker has no information about the value
returned by
max
other than it is
some

int
.

•

Unification

A third approach, deployed by the
F*
system
[
fstar
]
,
is to try to
unify
the types of the inputs
or outputs to determine suitable instance refinements.
Unfortunately, the interaction with the refinement
logic makes unification brittle: for example,
in
client
it is unclear how to unify the types
of the two inputs
0
and
5
to obtain the
instance type
{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0\leq\nu}}\}}}}}
.

Polymorphism and HOFs

Finally, support for type polymorphism is essential for
being able to easily use Higher-Order functions. For example,
consider the
fold
function below that accumulates some value
over the integers between
0
and
n
:

⬇

val

fold

:

(’
a

=>

int

=>

’
a
)

=>

’
a

=>

int

=>

’
a

let

fold

=

(
f
,

acc
,

n
)

=>

{

let

rec

loop

=

(
i
,

acc
)

=>

{

if

(
i

<

n
)

{

loop
(
i
+1,

f
(
acc
,

i
))

}

else

{

acc

}

};

loop
(0,

acc
)

}

We can use
fold
to sum the
int
egers from
0
to
m
as:

⬇

val

sumTo
:

m
:
nat

=>

nat

let

sumTo

=

(
m
)

=>

{

let

add

=

(
x
,

y
)

=>

{
x

+

y
};

fold
(
add
,

0,

0,

m
)

}

Readers familiar with the classical Floyd-Hoare proof rule for loops
might notice its similarity to the type signature of
fold
:

\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{{({\alpha}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}\rightarrow{\alpha}})}\rightarrow{{\alpha}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}\rightarrow{\alpha}}}}

The type variable
\alpha
is analagous to the loop
invariant
;
the accumulation function’s type
{\alpha}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}\rightarrow{\alpha}}

says that it
preserves
the invariant,
i.e.
if the input
accumulated value satisfies the invariant then so does the output;
the initial value of the accumulator must satisfy the invariant
\alpha
;
and hence, “by induction”, the final value, regardless of how many
accumulation steps is guaranteed to satisfy the invariant
\alpha
.
Hence, the VC generation mechanism of
\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}

let the checker infer that within
sumTo
,
the type parameter
\alpha
is instantiated to
nat
,

i.e.
that
nat
is an invariant of the accumulator.
Consequently, the value returned by
fold
,
and hence,
sumTo
must also be a
nat
.

This ability to automatically infer refinements
in the presence of polymorphism will prove especially
useful with user-defined
data types
,
as we shall see next.

Chapter 7
Data Types

There is only so much one can do with
int

and
bool
values: programs get much more
interesting once we start adding

data types
.
Next, lets look at
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
which extends

\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
with support for precisely specifying
and verifying properties of (algebraic)

user-defined data types
.

7.1
Examples

As usual, lets begin with a bird’s eye view
of the different kinds of specifications we
might write for data types.

7.1.1
Properties of Data

The simplest, but perhaps most ubiquitous and useful
examples, pertain to properties of the data
stored within polymorphic
containers
like
the
list
type defined as:

⬇

type

list
(’
a
)

=

|

Nil

|

Cons
(’
a
,

list
(’
a
))

The type declaration introduces the type
list
(’
a
)

which has two constructors:

⬇

val

Nil

:

list
(’
a
)

val

Cons

:

’
a

=>

list
(’
a
)

=>

list
(’
a
)

Lets use
Nil
and
Cons
to write a function
range
that
returns the sequence of
int
values between
a lower bound
lo
and upper bound
hi
.

⬇

val

range
:

lo
:
int

=>

:
int

=>

(

[
v
|
lo

<=

&&

<

=

(
lo
,

)

{

(

<

)

{

rest

=

(

+

hi
);

Cons
(
lo
,

rest
)

}

else

{

Nil

}

}

The signature for

specifies that
every

element in the output
list
is in the interval between

lo
and

.

7.1.2
Relationships between Data

The previous example showed how we can
capture properties of
individual

datum by refining the type parameter of

list
.
What if we want to relate the values of

multiple
data across a structure?
For example, here’s a data type definition
that specifies an
ordered list
of
non-decreasing values:

⬇

type

olist
(’
a
)

=

|

ONil

|

OCons

(
x
:’
a
,

xs
:
olist
(’
a
[
v
|
x
<=
v
]))

The type definition endows the constructors with refined signatures

⬇

val

ONil

:

olist
(’
a
)

val

OCons
:

x
:’
a

=>

olist
(’
a
[
v
|
x
<=
v
])

=>

olist
(’
a
)

The signature for

says that
the head
x
must be smaller than
each

element of the tail. Thus, the type checker will

accept
the term

⬇

=

(0,

OCons
(1,

OCons
(2,

ONil
)));

but will

the term

⬇

=

OCons
(0,

OCons
(2,

OCons
(1,

ONil
)));

That is, the constructor’s signature ensures
that
illegal
values do not
inhabit
the type.
More interestingly, we can specify and verify
the function in
fig.

7.1
that
insert
s
a value
x
into an ordered list
ys
.
We can use

to implement an

that it always returns an ordered list:

⬇

val

:

(’

)

=>

(’

)

(

)

{

(

){

=>

|

Cons

(

,

)

=>

insert
(

,

(
t
))

}

};

⬇

val

:

’

=>

olist
(’

)

=>

(’

)

(

{

(

=

(

,
