.

7.1.3
Properties of Structure

A third class of useful specifications
are
aggregate
properties of the
entire structure, for example,
the
height
of a tree, or
the
multi-set
of elements
of a list.
Next, lets see how these can be specified
by refining the
output
types of the
constructors with
ghost
functions
that specify the aggregate properties via
two steps.

1. Defining Measures

To specify the
length
of a list,
we introduce a function such that
\texttt{len}({xs})

represents the length of the list
{xs}
.

⬇

measure

len
:

list
(’
a
)

=>

nat

To ensure decidable VC validity checking
we ensure that
len
is
uninterpreted

in the refinement logic,
i.e.
the SMT solver only
knows that
len
satisfies the
axiom of congruence

\forall{xs},{ys}.\ {xs}={ys}\ \Rightarrow\ \texttt{len}({xs})=\texttt{len}({ys})

2. Refining Constructors

We use measures to specify the structure’s
properties, by appropriately refining the type
of the constructors’ output.

⬇

type

list
(’
a
)

=

|

Nil

=>

[
v
|
len
(
v
)

=

0]

|

Cons
(
x
:’
a
,
xs
:
list
(’
a
))

=>

[
v
|
len
(
v
)

=

1+
len
(
xs
)]

In the definition above, the output for
Nil
says
that it constructs a list of length
0
; the output
for
Cons
says that it constructs a list whose
length is one greater than the tail.

3. Using Measures

We can now use
len
in refinements in various ways.
First, to specify pre-conditions on
partial

functions. For example, refinement checking ensures
that due to the precondition — which will be checked
at uses of
head
— the
assert
(
false
)
never fails
at run-time:

⬇

val

head

:

list
(’
a
)[
v
|0

<

len
(
v
)]

=>

’
a

let

head

=

(
xs
)

=>

{

switch
(
xs
){

|

Cons
(
h
,

t
)

=>

h

|

Nil

=>

assert
(
false
)

}

};

Second, to specify post-conditions
e.g.
on the result
of list concatenation

⬇

val

append
:

xs
:
list
(’
a
)

=>

ys
:
list
(’
a
)

=>

list
(’
a
)[
v
|
len
(
v
)

=

len
(
xs
)+
len
(
ys
)]

let

rec

append

=

(
xs
,

ys
)

=>

{

switch

(
xs
)

{

|

Nil

=>

ys

|

Cons
(
h
,

t
)

=>

let

rest

=

append
(
t
,

ys
);

Cons
(
h
,

rest
)

}

};

Measures are Ghost Code

Measures only exist at the level of the
specification
:
they cannot be used in the implementation
1
1

1

Systems like

LiquidHaskell
allow the programmer to specify the measure as
a function that satisfies certain syntactic constraints,
and then automatically
generate
the constructor’s
refined types. However, that is merely a convenience:
conceptually, a measure exists only for specification.
.
However, it is easy to connect measures to run-time
values, via functions like

⬇

val

length
:

xs
:
list
(’
a
)

=>

int
[
v
|
v

=

len
(
xs
)]

let

rec

length

=

(
xs
)

=>

{

switch
(
xs
){

|

Nil

=>

0

|

Cons
(
h
,

t
)

=>

1

+

length
(
t
)

}

}

We can now use the result of
length
to determine
whether it is safe to compute the
head
of a
list

⬇

val

safeHead

:

’
a

=>

list
(’
a
)

=>

’
a

let

safeHead

=

(
default
,

xs
)

=>

{

let

nonEmpty

=

0

<

length
(
xs
);

if

(
nonEmpty
)

{

head
(
xs
)

}

else

{

default

}

};

Refinement typing establishes that when
nonEmpty
is
true
,
we indeed have
0

<

len
(
xs
)
thereby verifying the call
head
(
xs
)
.

7.2
Types and Terms

From the examples in
section

7.1

one might get the impression that
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}

must have multiple extensions over
\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
.
In fact, all three flavors of specifications —
reasoning about individual data, about
relationships between data, and reasoning
about structure — are supported by a
single pillar:
refined data constructors
.
Thus, the only mechanisms we need are a way to
“apply” the refined type when
constructing

new data and to “unapply” the type when

destructing
the data by pattern matching.
Next, lets see how these two ideas are formalized
in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
, whose syntactic additions are summarized
in Figure
7.2
.

Datatypes

First, we assume there is a set of

type constructors

C
(
e.g.

list
)
and
data constructors

D
(
e.g.

Nil
,
Cons
).
The
polarity

p
captures the position that
type variables appear in definitions of data types
and can be positive (
+
), negative (
-
),
both (
\pm
), or neither (
\epsilon
).
A
datatype

\delta
is a triple

\langle{C},\;{\overline{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}/{p}}},\;{\overline{{D}\!:\!{t}}}\rangle

comprising a type constructor
C
,
the list of type variables
over which the datatype is parameterized
together with their kind
and polarity
\overline{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}/{p}}
,
and a set of data constructors and their
refinement types
\overline{{D}\!:\!{t}}
.

Example: Ordered Lists

Suppose we wrote the following
ordered list type, refined with a
len

measure tracking the list’s size

⬇

type

ol
(’
a
)

=

|

ON

=>

[
v
|
len
(
v
)=0]

|

OC
(’
a
,
xs
:
ol
(’
a
[
v
|
x
<=
v
]))

=>

[
v
|
len
(
v
)=1+
len
(
xs
)]

We would represent the above as

\delta_{\texttt{OL}}\doteq\langle\texttt{OL},\ \{{\alpha}\!:\!{B}/{+}\},\ \{{\texttt{ON}}\!:\!{t_{N}};{\texttt{OC}}\!:\!{t_{C}}\}\rangle

The type variable
\alpha
appears in one positive position
and since
\alpha
elements are compared it is of base kind.
The types of the “nil” and “cons” constructors are respectively:

\displaystyle t_{N}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \forall{\alpha}\!:\!{B}.{{{\texttt{OL}}[{\alpha}]}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\texttt{len}({\nu})=0}}\}}}}}

\displaystyle t_{C}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \forall{\alpha}\!:\!{B}.{{{x}\!:\!{\alpha}}\rightarrow{{{{xs}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{x\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]}}\rightarrow{{{\texttt{OL}}[{\alpha}]}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\texttt{len}({\nu})=1+\texttt{len}({{xs}})}}\}}}}}}}

(7.1)

That is, the type
t_{N}
says that “nil”
returns an ordered list of length
0
;
and the type
t_{C}
says that “cons” takes
as input a head
{x}
of type

\alpha
and a tail
{xs}

each

of whose elements is an
\alpha
larger
than
{x}
, and returns an ordered
list whose size is one more than that
of the tail
{xs}
.

\square

Environments

We extend the environments to include all the
data type definitions
\delta
. For simplicity,
we will assume that all the data type definitions
and measure names are
global
, that is, they
belong in the top-level environment used for type
checking and synthesis.

Types

As hinted in the discussion for constructors above,
the language of
basic
types is extended to
include a
type constructor application
form

{C}[{\overline{t}}]
, where the type
constructor
C
is applied to the type arguments

\overline{t}
.
Intuitively one can think of the above as the
type obtained by instantiating the type
parameters
\overline{\alpha}
of
C

with the actual type arguments
\overline{t}
.
As before, these basic types can be refined, so

{{\texttt{OL}}[{\texttt{{nat}}}]}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{3\leq\texttt{len}({\nu})}}\}}}}

would correspond to the type of ordered lists
of non-negative
int
values comprising three
or more elements.

Alternatives

Each
alternative

{D}({\overline{x}})\rightarrow{e}

comprises a
pattern

D(\overline{x})

and the term
e
to be evaluated if the
scrutinee matches the pattern.

Terms

We add the data constructors
D
to the language of terms so that
polymorphic instantiation
e[t]
(from
\mathsf{\lambda_{\alpha}}
)
and function application
{e}\;{x}
(from
\mathsf{\lambda}
)
can be combined to
construct
values of user-defined types.
To
destruct
values of user-defined types, we introduce
a pattern-match form
\texttt{{switch}}\;y\;\{\overline{a}\}
where
the value bound to
y
is
scrutinized
by each
of the alternatives in
\overline{a}
.

Data Constructors

D

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

D_{1},D_{2},D_{3},\ldots

Type Constructors

C

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},\ldots

Polarity

p

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

+

\ \mid\

-

\ \mid\

\pm

\ \mid\

\epsilon

Datatypes

\delta

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

\langle{C},\;{\overline{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}/{p}}},\;{\overline{{D}\!:\!{t}}}\rangle

Environments

\mathrm{\Gamma}

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

\ldots

from Fig.
6.1

\ \mid\

\mathrm{\Gamma};{\delta}

type definitions

Basic Types

b

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

\ldots

from Fig.
6.1

\ \mid\

{C}[{\overline{t}}]

datatypes

Alternatives

a

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

{D}({\overline{x}})\rightarrow{e}

switch alternative

Terms

e

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

\ldots

from Fig.
6.1

\ \mid\

D

data constructor

\ \mid\

\texttt{{switch}}\;x\;\{\overline{a}\}

data destructor

Figure 7.2:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
: Syntax of Types and Terms

7.3
Declarative Typing

Next, lets see how the rules for well-formedness, subtyping, checking and synthesis
are extended to account for constructors and destructors.

\textrm{{Well-formedness}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t:k$}

\frac{\overline{k}=\mathsf{kinds}(\delta,\ C)\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t_{i}:k_{i}\ \mbox{for each}\ 1\leq i\leq\mid\!\overline{k}\!\mid\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{{C}[{\overline{t}}]}}\vdash p}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash{{C}[{\overline{t}}]}{{{\{{{x}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}:B}{{{\textsc{Wf-Data}}}}

Figure 7.3:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
: Rule for Well-formedness

\textrm{{Subtyping}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t_{1}\prec:t_{2}$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash s_{i}\prec:t_{i}\ \mbox{for each}\ i.p_{i}\in\{+,\pm\}\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t_{i}\prec:s_{i}\ \mbox{for each}\ i.p_{i}\in\{-,\pm\}\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};{{\nu_{1}}\!:\!{{{\{{{{C}[{\overline{s}}]}\!:\!{p_{1}}}\}}}}}\vdash{p_{2}}[\nu_{2}:=\nu_{1}]\and\overline{p}=\mathsf{polarities}(\delta,\ C)}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash{{C}[{\overline{s}}]}{{{\{{{\nu_{1}}\!:\!{p_{1}}}\}}}}\prec:{{C}[{\overline{t}}]}{{{\{{{\nu_{2}}\!:\!{p_{2}}}\}}}}}{{{\textsc{Sub-Data}}}}

Figure 7.4:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
: Rules for Subtyping

7.3.1
Well-formedness

The rule
Wf-Data
shown in
fig.

7.3
formalizes well-formedness of
datatypes. The rule checkes that the refinement of the type is well-formed,
that each of the type arguments has the proper kind, and that
the type constructor is fully applied.
The premises use the function
\mathsf{kinds}(\delta,\ C)

that retrieves the kinds of
C
from the data environment
\delta
:

\mathsf{kinds}(\delta,\ C)\doteq\overline{k}\quad\text{if}\ \langle{C},\;{\overline{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}/{p}}},\;{\overline{{D}\!:\!{t}}}\rangle\in\delta

7.3.2
Subtyping

The rule
Sub-Data
shown in
fig.

7.4

formalizes subtyping between datatypes.
In an environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}
, the type
{{C}[{\overline{s}}]}{{{\{{{\nu_{1}}\!:\!{p_{1}}}\}}}}

is a subtype of
{{C}[{\overline{t}}]}{{{\{{{\nu_{2}}\!:\!{p_{2}}}\}}}}
,
if the base refinement
p_{1}
entails
p_{2}
, and
each
of the
component types
s_{i}
is a subtype of the corresponding component

t_{i}
.
Subtyping of the components is checked using

\mathsf{polarities}(\delta,\ C)
which retrieves the
polarity information from the data environment
\delta

\mathsf{polarities}(\delta,\ C)=\overline{p}\ \text{if}\ \langle{C},\;{\overline{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}/{p}}},\;{\overline{{D}\!:\!{t}}}\rangle\in\delta

For each component with positive polarity (resp. negative) polarity
the rule uses covariant (resp. contravariant) subtyping.

Example: Subtyping in
insert

Consider the environment

\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq}\ \doteq\ {\alpha}\!:\!{B};\ {{x}}\!:\!{\alpha};\ {{y}}\!:\!{\alpha};\ {{ys^{\prime}}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{y\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]};\ {{ys}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{ys^{\prime}}}]};\ {x}\leq{y}

and the alias
{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{z}}]
that denotes
ordered lists of type
\alpha
whose size is one
more than that of
{z}
.
As the following entailment is valid

\displaystyle\forall{x},{y},\nu.\

\displaystyle{x}\leq{y}\ \Rightarrow\ {y}\leq\nu\ \Rightarrow\ {x}\leq\nu

the rule
Sub-Data
lets us conclude

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ {\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{{y}\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]\prec:\ {\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{{x}\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]

(7.2)

As the following entailment is valid

\displaystyle\forall{ys},\ {ys^{\prime}},\ {tl}.\

\displaystyle\texttt{len}({{ys}})=1+\texttt{len}({{ys^{\prime}}})\ \Rightarrow\ \texttt{len}({{tl}})=1+\texttt{len}({{ys^{\prime}}})\ \Rightarrow

\displaystyle\texttt{len}({\nu})=1+\texttt{len}({{tl}})\ \Rightarrow\ \texttt{len}({\nu})=1+\texttt{len}({{ys}})

the rule
Sub-Data
lets us conclude

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq};{{tl}}\!:\!{t_{y}}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ {\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{tl}}]\ \prec:\ {\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{ys}}]

(7.3)

where
t_{y}\doteq{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha_{x},{ys^{\prime}}}]

\square

7.3.3
Checking

\textrm{{Type Checking}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma}\mid{y}\vdash a_{i}\Leftarrow t\ \mbox{for each}\ i}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{{switch}}\;y\;\{\overline{a}\}\Leftarrow t}{{{\textsc{Chk-Swt}}}}

\textrm{{Checking Alternatives}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\mid{y}\vdash a\Leftarrow t$}

\frac{s=\mathsf{ctor}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ D,\ y)\and\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}=\mathsf{unapply}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ \overline{z},\ s)\and\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}\vdash e\Leftarrow t}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\mid{y}\vdash{D}({\overline{z}})\rightarrow{e}\Leftarrow t}{{{\textsc{Chk-Alt}}}}

Figure 7.5:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
: Rules for Type Checking

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{unapply}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times X\times X^{*}\times T)\rightarrow\mathrm{\Gamma}\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{unapply}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ z;\overline{z},\ {{x}\!:\!{s}}\rightarrow{t})&\doteq&\mathsf{unapply}(\mathrm{\Gamma};{{z}\!:\!{s}},\ y,\ \overline{z},\ {t}[x:=z])\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{unapply}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ \emptyset,\ t)&\doteq&\mathrm{\Gamma};{{y}\!:\!{\mathsf{meet}(\mathrm{\Gamma}(y),t)}}\\[7.22743pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{ctor}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times D\times X)\rightarrow T\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{ctor}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ D,\ y)&\doteq&{s}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{t}]\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad{C}[{\overline{t}}]&=&\mathrm{\Gamma}(y)\\
\quad\quad\forall{{\overline{\alpha}}\!:\!{k}}.{s}&=&\mathrm{\Gamma}(D)\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 7.6:
Meta-functions for Type Checking Switch Alternatives

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{meet}&:&(T\times T)\rightarrow T\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{meet}({b}{{{\{{{\nu_{1}}\!:\!{p_{1}}}\}}}},{b}{{{\{{{\nu_{2}}\!:\!{p_{2}}}\}}}})&\doteq&{b}{{{\{{{\nu_{1}}\!:\!{p_{1}\wedge{p_{2}}[\nu_{2}:=\nu_{1}]}}\}}}}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{meet}({{x_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}}}\rightarrow{t_{1}},{{x_{2}}\!:\!{s_{2}}}\rightarrow{t_{2}})&\doteq&{{x_{1}}\!:\!{\mathsf{meet}(s_{1},s_{2})}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{meet}(t_{1},{t_{2}}[x_{2}:=x_{1}])}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{meet}(\forall{{\alpha_{1}}\!:\!{k}}.{t_{1}},\forall{{\alpha_{2}}\!:\!{k}}.{t_{2}})&\doteq&\forall{{\alpha_{1}}\!:\!{k}}.{\mathsf{meet}(t_{1},{t_{2}}[\alpha_{2}:=\alpha_{1}])}\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 7.7:
Conjoining Types

The rule
Chk-Swt
shown in
fig.

7.5

describes how to
check
that a switch expression has
a given type
t
, by checking that each alternative of
the switch produces a value of type
t
.

Checking an Alternative

The judgment

\mathrm{\Gamma}\mid{y}\vdash{D}({\overline{z}})\rightarrow{e}\Leftarrow t

states that in the
environment

\mathrm{\Gamma}

when the
scrutinee

y

matches the
pattern

D(\overline{z})

the evaluated
result

e
has type
t
.
The rule
Chk-Alt
establishes this judgment
in three steps.

1.

We use
\mathsf{ctor}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ D,\ y)

summarized in
fig.

7.6
to get

s
, the monomorphic
instantiation

of the polymorphic type of constructor
D
.
In other words,
s
is the type of
D

at
this
particular match-instance.

2.

We invoke
\mathsf{unapply}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ \overline{z},\ s)

summarized in
fig.

7.6

to obtain the environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}
which
is
\mathrm{\Gamma}
extended with the types for
the pattern match bindings
\overline{z}

and also, with additional refinements for
the scrutinee
y
that are revealed
by matching against this particular pattern.

3.

We check that result
e
has
the type
t
in environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}
.

Unapply

At destruction sites we use

\mathsf{unapply}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ \overline{z},\ s)

summarized in
fig.

7.6
.
The function
\mathsf{unapply}
can be viewed as
the dual of function application
Given the output type of the constructed
value (
s
), we want to
(1) determine the types that the inputs
(
\overline{z}
) must have had,
and to then
(2) add those bindings to get the
environment used to check the
alternative’s body
e
.

\mathsf{unapply}
does so by recursively
“zipping” together the match-binders

\overline{z}
with the input binders
of the constructor’s (function) type
s
.
If the sequence of binders is
non-empty
(
z;\overline{z}
)
and the constructor type is
{{x}\!:\!{s}}\rightarrow{t}
then
we extend
\mathrm{\Gamma}
with the binding
{z}\!:\!{s}
, and
recurse on the extended environment and the remaining binders

\overline{z}
and the “rest” of the constructor type,
i.e.
its output
t
after substituting the formal
x
with
the “actual”
z
.
Once the sequence of binders is
empty
(
\emptyset
)
then constructor type
t
is exactly the result of

D(\overline{z})
. Crucially,
t
can have
extra information about the scrutinee
y
that
holds under this particular pattern match, and so we

strengthen
the type of
y
by using
\mathsf{meet}

to conjoin the old type
\mathrm{\Gamma}(y)
with the
pattern-match result
t
, and return the extended
environment as the final result (that is used to check
the alternative’s body
e
.)

\displaystyle\lambda{x},\ {ys}.\

\displaystyle\texttt{{switch}}\ ({ys})

\displaystyle\quad\mid{\texttt{OC}}({{y},{ys^{\prime}}})\ \rightarrow

\displaystyle\quad\quad\texttt{{if}}\ {x}\leq{y}\ \texttt{{then}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\texttt{{let}}\ {tl}={{\texttt{OC}[\alpha_{{x}}]}\;{{y}}}\;{{ys^{\prime}}}\ \texttt{{in}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad{{\texttt{OC}[\alpha]}\;{{x}}}\;{{tl}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\texttt{{else}}\ \ldots

\displaystyle\quad\mid\texttt{ON}\ \rightarrow\ldots

Figure 7.8:
Definition of
insert
(
fig.

7.1
) with elaborated type applications.

Example: Checking in
insert

Lets see how the declarative rules let
us
check
the implementation of the

insert
function from
fig.

7.1
.
Suppose our goal is to check that

insert
implements the type

{{{{x}}\!:\!{\alpha}}\rightarrow{{{{ys}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}}[{\alpha}]}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{ys}}]}}}

i.e.
that
insert
returns an ordered list with
one more element than the input list
ys
.
The
fig.

7.8
shows a fragment of
the definition of
insert
elaborated with
explicit type applications at constructor application
sites.
Rule
Chk-Lam
reduces type
checking to the following judgment
that checks the body of
insert

against the specified output type in
the context
\mathrm{\Gamma}
comprising the
bindings for the inputs
{x}
and
{ys}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ \texttt{{switch}}\;{ys}\;\{\ {\texttt{OC}}({{y},{ys^{\prime}}})\rightarrow{e^{\prime}}\ \mid\ \ldots\}\Leftarrow\ {\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{ys}}]

The rule
Chk-Alt
reduces the above into
obligations for each alternative. For the
OC

case, we get:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}\ \mid\ {ys}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ {\texttt{OC}}({{y},{ys^{\prime}}})\rightarrow{e^{\prime}}\Leftarrow\ {\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{ys}}]

By the definition of
\mathsf{unapply}
the above judgment reduces to

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ e^{\prime}\Leftarrow\ {\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{ys}}]

where
\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}
is
\mathrm{\Gamma}
extended with the bindings computed by
\mathsf{unapply}

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ \mathrm{\Gamma};\ {{y}}\!:\!{\alpha};\ {{ys^{\prime}}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{y\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]};\ {{ys}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{ys^{\prime}}}]}

As
e^{\prime}
is
\texttt{{if}}\;{x}\leq{y}\;\texttt{{then}}\;{e^{\prime}_{\leq}}\;\texttt{{else}}\;\ldots

by rule
Chk-If
, eliding the else-branch,
and recalling that
e^{\prime}_{\leq}\doteq\texttt{{let}}\;{tl}=e_{y}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{x}

(
fig.

7.9
) the above judgment reduces to

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime};\ {x}\leq{y}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ \texttt{{let}}\;{tl}=e_{y}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{x}\Leftarrow\ {\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{ys}}]

(7.4)

In the sequel, we will show how the synthesis rules establish

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ e_{{y}}\Rightarrow t_{{y}}

(7.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq};{{tl}}\!:\!{t_{y}}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ e_{{x}}\Rightarrow{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{tl}}]

(7.6)

which
Chk-Let
and
Chk-Syn
combine with the subtyping judgment

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq};{{tl}}\!:\!{t_{y}}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ {\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{tl}}]\prec:\ {\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{ys}}]\quad\mbox{previously derived in (\ref{eq:ins:seven})}

to yield the goal (
7.4
).

\square

\begin{array}[]{lcl}t_{\texttt{ins}}&\doteq&{{{x}}\!:\!{\alpha}}\rightarrow{{{{ys}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}}[{\alpha}]}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{ys}}]}}\\
{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{z}}]&\doteq&{\texttt{OL}}[{\alpha}]{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\texttt{len}({\nu})=1+\texttt{len}({{z}})}}\}}}}\\
\mathrm{\Gamma}&\doteq&{\alpha}\!:\!{B};\ {\texttt{insert}}\!:\!{t_{{ins}}};\ {{x}}\!:\!{\alpha};\ {{ys}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}}[{\alpha}]}\\
\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}&\doteq&\mathrm{\Gamma};\ {{y}}\!:\!{\alpha};\ {{ys^{\prime}}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{y\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]};\ {{ys}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{ys^{\prime}}}]}\\
\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq}&\doteq&\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime};\ {x}\leq{y}\\
e^{\prime}&\doteq&\texttt{{if}}\;{x}\leq{y}\;\texttt{{then}}\;{e^{C}_{\leq}}\;\texttt{{else}}\;\ldots\\
e^{\prime}_{\leq}&\doteq&\texttt{{let}}\;{tl}=e_{y}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{x}\\
e_{{y}}&\doteq&{{\texttt{OC}[\alpha_{{x}}]}\;{{y}}}\;{{ys^{\prime}}}\\
e_{{x}}&\doteq&{{\texttt{OC}[\alpha]}\;{{x}}}\;{{tl}}\\
\alpha_{{x}}&\doteq&{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{{x}\leq\nu}}\}}}}\\
t_{y}&\doteq&{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha_{x},{ys^{\prime}}}]\\
\end{array}

Figure 7.9:
Definitions for checking and synthesizing types for
insert

fig.

7.1
.

7.3.4
Synthesis

As we said at the outset, the key
mechanism to supporting datatypes
are refined
data constructors
.
Their types are applied at construction
sites in a manner identical to plain
function application, where given the
input type we synthesize the output.
Hence, to synthesize the types for constructor
applications we add the rule
Syn-Data

shown in
fig.

7.5
.
The rule synthesizes the type for a data
constructor
D
by looking up its type
in the environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}
; after this,
the rules for application

i.e.

Syn-App
from
fig.

3.5

suffice for constructor applications.

Example: Synthesis in
insert

Lets see how the synthesis rules let us establish the
judgments (
7.5
) and (
7.6
)
that are needed to check the
then
branch of the
implementation of
insert
from
fig.

7.1
.

First, lets establish the type synthesized
for the constructor application
of
7.5
(where the abbreviations
\alpha_{x}
,
t_{y}

are summarized in
fig.

7.9
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ {{\texttt{OC}[\alpha_{{x}}]}\;{{y}}}\;{{ys^{\prime}}}\Rightarrow{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha_{x},{ys^{\prime}}}]

Syn-Data
synthesizes the polymorphic type
of the constructor
OC
(
7.1
) and

Syn-TApp
synthesizes its instantiation to yield

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ {\texttt{OC}[\alpha_{{x}}]}\Rightarrow{{{z}}\!:\!{\alpha_{{x}}}}\rightarrow{{{{zs}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha_{{x}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{{z}\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha_{x},{zs}}]}}

(7.7)

The rule
Syn-App
requires that the types of
the first and second arguments
{y}
and
{ys^{\prime}}

must be subtypes of the constructor’s respective input
types.
The subtyping for the first argument
y
follows
from the validity of the entailment

\forall{x},{y},\nu.\ {x}\leq{y}\ \Rightarrow\ \nu={y}\ \Rightarrow\ {x}\leq\nu

The subtyping for the second argument was established
in (
7.2
).
Hence,
Syn-App
establishes (
7.5
).

Next, lets synthesize a type for the constructor
application (
7.6
) in the environment

\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq}
extended by binding
t_{y}

synthesized in (
7.5
) to
{tl}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq};{{tl}}\!:\!{t_{y}}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ {{\texttt{OC}[\alpha]}\;{{x}}}\;{{tl}}\Rightarrow{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{tl}}]

Again via
Syn-Data
and
Syn-TApp

the constructor’s instance type is

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq};{{tl}}\!:\!{t_{y}}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ {\texttt{OC}[\alpha]}\Rightarrow{{{z}}\!:\!{\alpha}}\rightarrow{{{{zs}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{{z}\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{\alpha,{zs}}]}}

(7.8)

As
\mathrm{\Gamma}({x})=\alpha
we trivially get that the first
argument
{x}
is a subtype of the first input type
\alpha
.
Similarly from the binding
{{tl}}\!:\!{t_{y}}
,
the second argument
{tl}
is a subtype
of the second input type
{\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{{x}\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]
,
after substituting the parameter
{z}
for the actual
{x}
.
Hence, by
Syn-App
and the type of the
constructor (
7.8
), we arrive at our
destination (
7.6
).

\square

\textrm{{Type Synthesis}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Rightarrow t$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma}(D)=t}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash D\Rightarrow t}{{{\textsc{Syn-Data}}}}

Figure 7.10:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
: Rules for Type Synthesis

7.4
Verification Conditions

The declarative rules for checking destructor types and synthesizing
constructor types readily translate to an algorithm for verification
condition generation.

Subtyping

The subtyping constraint for two polymorphic
datatypes types follows the rule
Sub-Data
.
As shown in
fig.

7.11
,
the constraint is the conjuction of the
constraint generated by the top-level
refinements of the data types (
c_{o}
),
the positive (
c_{p}
) and negative (
c_{n}
)
type components.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{sub}&:&(T\times T)\rightarrow C\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{sub}({{C}[{\overline{s}}]}{{{\{{{\nu_{1}}\!:\!{p_{1}}}\}}}},\ {{C}[{\overline{t}}]}{{{\{{{\nu_{2}}\!:\!{p_{2}}}\}}}})&\doteq&c_{o}\wedge c_{p}\wedge c_{n}\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad\overline{p}&=&\mathsf{polarities}(\delta,\ C)\\
\quad\quad c_{o}&=&\forall{{\nu_{1}}\!:\!{b}}.\ {p_{1}}\Rightarrow{{p_{2}}[\nu_{2}:=\nu_{1}]}\\
\quad\quad c_{p}&=&\bigwedge_{\forall i.p_{i}\in\{+,\pm\}}\mathsf{sub}(s_{i},\ t_{i})\\
\quad\quad c_{n}&=&\bigwedge_{\forall i.p_{i}\in\{-,\pm\}}\mathsf{sub}(t_{i},\ s_{i})\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 7.11:
Subtyping Constraints for
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
, extends cases of
fig.

6.6

Checking

Recall that the
\mathsf{check}
function takes an environment

\mathrm{\Gamma}
, term
e
and type
t
and returns a Horn
constraint
c
whose satisfiability implies
\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t

(
3.4.3
).
Figure
7.12
shows how
we extend the function to account for
destructor (pattern-match) terms

\texttt{{switch}}\;y\;\{\overline{a}\}
by invoking

\mathsf{checkAlt}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ a,\ t)
which implements
rule
Chk-Swt
by computing the conjunction of
the constraints for
each
alternative.
Thus, the hard work is done by

\mathsf{checkAlt}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ {D}({\overline{z}})\rightarrow{e},\ t)

which implements rule
Chk-Alt
.
To do so,
\mathsf{checkAlt}
first obtains the data
constructor’s type
s
at this instance.
Next, it uses
\mathsf{unapply}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ \overline{z},\ s)

to obtain the environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}
extended with types
for the pattern binders.
Finally, it recursively invokes
\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime},\ e,\ t)

to recursively compute the constraint for the pattern-expression
under the extended environment.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{check}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E\times T)\rightarrow C\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ \texttt{{switch}}\;y\;\{\overline{a}\},\ t)&\doteq&\bigwedge_{a\in\overline{a}}\ \mathsf{checkAlt}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ a,\ t)\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{checkAlt}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times X\times A\times T)\rightarrow C\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{checkAlt}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ {D}({\overline{z}})\rightarrow{e},\ t)&\doteq&({\overline{{z::s}}})\Rightarrow({{y::s^{\prime}}})\Rightarrow c\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad c&=&\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime},\ e,\ t)\\
\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};{\overline{{z}\!:\!{s}};{y}\!:\!{s^{\prime}}}\ \mbox{as}\ \mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}&=&\mathsf{unapply}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ \overline{z},\ s)\\
\quad\quad s&=&\mathsf{ctor}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ y,\ D)\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{synth}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E)\rightarrow(C\times T)\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ D)&\doteq&(\mathit{true},\ \mathrm{\Gamma}(D)=t)\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 7.12:
Verification Condition Generation for
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
, extends cases of
fig.

6.6

Synthesis

The function
\mathsf{synth}
, shown in
fig.

7.12
,
implements rule
Syn-Data
by returning the
environment’s (polymorphic) type for the constructor

D
.
This lets us then handle constructor applications just
like plain function applications.

\displaystyle c\ \doteq\

\displaystyle\forall{x},\ {ys}.

\displaystyle\quad\forall{y},\ {ys^{\prime}}.\ \texttt{len}({{ys}})=1+\texttt{len}({{ys^{\prime}}})\ \Rightarrow

\displaystyle\quad\quad{x}\leq{y}\ \Rightarrow c_{y}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\forall{tl}.\ \texttt{len}({{tl}})=1+\texttt{len}({{ys^{\prime}}})\ \Rightarrow\ c_{x}\ \wedge

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\forall\nu.\ \texttt{len}({\nu})=1+\texttt{len}({{tl}})\Rightarrow\texttt{len}({\nu})=1+\texttt{len}({{ys}})

\displaystyle\quad\wedge{x}\not\leq{y}\ \Rightarrow\ \ldots\mbox{(constraint for $\texttt{{else}}$ branch)}

\displaystyle\wedge\ldots\mbox{(constraint for $\texttt{ON}$ case)}

\displaystyle c_{y}\ \doteq\

\displaystyle\forall{\nu}.\ \nu={y}\ \Rightarrow\ \kappa_{y}({\nu})\ \wedge

\displaystyle\forall{\nu}.\ {y}\leq\nu\ \Rightarrow\ (\kappa_{y}({\nu})\wedge{y}\leq\nu)

\displaystyle c_{x}\ \doteq\

\displaystyle\forall{\nu}.\ \nu={x}\ \Rightarrow\ \kappa_{x}({\nu})\ \wedge

\displaystyle\forall{\nu}.\ \kappa_{y}({\nu})\ \Rightarrow\ (\kappa_{x}({\nu})\wedge{x}\leq\nu)

Figure 7.13:
Verification Condition for definition of
insert
(
fig.

7.1
).

Example: VC for
insert

Figure
7.13
shows a part
of the VC generated by invoking
\mathsf{check}
on
the environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}
, the definition of

insert
from
fig.

7.8

– with the type applications replaced
with holes
{\alpha}{{\{{\star}\}}}
–
and the goal type
t_{\texttt{ins}}
.
(The abbreviations
\mathrm{\Gamma}
and
t_{\texttt{ins}}
are
shown in
fig.

7.9
.)
The VC generation recurses into the body,
to generate two constraints for the “cons”
and “nil” cases respectively.
The latter is elided for brevity.
The former adds the pattern match binders
for
y
and
ys^{\prime}
and adds the
hypothesis that the size of
ys

is one more than that of
ys^{\prime}
.
We then recurse into the
if
-expression:
with one sub-constraint for the
then

branch (with the hypothesis
{x}\leq{y}
)
and a second for the
else
branch,
elided for brevity.
Recall that the
then
-expression is

\texttt{{let}}\;{tl}=e_{y}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{x}

Where
e_{y}
and
e_{x}
are the two
constructor applications in
fig.

7.9
.
The
then
-constraint has a sub-constraint
c_{y}

that arises from calling
\mathsf{synth}
on
e_{y}
and which
also produces the hypothesis that the size of
tl
is
one more than that of
ys^{\prime}
.
Similarly, we then again invoke
\mathsf{synth}
on
e_{x}

to get
c_{x}
and the result type: a list
\nu
whose
length is one more than
{tl}
which must then imply the
post-condition, that the output
\nu
’s size is in fact,
one greater than the input
ys
.

Next, lets look at the constraint
c_{y}

shown in
fig.

7.13
, returned by
\mathsf{synth}

on the constructor application term

e_{{y}}\ \doteq\ {{\texttt{OC}[{\alpha}{{\{{\star}\}}}]}\;{{y}}}\;{{ys^{\prime}}}

As described in
section

6.4
, we generate
a fresh template
{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{y}({\nu})}}\}}}}

for the hole, which means that at this site, the
constructor
OC
has the following type
(analogous to that shown in (
7.7
),
except with
\kappa_{y}({\nu})
instead of
the to-be-inferred refinement
{x}\leq\nu
:)

{{{z}}\!:\!{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{{y}}({\nu})}}\}}}}}}\rightarrow{{{{zs}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{{y}}({\nu})\wedge{z}\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{{y}}({\nu})}}\}}}}},{zs}}]}}

Thus,
c_{y}
has two conjuncts,
one each for the subtyping obligations
for the inputs
{y}
,
{ys^{\prime}}
.
The first conjunct ensures that the
first argument
{y}
is indeed a
subtype of the constructor’s first input.
The second conjunct checks that the
second input
{ys^{\prime}}
whose type,
per
\mathsf{unapply}
is
{\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{{y}\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]

(as shown in
\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\leq}
in
fig.

7.9
)
is a subtype of the second input of the constructor.

Finally, lets consider the constraint
c_{x}

shown in
fig.

7.13
that is returned by

\mathsf{synth}
on the constructor application term

e_{{x}}\ \doteq\ {{\texttt{OC}[{\alpha}{{\{{\star}\}}}]}\;{{x}}}\;{{tl}}

Again, we generate a fresh template for the hole

{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{x}({\nu})}}\}}}}

which means that at this site, the constructor
OC

has the type

{{{z}}\!:\!{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{{x}}({\nu})}}\}}}}}}\rightarrow{{{{zs}}\!:\!{{\texttt{OL}}[{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{{x}}({\nu})\wedge{z}\leq\nu}}\}}}}}]}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{{x}}({\nu})}}\}}}}},{zs}}]}}

Hence, the constructor application yields the
constraint
c_{{x}}
with one conjunct
for each of the input arguments
x
and
tl
.
As the output type of the
first

constructor application, and so the
type of
tl
is

{{\texttt{OL}^{+}}[{{{\alpha}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{{y}}({\nu})}}\}}}}},{ys^{\prime}}}]}

the second conjunct of
c_{{x}}
– which
represents the subtyping obligation for the
input argument
tl
– has the antecedent

\kappa_{{y}}({\nu})
.

Solution
We encourage the reader to verify that the assignment

\displaystyle\kappa_{{y}}({\nu})

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ {x}\leq\nu

\displaystyle\kappa_{{x}}({\nu})

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ \mathit{true}

satisfies the Horn constraint in
fig.

7.13
, by yielding,
after substitution, a valid VC, thereby verifying that
insert

implements
t_{\texttt{ins}}
.

\square

7.5
Discussion

To sum up, in this chapter we saw how to extend the language
to support refinements on user defined algebraic data-types.
We saw how to encode three classes of properties – invariants
of individual datum, properties relating multiple data, and
properties of the structure itself – using a single mechanism:
a refined type for the data constructors.
This approach lets us reuse the rules for function
application

to
generalize
properties when
constructing
the structure.
Dually, it lets us define a notion of
un-application
to let
us
instantiate
properties when
destructing
the structure.

Refinements on Data vs. Type Constructors

Our approach is using refinements of data constructors.
An alternative approach would be to refine type constructors.
For example, the ordered lists of
section

7.1.2

could be alternatively defined as
list

int

{
l
:

isOrdered

l
}
,
where
isOrdered
is a boolean function that checks is the input
list is ordered.

[
Igarashi15
]
compare the two alternative approaches and conclude
that refinements of type constructors are easier for the programmer to

specify
, but refinements on data constructors are much easier to
(automatically)
verify
.

Types as a Decision Procedure

The ease of verification arises from the fact that
refined data constructors give rise to a simple
syntax-directed approach to establish invariants
of complex heap-allocated data structures.
Crucially, this approach does not require the
expensive blur and materialize mechanisms of
shape analysis
[
SagivRepsWilhelm02j
]
,
or the undecidable (universal) quantifier-based
reasoning used by Floyd-Hoare logic based
deductive verifiers like
Dafny

[
dafny
]
.
Instead,
subtyping
provides a syntactic
proof system for checking entailment between
quantfied assertions.
Subtyping lets us prove the (quantified) assertion
that if every element of a list is positive then
every element is non-negative, by proving the
(quantifier-free) assertion that if
\nu

is positive then
\nu
is non-negative.
Similarly, the constructor application
is a syntactic heuristic for generalizing
facts about individual elements into facts
about the whole structures:
e.g.
if
x
is
a
nat
and
xs
is a
list
(
nat
)
, then

Cons
(
x
,

xs
)
is a
list
(
nat
)
.
Finally, the destructor sites provide
a syntactic heuristic for instantiating
quantified facts about the whole structure:

e.g.
if
xs
is a
list
(
nat
)
and
is destructed to
Cons
(
x
,

xs
’)

then
x
is a
nat
.

Refinements for Pointer-based Structures

While this chapter develops this ideas for a purely functional
language, related ideas have been proposed for imperative languages.
Notable examples include
[
Madhu2013
]
which introduces the idea of
“Natural Proofs” which shows how separation-logic based verifiers
need only fold and unfold recursive predicates (which encode the same
information as our recursive types) at the equivalent of constructor
application and destruction sites, and
[
Bakst16
]
which shows
how to add refinements to an alias type system
[
AliasTypesRec
]

that precisely tracks locations and aliasing, yielding an expressive
and automated way to reason about invariants of pointer-based data
structures.

Chapter 8
Refinement Polymorphism

Modern programming languages allow code and data to
abstract over the concrete types that
they work with. For example, it is commonplace to
define polymorphic arrays or hash-tables that can,

e.g.
hold
int
or
string
or other values,
and to write type-agnostic functions that operate
over these structures.
In §
6
we saw how polymorphic types
could be instantiated at different use-sites
to precisely track invariants in a context-sensitive
manner.

However, we also often write monomorphic
functions and data that nevertheless abstract
over the refinements satisfied by the
data they manipulate.
This abstraction is often implicit,

i.e.
not captured in the function’s
type specification, which makes it
hard to establish the invariants
at usage sites.
Hence, next, lets develop a means
for specifying signatures that
abstract over the refinements,
see how to automatically verify
these signatures, and learn how
to instantiate them automatically
at client sites, all while keeping
verification efficiently decidable.

8.1
Examples

Lets crack our knuckles with some illustrative examples.

8.1.1
Problem: Picking the Right Specification

Consider
maxI
(
x
,
y
)
which returns the larger of
x
and
y
.

⬇

val

maxI

:

int

=>

int

=>

int

let

maxI

=

(
x
,

y
)

=>

{

if

(
x

<

y
)

{

y

}

else

{

x

}

};

Suppose that we want to verify that
maxI
(
a
,

b
)

must be non-negative if
a
and
b
are non-negative.

⬇

val

bigger
:

nat

=>

nat

=>

nat

let

bigger

=

(
a
,

b
)

=>

{

maxI
(
a
,

b
)

};

Unfortunately, the above
bigger
will
fail

as the specified type is silent about what properties
hold of the
int
returned by
maxI
!

Premature Specialization

We could try to type
maxI
as

⬇

val

maxI

:

nat

=>

nat

=>

nat

This signature would let us verify
bigger

but would prematurely restrict
maxI
to
non-negative values.
For example, if we had a refinement for
valid 8-bit
int
s

⬇

type

int8

=

int
[
v
|0

<=

v

&&

v

<

256]

we would end up
rejecting
the perfectly correct

⬇

val

brighter

:

int8

=>

int8

=>

int8

let

brighter

=

(
c1
,

c2
)

=>

{

maxI
(
c1
,

c2
)

}

Instead, we might specify that the output

⬇

val

maxI
:

x
:
int

=>

y
:
int

=>

int
[
v
|
v
=
x

||

v
=
y
]

This would let us verify
bigger
and
brighter
.
This approach suffices when there are just two

int
inputs but does not scale up to unbounded
collections, as in
maxL
which computes the
largest element of a
list

⬇

let

rec

maxL

=

(
default
,

xs
)

=>

{

fold_right
(
maxI
,

default
,

xs
)

};

Since the list is unbounded we have no way to say
the output is
one of
the elements of
xs
.
We could use an
existential quantifier
but
that would lead to verification conditions that
are outside the boundaries of decidable SMT based
validity checking.
Now suppose we use
maxL
with different kinds of values,
for example:

⬇

val

biggerL

:

list
(
nat
)

=>

nat

let

biggerL

=

(
xs
)

=>

{

maxL
(0,

xs
)

}

val

brighterL

:

list
(
int8
)

=>

int8

let

brighterL

=

(
xs
)

=>

{

maxL
(0,

xs
)

}

How can we verify that
maxList
returns
a
nat
or
int8
values when invoked with
lists of
nat
and
int8
values respectively?

8.1.2
Solution: Abstracting Over Refinements

If we take a step back, we might notice that the fact
that
maxI
returns
one of
its two inputs
x

and
y
can be rephrased as follows: if there is some
property
p
that
both
inputs satisfy, then the
output must also satisfy
p
.
That is, we can make the refinement
p
itself be
a
parameter
in the specification of
maxI

⬇

val

maxI
:

int
[
v
|
p

v
]

=>

int
[
v
|
p

v
]

=>

int
[
v
|
p

v
]

The above specification says that
for any

predicate
p
over
int
values,
maxI
takes two
inputs
x
and
y
which respectively satisfy
p

x

and
p

y
and returns an output
v
that satisfies
p

v
.

It is only useful to parameterize
over refinements if there is some convenient
way to
instantiate
the parameters.
We will extend the machinery for
inferring refinements for holes
{\{{\star}\}}
to
automatically instantiate the refinement
parameters at the usage-sites —
e.g.
in
bigger

and
brighter
— where
p
will be
instantiated to the concrete refinements

\displaystyle p_{\texttt{nat}}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \lambda\nu.\ 0\leq\nu

(8.1)

\displaystyle p_{\texttt{int8}}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \lambda\nu.\ 0\leq\nu\wedge\nu<256

(8.2)

thereby verifying those two client functions.

The above method scales up to unbounded lists.
We can specify

⬇

val

maxL
:

int
[
v
|
p

v
]

=>

list
(
int
[
v
|
p

v
])

=>

int
[
v
|
p

v
]

which says that when
maxL
is given a
default

int

and a
list
(
int
)
all of which satisfy the predicate
p
,
the output is also an
int
that satisfies
p
.
Once again, we can verify the clients
biggerL
and

brighterL
by instantiating
p
as
p_{\texttt{nat}}
and

p_{\texttt{int8}}
at the respective call-sites.

Preserving Decidability

At first glance, it may appear that these abstract
predicate variables
p
have taken us into the realm
of higher-order logics, and that we must leave decidable
SMT based checking at the door.
Fortunately, that is not the case.
We will see how to
encode abstract refinements
p
as
uninterpreted
functions
in the refinement logic, which will allow us
to continue with SMT based Horn VC checking.

8.1.3
Abstracting Refinements over Data Types

Refinement parameters are a natural fit
for
datatype
definitions.

Dependent Pairs

For example, we can specify a
pair
datatype where
there is some relationship between the first and
second component, by parameterizing the datatype
definition with a refinement parameter
p
:

⬇

type

pair
(’
a
,

’
b
)(
p

:

’
a

=>

’
b

=>

bool
)

=

|

MkPair
(
x
:’
a
,

y
:’
b
[
v
|
p

x

v
])

The definition is parameterized by a binary
predicate
p
that relates the two elements
of the
pair
.
Hence, we can define the set of
int
pairs
where the second component exceeds the first
as:

⬇

type

incPair

=

pair
(
int
,
int
)((
x
,

y
)

=>

x

<

y
)

The refinement parameter is
asserted

when the pair is
constructed
.
Hence,
okPair
will verify but
badPair

will be rejected:

⬇

val

okPair
:

n
:
int

=>

incPair

let

okPair

=

(
n
)

=>

{

MkPair
(
n
,

n
+1)

};

val

badPair
:

x
:
int

=>

incPair

let

badPair

=

(
n
)

=>

{

MkPair
(
n
,

n
-1)

};

Dually, the refinement is
assumed

when the pair is
destructed
, and so
the below verifies:

⬇

val

chkPair

:

incPair

=>

nat

let

chkPair

=

(
p
)

=>

{

switch

(
p
)

{

MkPair
(
x1
,

x2
)

=>

x2

-

x1

}

};

Abstracting Refinements over Lists

The combination of recursion and refinement
parameters allows us to compactly specify
various interesting properties of collections
without baking them into the datatype’s
definition.
Recall the definition of ordered lists
of non-decreasing values from
section

7.1

⬇

type

olist
(’
a
)

=

|

ONil

|

OCons

(
x
:’
a
,

xs
:
olist
(’
a
[
v
|
x
<=
v
]))

Order is established by the signature for
OCons

that requires that every element of the tail
xs

is greater than the head
x
.
Refinement parameters let us abstract the particular
relation: we can specify a generic
list
as

⬇

type

list
(’
a
)(
p
:’
a

=>’
a

=>

bool
)

=

|

Nil

|

Cons
(
x
:’
a
,

xs
:
list
(’
a
[
v
|
p

x

v
])((
y
,
z
)

=>

p

y

z
))

The definition is parameterized by a binary predicate
p

that relates
every
element of the tail with the head

x
at each application of the constructor
Cons
.
That is, the specification says that if

\texttt{Cons}(x_{1},\texttt{Cons}(x_{2},\ldots\texttt{Cons}(x_{n},\texttt{Nil}))):\texttt{list}{(\alpha)}(p)

then for each
1\leq i<j\leq n
we have
p(x_{i},x_{j})
.

Ordered Lists

We can now specify non-decreasing lists by instantiating the
refinement parameter
p
appropriately:

⬇

type

incList
(’
a
)

=

list
(’
a
)((
x1
,

x2
)

=>

x1
<=
x2
)

val

checkInc

:

(
int
)

=>

incList
(
int
)

let

checkInc

=

(
x
)

=>

{

Cons
(
x
,

Cons
(
x
+1,

Cons
(
x
+2,

Nil
)))

};

Similarly, we can define non-increasing lists

⬇

type

decList
(’
a
)

=

list
(’
a
)((
x1
,
x2
)

=>

x1
>=
x2
)

val

checkDec

:

(
int
)

=>

decList
(
int
)

let

checkDec

=

(
x
)

=>

{

Cons
(
x
+2,

Cons
(
x
+1,

Cons
(
x
,

Nil
)))

};

or
duplicate-free
lists, simply by changing the relation:

⬇

type

uniqList
(’
a
)

=

list
(’
a
)((
x1
,
x2
)

=>

x1
!=
x2
)

val

checkUnique

:

(
int
)

=>

uniqList
(
int
)

let

checkUnique

=

(
x
)

=>

{

Cons
(
x
+2,

Cons
(
x
,

Cons
(
x
+1,

Nil
)))

};

We can omit the refinement instantiation to write just

list
(’
a
)
to denote the trivially (un)refined instance

list
(’
a
)((
x1
,
x2
)

=>

true
)
.
Now, the machinery developed for reasoning about datatypes
in
section

7.1
lets us verify properties
of code manipulating (abstractly) refined lists,

e.g.
that the following insertion-sort produces
ordered lists

⬇

val

isort

:

list
(’
a
)

=>

incList
(’
a
)

let

isort

=

(
xs
)

=>

{

foldr
(
insert
,

Nil
,

xs
);

};

8.2
Types and Terms

Figure
8.1
summarizes how we extend
the language of types to include abstraction
over refinements, and the language of terms to
include instantiation of refinement variables.

Refinement Variables
We write
\rho

to denote refinement variables that abstract
over concrete or specific refinements.
Refinement variables
\rho
are of the
form
{\kappa}\!:\!{{\overline{b}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}
,

i.e.
represent a
bool
valued
predicates

over some (non-empty) sequence of base types.

Abstracting over Refinements
We abstract
over refinements either in function signatures of
the form
\forall{\rho}.\ {t}
or in data type
definitions
\langle{C},\;\overline{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}/{p}},\;\overline{{\rho}/{p}},\;{\overline{{D}\!:\!{t}}}\rangle

which are now parameterized by a (possibly empty)
sequence of refinement variables and their respective polarities
in addition to
type variables from Figure
7.2
.
For example, we might assign
maxI
the type

t_{\texttt{maxI}}\ \doteq\ \forall{{\kappa}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}}.\ {{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}}}

(8.3)

which captures the intuition that when given two
{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}
inputs,
the function is guaranteed to return an
{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}
output, for any
refinement
\kappa
on
int
values. In the sequel, we will
elide the refinement variables’ sort when it is clear from
the context.

Implicit Refinement Application
We instantiate refinements
either in
refinement application
terms of the
form
e[\star]
or in
type-constructor application

types of the form
{C}[{\overline{t}}](\overline{\varphi})
,
where
\varphi
is a
concrete refinement

\lambda{\overline{{x}\!:\!{b}}}.\ {p}

which is a boolean valued predicate over a set of variables
\overline{{x}\!:\!{b}}
.
We leave the refinement instances
implicit

for terms (denoted by
\star
) as, like type instances,
these are ubiquitous, and hence, should be automatically
synthesized.
For example,
brighter
is defined via the following
term in which the refinement variable in the signature
of
maxI
is implicitly instantiated at the usage site:

\texttt{{let}}\ \texttt{brighter}\ =\ \lambda{x,y}.\ {\ {{\texttt{maxI}[\star]}\;{x}}\;{y}}

(8.4)

Explicit Refinement Application

However, we allow
explicit
refinement instances
for data types as we often want to specify signatures
where the constructor is refined with a particular
concrete refinement. For example, the definitions
of
incPair
,
incList
, and
decList
from

section

8.1
are, respectively:

type

incPair

\displaystyle=\texttt{pair}{(\texttt{{int}},\texttt{{int}})}(\lambda{x_{1},x_{2}}.\ {x_{1}<x_{2}})

(8.5)

type

incList

\displaystyle=\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}(\lambda{x_{1},x_{2}}.\ {x_{1}\leq x_{2}})

(8.6)

type

decList

\displaystyle=\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}(\lambda{x_{1},x_{2}}.\ {x_{1}\geq x_{2}})

(8.7)

Ref. Var.

\rho

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

{\kappa}\!:\!{{\overline{b}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}

Abs. Refine.

\varphi

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

\lambda{\overline{{x}\!:\!{b}}}.\ {p}

Datatypes

\delta

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

\langle{C},\;\overline{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}/{p}},\;\overline{{\rho}/{p}},\;{\overline{{D}\!:\!{t}}}\rangle

Basic Types

b

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

\ldots

from Fig.
7.2

\ \mid\

{C}[{\overline{t}}](\overline{\varphi})

datatypes

Types

t

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

…

from
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
Fig.
7.2

\ \mid\

\forall{\rho}.\ {t}

ref. polymorphism

Terms

e

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

\ldots

from
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
Fig.
7.2

\ \mid\

e[\star]

refinement application

Figure 8.1:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}
: Syntax of Types and Terms

8.3
Declarative Typing

Next, lets look at how the declarative typing rules are extended to
accomodate abstract refinements.

8.3.1
Well-formedness

\textrm{{Well-formedness}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t:k$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma};{{\kappa}\!:\!{{\overline{b}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}}\vdash t:k}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\forall{{\kappa}\!:\!{{\overline{b}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}}.\ {t}:\star}{{{\textsc{Wf-RAbs}}}}

Figure 8.2:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\rho}}
: Rules for Well-formedness

Rule
Wf-RAbs
shown in
fig.

8.2

states that a refinement polymorphic type is well-formed
with kind
\star
, if the underlying (quantified)
type is also well-formed.

8.3.2
Subtyping

\textrm{{Abs. Refinement Implication}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\varphi_{1}\prec:\varphi_{2}$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma};{\overline{{x_{1}}\!:\!{b}}}\vdash p_{1}\Rightarrow{p_{2}}[\overline{x_{2}}:=\overline{x_{1}}]}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\lambda{\overline{{x_{1}}\!:\!{b}}}.\ {p_{1}}\prec:\lambda{\overline{{x_{2}}\!:\!{b}}}.\ {p_{2}}}{{{\textsc{Sub-CRef}}}}

\textrm{{Subtyping}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t_{1}\prec:t_{2}$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash s_{i}\prec:t_{i}\ \mbox{for each}\ i.p_{i}\in\{+,\pm\}\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash t_{i}\prec:s_{i}\ \mbox{for each}\ i.p_{i}\in\{-,\pm\}\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\phi_{i}\prec:\varphi_{i}\ \mbox{for each}\ i.p_{ri}\in\{+,\pm\}\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\varphi_{i}\prec:\phi_{i}\ \mbox{for each}\ i.p_{ri}\in\{-,\pm\}\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};{{\nu_{1}}\!:\!{{{\{{{{C}[{\overline{s}}]}\!:\!{p_{1}}}\}}}}}\vdash{p_{2}}[\nu_{2}:=\nu_{1}]\and(\overline{p},\overline{p_{r}})=\mathsf{polarities}(\delta,\ C)\quad\ }{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash{{C}[{\overline{s}}](\overline{\phi})}{{{\{{{\nu_{1}}\!:\!{p_{1}}}\}}}}\prec:{{C}[{\overline{t}}](\overline{\varphi})}{{{\{{{\nu_{2}}\!:\!{p_{2}}}\}}}}}{{{\textsc{Sub-Con}}}}

Figure 8.3:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\rho}}
: Rules for Subtyping

Figure
8.3
summarizes the new rules for subtyping.
The rule
Sub-CRef
shows the rule for checking
subsumption between two concrete refinements
\lambda{\overline{{x_{1}}\!:\!{b}}}.\ {p_{1}}

and
\lambda{\overline{{x_{2}}\!:\!{b}}}.\ {p_{2}}
, by checking that
p_{1}
is subsumed
by
p_{2}
after suitably renaming the bound variables.
The second rule
Sub-Con
shows how to extend the rule for checking
subtyping between two refined instances

{{C}[{\overline{s}}](\overline{\phi})}{{{\{{{\nu_{1}}\!:\!{p_{1}}}\}}}}

and

{{C}[{\overline{t}}](\overline{\varphi})}{{{\{{{\nu_{2}}\!:\!{p_{2}}}\}}}}

of a type constructor
C
, by checking the subsumption holds between
the corresponding concrete refinements
\phi
and
\varphi
of the two
instances according to their polarity using
Sub-CRef
, and checking the subsumption of the
base refinements
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
and type components as before.

Example: Subtyping in
incPair

As an example, let

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{=}}\ {a}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{=}}\ \lambda{x,y}.\ {x=a\wedge y=a+1}

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{=}}\ \lambda{x,y}.\ {x<y}

and consider the subtyping obligation

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\vdash\ {\texttt{pair}{(\texttt{{int}},\texttt{{int}})}(\varphi)}\ \prec:\ {\texttt{pair}{(\texttt{{int}},\texttt{{int}})}(\phi)}

that could arise in the course of checking that

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\vdash\ \texttt{MkPair}(a,a+1)\Leftarrow\texttt{incPair}

where
incPair
is defined in
8.5
.
Rule
Sub-Con
reduces the subtyping obligation
to the following concrete refinement subsumption

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\vdash\ \varphi\ \prec:\ \phi

and then
Sub-CRef
reduces the above to the entailment

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma},{x,y}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}

\displaystyle\vdash\ (x=a\wedge y=a+1)\ \Rightarrow\ (x<y)

that is readily validated by the SMT solver.

\square

8.3.3
Checking

\textrm{{Type Checking}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t$}

\frac{\rho={\kappa}\!:\!{{\overline{b}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}\quad\varphi=\lambda{\overline{x}}.\ {f_{\kappa}(\overline{x})}\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};{{f_{\kappa}}\!:\!{{\overline{b}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}}\vdash{e}[\rho:=\varphi]\Leftarrow{s}[\rho:=\varphi]}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow\forall{\rho}.\ {s}}{{{\textsc{Chk-RAbs}}}}

Figure 8.4:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\rho}}
: Rules for Type Checking

The rule
Chk-RAbs
checks that a term
e
implements the
abstractly refined signature
\forall{\rho}.\ {s}
.
The key idea is to use the refinement variable

\rho={\kappa}\!:\!{{\overline{b}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}
to
(1) generate a fresh
uninterpreted function symbol

f_{\kappa}
,
(2) substitute all occurrences of
\kappa
with
f_{\kappa}

in the term
e
and the type
s
, to respectively
obtain
{e}[\kappa:=f_{\kappa}]
and
{s}[\kappa:=f_{\kappa}]

and then
(3) perform the check on the substituted type and term, in an environment
extended with a binding for the uninterpreted function
f_{\kappa}
.

Refinement Instantiation

We replace all occurences of the refinement variable
\rho\doteq{\kappa}\!:\!{\cdot}

with an uninterpreted function
f_{\kappa}
via an operation
{s}[\rho:=\varphi]

shown in
fig.

8.6
which
instantiates
(or
substitutes
)
a concrete refinement
\varphi
for a refinement variable
\rho

in a signature
s
.
The operation traverses
s
to replace
all occurences of
\kappa({\overline{x}})
with
{p}[\overline{y}:=\overline{x}]

when
\rho\doteq{\kappa}\!:\!{\cdot}
and
\varphi\doteq\lambda{\overline{y}}.\ {p}
,

i.e.
we replace the
parameters
of the refinement (
\overline{y}
)
with the
arguments
(
\overline{x}
) in the concrete refinement
p
.

Example: Checking
maxI

Recall the implementation and specification of
maxI
(§
8.1
)

\displaystyle e_{\texttt{maxI}}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \lambda{x,y}.\ {\texttt{{if}}\;x<y\;\texttt{{then}}\;{y}\;\texttt{{else}}\;x}

(8.8)

\displaystyle t_{\texttt{maxI}}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \forall{\kappa}.\ {{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}}}

(8.9)

Lets consider the goal of verifying that the above implementation
checks against its specification (
8.3
)

\emptyset\vdash\ e_{\texttt{maxI}}\Leftarrow t_{\texttt{maxI}}\\

(8.10)

The rule
Chk-RAbs
reduces the above to

\displaystyle\emptyset

\displaystyle\vdash\ e_{\texttt{maxI}}\Leftarrow{{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{f_{\kappa}(\nu)}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{f_{\kappa}(\nu)}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{f_{\kappa}(\nu)}}\}}}}}}}

which rules
Chk-Lam
,
Chk-If
and
Syn-Var

reduce to two goals

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\vdash\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\nu=x}}\}}}}\ \prec:\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{f_{\kappa}(\nu)}}\}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\vdash\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\nu=y}}\}}}}\ \prec:\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{f_{\kappa}(\nu)}}\}}}}

where
\mathrm{\Gamma}
has bindings for the refinement and value parameters

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\doteq\ {f_{\kappa}}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}},{x}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{f_{\kappa}(\nu)}}\}}}}},{y}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{f_{\kappa}(\nu)}}\}}}}}

The two goals above check that the specified output type
is indeed returned in each branch. Both the above reduce to the entailment

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma},{\nu}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}

\displaystyle\vdash\ \nu=y\ \Rightarrow\ f_{\kappa}(\nu)

that is easily confirmed by the SMT solver.

\square

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{ctor}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times D\times X)\rightarrow T\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{ctor}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ D,\ y)&\doteq&{{s}[\overline{\alpha}:=\overline{t}]}[\overline{\rho}:=\overline{\varphi}]\\[3.61371pt]
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad{C}[{\overline{t}}](\overline{\varphi})&=&\mathrm{\Gamma}(y)\\
\quad\quad\forall{{\overline{\alpha}}\!:\!{k}}.{\forall{\overline{\rho}}.\ {s}}&=&\mathrm{\Gamma}(D)\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 8.5:
Meta-functions for Checking Alternatives;
\mathsf{unapply}
as in Figure
7.6

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\textbf{\emph{\mbox{Predicates}}}&{\kappa({\overline{x}})}[\rho:=\varphi]&\doteq&{p}[\overline{y}:=\overline{x}]\ \mbox{if}\ \rho={\kappa}\!:\!{\cdot},\ \varphi=\lambda{\overline{y}}.\ {p}\\
&{p_{1}\bowtie p_{2}}[\rho:=\varphi]&\doteq&{p_{1}}[\rho:=\varphi]\bowtie{p_{2}}[\rho:=\varphi]\\
&{p}[\rho:=\varphi]&\doteq&p\\[7.22743pt]
\textbf{\emph{Abstr. Refs}}&{(\lambda{\overline{{x}\!:\!{b}}}.\ {p})}[\rho:=\varphi]&\doteq&\lambda{\overline{{x}\!:\!{b}}}.\ {({p}[\rho:=\varphi])}\\[7.22743pt]

&{({{x}\!:\!{s}}\rightarrow{t})}[\rho:=\varphi]&\doteq&{{x}\!:\!{{s}[\rho:=\varphi]}}\rightarrow{{t}[\rho:=\varphi]}\\[7.22743pt]
\end{array}

Figure 8.6:
Instantiating a refinement variable
\rho
with a concrete refinement
\varphi
.

Checking Case Alternatives

We continue to check case alternatives using

Chk-Alt
from
fig.

7.5
.
However, we must extend the definition of

\mathsf{ctor}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ D,\ y)
— which
defines the monomorphic instantiation of
the polymorphic type of the data constructor

D
corresponding the case-scrutinee
y
—
to also account for refinement polymorphism.
This extension is summarized by the definition
in
fig.

8.5
, where we obtain both the
monomorphic types
\overline{t}
and additionally
the concrete refinements
\overline{\varphi}
from the
environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}
signature of
y
, and use
those to respectively instantiate the type (
\overline{\alpha}
)
and refinement (
\overline{\rho}
) variables in the signature
of the data constructor
D
, where the latter is done
via the refinement instantiation mechanism described above.

Example: Checking
chkPair

Recall
chkPair
from §
8.1

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle\doteq\ \lambda{p}.\ {\texttt{{switch}}\;p\;\{{\texttt{MkPair}}({x,y})\rightarrow y-x\}}

Lets see how the rules establish that

\displaystyle\emptyset

\displaystyle\vdash\ e\Leftarrow{\texttt{incPair}}\rightarrow{\texttt{{nat}}}

First,

reduces the above to

\displaystyle{p}\!:\!{\texttt{incPair}}

\displaystyle\vdash\ \texttt{{switch}}\;p\;\{{\texttt{MkPair}}({x,y})\rightarrow y-x\}\Leftarrow\texttt{{nat}}

Via

(

) we extend

s\doteq\ \mathsf{ctor}({p}\!:\!{\texttt{incPair}},\ \texttt{MkPair},\ p)

at

\displaystyle::\ \forall{{\alpha,\beta}\!:\!{k}}.{\forall{\kappa}.\ {{{a}\!:\!{\alpha}}\rightarrow{{{b}\!:\!{{\beta}{{\{{\kappa(a,b)}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{pair}}[{\alpha,\beta}](\lambda{a,b}.\ {\kappa(a,b)})}}}}

as
{p}\!:\!{\texttt{incPair}}
, we get

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\doteq\ {{a}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{{b}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{a<b}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{pair}}[{\texttt{{int}},\texttt{{int}}}](\lambda{a,b}.\ {a<b})}}

Thus,
\mathsf{unapply}
(

)
extends the environment with pattern binders

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \mathrm{\Gamma},\ {x}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}},\ {y}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{x<y}\}}}}

to obtain the goal for the case-alternative

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\vdash\ y-x\Leftarrow\texttt{{nat}}

which,

and
