reduce to the entailment

\mathrm{\Gamma},{x,y,\nu}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}\vdash\ (x<y)\Rightarrow(\nu=y-x)\rightarrow(0\leq v)

that is readily verified by the SMT solver.

\square

8.3.4
Synthesis

\textrm{{Type Synthesis}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Rightarrow t$}

\frac{\rho={\kappa}\!:\!{{\overline{b},b}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Rightarrow\forall{\rho}.\ {s}\quad{b}{{\{{\star}\}}}\triangleright{b}{{{\{{{x}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};\overline{{x}\!:\!{b}}\vdash{b}{{{\{{{x}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}:B}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e[\star]\Rightarrow{s}[\rho:=\lambda\overline{{x}\!:\!{b}},{x}\!:\!{b}.{p}]}{{{\textsc{Syn-RApp}}}}

Figure 8.7:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\rho}}
: Rules for Type Synthesis

To make refinement polymorphism ergonomic, we need a way
to automatically synthesize appropriate concrete refinements
for each term
e[\star]
corresponding to
uses

of terms
e
whose types abstract over the refinements.
Otherwise, the programmer would have to bear the burden
of writing concrete refinements at the ubiquitous usage
sites, and worse, the resulting code would be very difficult
to read!
Thus, refinement synthesis is analogous to how we usually
want to relieve the programmer of the burden of providing
(monomorphic) instances at uses of a polymorphic signature.
Next, lets see how this instantiation can be achieved
by the refinement synthesis machinery introduced in

\mathsf{\lambda_{\kappa}}
(§
5
).

Instantiating Refinement Variables

The rule
Syn-RApp
shown in
fig.

8.7

shows how to synthesize types at refinement application sites

e[\star]
.
The rule says if we synthesize for
e

a type
\forall{\rho}.\ {s}
which uses
an abstract refinement variable

\rho\equiv{\kappa}\!:\!{{\overline{b},b}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}

then we can
instantiate

\rho
in
s

with any
well-formed
concrete refinement

\lambda{\overline{{x}\!:\!{b}},{x}\!:\!{b}}.\ {p}

whose sort is compatible with that of
\rho
,

i.e.
that is parameterized by the sorts compatible
with the inputs of
\kappa
.

Observe that
Syn-RApp
mimics

Chk-Rec
from
fig.

5.3

which uses the hole instantiation judgment

s\triangleright t
from
fig.

5.2

to declaratively
guess
a suitable
way to replace the holes
{\{{\star}\}}
in
s

with concrete refinements.
In essence,
Syn-RApp
views the refinement
variable
\rho\equiv{\kappa}\!:\!{{\overline{b},b}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}

which denotes a relation over values of types

\overline{b},b
as an
unknown

refinement over the base type
{b}{{\{{\star}\}}}

and requires that we fill the hole with any
concrete refinement
p
that is well-formed
under an environment extended with (
i.e.
can refer to)
binders
\overline{x}
for the other parameters

\overline{b}
.

Example: Synthesis in
brighter

Lets use
Syn-RApp
to verify
brighter
from §
8.4
. Let

int8

\displaystyle\doteq\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0\leq\nu<256}}\}}}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\doteq\ {\texttt{maxI}}\!:\!{t_{\texttt{maxI}}},{x}\!:\!{\texttt{int8}},{y}\!:\!{\texttt{int8}}

where
t_{\texttt{maxI}}
is from (
8.3
). Using
Syn-Var
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\vdash\ \texttt{maxI}\Rightarrow t_{\texttt{maxI}}

Hence, as
{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\star}\}}}\triangleright\texttt{int8}

and
\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{int8}:B

Syn-RApp
lets us
instantiate the refinement variable
\kappa
quantifying
the signature of
t_{\texttt{maxI}}
with the concrete
refinement
\lambda{x}.\ {0\leq x<256}
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \texttt{maxI}[\star]\Rightarrow{\texttt{int8}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{int8}}\rightarrow{\texttt{int8}}}

Now, the function application rule
Syn-App
lets us establish

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\doteq\ {{{\texttt{maxI}[\star]}\;{x}}\;{y}}\Rightarrow\texttt{int8}

which, via rules
Chk-Syn
and
Chk-Lam

establishes that the implementation of
brighter

\lambda{x,y}.\ {{{\texttt{maxI}[\star]}\;{x}}\;{y}}

indeed checks against its specified type

{\texttt{int8}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{int8}}\rightarrow{\texttt{int8}}}
.

\square

8.4
Verification Conditions

The declarative rules simply guess suitable concrete
refinements at each instantiation site. Next, lets see how
to implement those rules via the method of Horn constraints
introduced in §
5
, as summarized
in
fig.

8.8
.

Checking

To extend the
\mathsf{check}
function to implement
Chk-RAbs

we add a case for checking a term
e
against a type of form

\forall{\rho}.\ {s}
under environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}
.
To this end, we substitute the refinement variable
\rho
with
a concrete refinement
\varphi
corresponding to an uninterpreted function
application
\lambda{\overline{x}}.\ {f_{\kappa}(\overline{x})}

and then return the Horn constraint
c
corresponding to
the VC for checking the (substituted) term
e^{\prime}
against
the (substituted) type
s^{\prime}
.

Example: VC for
maxI

Lets see the VC

\mathsf{check}(\emptyset,\ e_{\texttt{maxI}},\ t_{\texttt{maxI}})

generated to verify that the implementation (
8.8
)
of
maxI
adheres to its specification (
8.9
).
The VC is obtained by substituting the refinement
variable
\kappa
with the uninterpreted function
symbol
f_{\kappa}
and then recursively invoking

\mathsf{check}
on the substituted body and signature,
which produces the VC:

\forall f_{\kappa},{x},{y},\nu.\ f_{\kappa}({x})\Rightarrow f_{\kappa}({y})\Rightarrow((\nu={x}\Rightarrow f_{\kappa}(\nu))\wedge(\nu={y}\Rightarrow f_{\kappa}(\nu)))

which mirrors the entailments for the checking judgment (
8.10
).

\square

Synthesis

Finally, in
fig.

8.8
we extend
\mathsf{synth}

to account for refinement instantiation terms

e[\star]
via an algorithmic implementation
of
Syn-RApp
.
First, we recursively invoke
\mathsf{synth}
to
As in the declarative rule, we recursively
invoke
\mathsf{synth}
to synthesize the
refinement-polymorphic signature

\forall{\rho}.\ {s}
for
e
.
Next, as in
fig.

5.5
we implement the declarative

{b}{{\{{\star}\}}}\triangleright{b}{{{\{{{x}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}

by using
\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma};\overline{{x}\!:\!{b}},\ {b}{{\{{\star}\}}})

to obtain a new
template

{b}{{{\{{{x}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}

where
p
contains refinement (Horn) variables for the unknown
concrete refinements to be used for instantiation.
We use
p
to build a concrete refinement that is substituted
for
\rho
in
s
to return the synthesized type for
e[\star]
,
together with the VC
c
obtained for
e
.

Example: Checking of
brighter

Lets see how the above procedure computes a Horn constraint (VC)
whose satisfiability implies that
brighter
(
8.4
)
implements the specification
{\texttt{int8}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{int8}}\rightarrow{\texttt{int8}}}
.
Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\doteq\ {\texttt{maxI}}\!:\!{t_{\texttt{maxI}}}

\displaystyle e_{\texttt{br}}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \lambda{x,y}.\ {\ {{\texttt{maxI}[\star]}\;{x}}\;{y}}

\displaystyle t_{\texttt{br}}

\displaystyle\doteq\ {\texttt{int8}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{int8}}\rightarrow{\texttt{int8}}}

respectively be the environment, implementation and specification
for
brighter
, where
t_{\texttt{maxI}}
is from (
8.3
).
Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \mathrm{\Gamma},\ {x}\!:\!{\texttt{int8}},\ {y}\!:\!{\texttt{int8}}

By consulting
\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime},\ \texttt{maxI})
returns
(\mathit{true},\ t_{\texttt{maxI}})
where

\displaystyle t_{\texttt{maxI}}

\displaystyle\doteq\ \forall{\kappa}.\ {{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa({\nu})}}\}}}}}}})

Hence, we invoke
\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma},{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\star}\}}},\
)
to obtain the template
{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{\texttt{br}}({\nu})}}\}}}}
with a new Horn variable
\kappa_{\texttt{br}}

denoting the unknown concrete refinement at this instantiation site.
Upon substituting the corresponding concrete refinement for the
refinement variable we get the template (eliding the trivial Horn constraint
\mathit{true}
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime},\ \texttt{maxI}[\star])

\displaystyle\doteq{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{\texttt{br}}({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{\texttt{br}}({\nu})}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\kappa_{\texttt{br}}({\nu})}}\}}}}}}

Then, via the usual cases for function application, we get the VC

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e_{\texttt{br}},\ t_{\texttt{br}})&\doteq&\lx@intercol\forall x.(0\leq x<256)\Rightarrow\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&&\lx@intercol\quad\forall y.(0\leq y<256)\Rightarrow\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&.&&\forall\nu.(\nu=x)\Rightarrow\kappa_{\texttt{br}}({\nu})\\
&&\quad\quad\wedge&\forall\nu.(\nu=y)\Rightarrow\kappa_{\texttt{br}}({\nu})\\
&&\quad\quad\wedge&\forall\nu.\kappa_{\texttt{br}}({\nu})\Rightarrow(0\leq v<256)\end{array}

The first two conjuncts check that the arguments
x
and
y
respectively satisfy
the input types (preconditions) of
\texttt{maxI}[\star]
, and the last conjunct
stipulates that the output type (postcondition) of the call is a valid
int8

value, all assuming the values
x
and
y
inhabit
int8
as specified by the
input type of
brighter
.
The above VC can be satisfied by assigning
\kappa_{\texttt{br}}\doteq\lambda{a}.\ {0\leq a<256}
,
and hence we verify that
brighter
correctly implements its specification.

\square

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{check}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E\times T)\rightarrow C\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e,\ \forall{\rho}.\ {s})&\doteq&({{f_{\kappa}::{\overline{t}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}})\Rightarrow c\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad c&=&\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime},\ {e}[\rho:=\varphi],\ {s}[\rho:=\varphi])\\
\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\prime}&=&\mathrm{\Gamma};{{f_{\kappa}}\!:\!{{\overline{t}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}}\\
\quad\quad\varphi&=&\lambda{\overline{x}}.\ {f_{\kappa}(\overline{x})}\\
\quad\quad{\kappa}\!:\!{{\overline{t}}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}&=&\rho\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{synth}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E)\rightarrow(C\times T)\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e[\star])&\doteq&(c,{s}[\rho:={\lambda\overline{{x}\!:\!{b}},{x}\!:\!{b}.{p}}])\\[3.61371pt]
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad(c,\forall{\rho}.\ {s})&=&\mathsf{synth}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ e)\\
\quad\quad{\kappa}\!:\!{{\overline{b},b}\rightarrow{\texttt{bool}}}&=&\rho\\
\quad\quad\overline{x}&=&\mbox{fresh variables of sort}\ $$\overline{b}$$\\
\quad\quad{b}{{{\{{{x}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}&=&\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma};\overline{{x}\!:\!{b}},\ {b}{{\{{\star}\}}})\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 8.8:
Algorithmic Checking for
\mathsf{\lambda_{\rho}}
, extends cases of
fig.

7.12

8.5
Discussion

In
\mathsf{\lambda_{\rho}}
we saw how we often want to specify signatures
that abstract over refinements, how these signatures can be
checked using uninterpreted functions, and how we can extend
the Horn-constraint based inference method from §
5

to automatically instantiate abstract refinements at usage sites.
The method was introduced by
[
Vazou13
]
who illustrated
a variety of applications in establishing invariants of
data structures.

[
Gordon17
]
demonstrated that abstract refinements could
be used to encode a form of concurrent rely-guarantee reasoning,
enabling the verification of implementations of lock-free data structures.
Subsequent work by
[
polikarpova16
]
showed how refinement
polymorphism allows writing compact specifications from which
implementations can be automatically
synthesized
.

Bounded Quantification

The abstract refinements in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\rho}}
were completely unconstrained.
However, we could imagine a form of
bounded quantification

for refinement variables analogous to type variables
[
fbounded
]
,
which would restrict instantiating refinements to those satisfying
particular conditions.
Such an extension was explored by
[
Vazou15
]
who showed how
the programmer can use Horn constraints to express bounds which
allow specifying expressive signatures whilst preserving decidable
and automatic verification.
These extensions enable, for example, encoding a monadic
information flow control (IFC) mechanism, purely within
refinement types
[
liftyICFP20
]
enabling
the construction of web applications adhering to expressive
data-sensitive security policies
[
binah
]
.

Chapter 9
Termination

The problem of verifying that the execution of a certain
piece of code always terminates is perhaps one of the
oldest in computing, going back all the way to
[
Turing36
]
’s
work on the undecidability of the Halting Problem.
Of course, just because a problem is undecidable,
doesn’t mean that it goes away!
Indeed, as
[
rice53
]
pointed out
all
non-trivial
semantic properties of programs are undecidable. That is,
it is just as undecidable to guarantee that,
e.g.
a program
will not attempt to add
int
and
bool
values.
Yet, we have developed syntactic disciplines that have turned
verifying the absence of errors like adding
int
and
bool
values into into a routine part of compiling code.
Next, lets see how refinements allow us to develop a simple
and practical discipline for verifying, at compile time,
that functions always terminate.

9.1
Examples

Lets see some examples that illustrate
how to verify termination.

Well-founded Metrics

Consider the function
sum
which adds up the numbers from
0
to
n
:

⬇

val

sum

:

n
:
nat

=>

nat

let

rec

sum

=

(
n
)

=>

{

if

(
n

<

1)

{

0

}

else

{

n

+

sum
(
n
-1)

}

}

Why
does

sum

n
terminate? First, notice that

sum
will not terminate if invoked on a negative number,

e.g.

sum
(-3)
will diverge. We eliminate this possibility
by requiring the precondition that
n
:
nat

i.e.
the inputs
be non-negative.
Now, when
n
equals
zero
, the procedure simply returns
the result
0
. Otherwise, it recurses on a
strictly

smaller input, until, ultimately, it reaches
0
, at which
point it terminates.

Proving Termination by Induction

Thus we can, somewhat more formally, prove termination

by induction
on
n
.

•

Base case

sum
terminates for inputs
k=

0
.

•

Induction Hypothesis

Assume
sum
terminates on all
k\leq

n
.

•

Inductive Step

Check that
sum
(
n
+1)
only recursively invokes
sum
(
n
)

which satisfies the induction hypothesis and hence terminates.

This reasoning suffices to convince ourselves that
sum
(
n
)

terminates for every non-negative
n
. That is, we have shown
that
sum
terminates because a
well-founded
metric:
here the non-negative
n
is
strictly decreasing

at each recursive call.

Proving Termination with Types

We can capture the above reasoning via the type system as follows.
First we require that
sum
only be called with non-negative
nat

values, which were defined as

⬇

type

nat

=

int
[
v
|0

<=

v
]

Second, we to ensure that the recursion is on
strictly smaller

values, we need only typecheck the
implementation
of
sum
in
an environment that requires
sum
only be called with inputs smaller
than
n
,
i.e.
we check the body in a environment of the form

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{\texttt{sum}}\ \doteq\ {n}\!:\!{\texttt{{nat}}},\ {\texttt{sum}}\!:\!{{{n^{\prime}}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{0\leq n^{\prime}<n}\}}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{{nat}}}}

The above ensures that any (recursive) call in the body
only calls
sum
with inputs smaller than the “current”
parameter
n
. Notice that if we had not required
n
to be
non-negative, then the parameter
n
-1
passed in at the
recursive call would be smaller than
n
but would not
be non-negative, and hence, would fail the strengthened
precondition for
sum
, as indeed it should, as such a
computation does not terminate!

Recursion on Multiple Parameters

The above method works even when there are multiple
parameters, as long as there is
some

nat
-valued
parameter that is used to limit the recursion.
Consider the following tail-recursive variant of
sum

⬇

let

rec

sumT

=

(
total
,

n
)

=>

{

if

(
n

==

0)

{

total

}

else

{

sumT
(
total

+

n
,

n

-

1)

}

}

The function
sumT
(
total
,

n
)
takes two parameters:

n
as before, and
total
which holds the accumulated
summation of the “previously seen” seen.
That is,
sumT
(0,3)
evaluates as follows:

⬇

sumT
(0,3)

\dashrightarrow

sumT
(3,2)

\dashrightarrow

sumT
(5,1)

\dashrightarrow

sumT
(6,0)

\dashrightarrow

6

Specifying Termination Metrics

The accumulation parameter
total
is not strictly decreasing.
However, the parameter
n

is
decreasing and non-negative
and serves to witness that
sumT
always terminates.
But how might the type-checker

guess
that it should use
n
instead of
total
? While one can imagine
a variety of pragmatic and effective heuristics to make such guesses, for our
purposes, we shall simply give the type checker an explicit
termination metric

⬇

val

sumT
:

total
:
nat

=>

n
:
nat

=>

nat

/

n

In the above, we end the type signature for
sumT
with
/

n
to denote
that the value
n
should be used as the termination metric.
The typechecker will verify that the value of the metric

n
is indeed well-founded:
i.e.
non-negative and strictly
decreasing at each recursive call, and if so, will deem
the function terminating.

Metric Expressions

Metrics generalize to situations where no single parameter
is decreasing, but some
expression
over the parameters
is. For example, consider the function
range
(
i
,

j
)
which returns
the list of
int
egers between
i
and
j

⬇

val

range

:

i
:
int

=>

j
:
int

=>

list
(
int
)

/

j

-

i

let

rec

range

=

(
i
,

j
)

=>

{

if

(
i

<

j
)

{

Cons
(
i
,

range
(
i
+1,

j
))

}

else

{

Nil

}

}

In the above, neither argument is decreasing:
i

increases

at each call, and
j
is unchanged. Nevertheless, the function terminates
as the
gap
between
i
and
j
diminishes at each recursive call,
and the function terminates when that gap reaches
0
.
We can make this intuition precise via the termination metric
/

j
-
i
.
Armed with this information, the type checker ensures
that at each recursive call in the body, the value of

j
-
i
is decreasing and non-negative.
That is, we will check the implementation of
range

in an environment

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{\texttt{range}}\ \doteq\ {i,j}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}},\ {\texttt{range}}\!:\!{{{i^{\prime}}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{{j^{\prime}}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{0\leq j^{\prime}-i^{\prime}<j-i}\}}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{{int}}}}}

(9.1)

that stipulates that recursive calls to
range
must have
strictly smaller, non-negative gaps, to verify that
range

indeed always terminates.

Lexicographic Metrics

Sometimes, it is convenient to split up the termination
metric across
multiple
smaller metrics.
For example consider Ackermann’s function

⬇

val

ack

:

m
:
nat

=>

n
:
nat

=>

nat

/

m
,

n

let

rec

ack

=

(
m
,

n
)

=>

{

if

(
m

==

0)

{

n

+

1

}

else

{

if

(
n

==

0)

{

ack

(
m

-

1,

1)

}

else

{

ack

(
m

-

1,

ack

(
m
,

n

-

1))

}

}

}

Why does
ack
terminate? At each iteration either the

first
parameter
m
decreases, or
m
remains the same
and the
second
parameter
n
decreases.
Each time that
n
reaches
0
, it cannot decrease further
so
m
must decrease. Hence,
m
will eventually reach
0

and
ack
will terminate.
In other words, the
pair

(
m
,

n
)
decreases in the

lexicographic order
on pairs, which is a well-ordering
that has no infinite descending chains
[
NipkowRewriting
]
.

Specifying Lexicographic Orders via Types

We can extend our notion of metrics to account for lexicographic
orders by allowing the user to write a
sequence
of metrics.
For example, we type
ack
with the signature with the termination
metric
/

m
,

n
and then we will use the sequence to check the
implementation of
ack
in environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}_{\texttt{ack}}

{m,n}\!:\!{\texttt{{nat}}},\ {\texttt{ack}}\!:\!{{{m^{\prime}}\!:\!{\texttt{{nat}}}}\rightarrow{{{n^{\prime}}\!:\!{{\texttt{{nat}}}{{\{{m^{\prime}<m\vee(m^{\prime}=m\wedge n^{\prime}<n)}\}}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{{nat}}}}}

The signature for
ack
limits recursive uses of
ack
to parameters
that satisfy the lexicographic ordering to ensure that
ack
terminates.

Structural Recursion

Often the recursion is over the elements of a datatype
like a list or a tree. For example, consider the
function that appends two lists
xs
and
ys

⬇

val

append

:

xs
:
list
(’
a
)

=>

ys
:
list
(’
a
)

=>

list
(’
a
)

/

len
(
xs
)

let

rec

append

=

(
xs
,

ys
)

=>

{

switch

(
xs
)

{

|

Nil

=>

ys

|

Cons

(
x
,

xs
’)

=>

Cons

(
x
,

append

(
xs
’,

ys
))

}

}

The function
append
recurses on the
tail
of the first list
xs

and stops when that list is empty
i.e.
equal to
Nil
.
This is a form of
structural recursion
where each recursive call
is over sub-structures (
e.g.
the tail) of some input parameter.
We can verify the termination of structurally recursive functions by
using
measures
to specify suitable metrics.
For
append
we specify the metric
/

len
(
xs
)
which tells the type
checker to limit recursive calls in the implementation of
append

to lists whose length is smaller than
xs
. That is, we
check the implementation of
append
in an environment where

{xs,ys}\!:\!{\texttt{list}{(\alpha)}}
, and
append
is limited to

{{xs^{\prime}}\!:\!{{\texttt{list}{(\alpha)}}{{\{{\texttt{len}(xs^{\prime})<\texttt{len}(xs)}\}}}}}\rightarrow{{{ys^{\prime}}\!:\!{\texttt{list}{(\alpha)}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{list}{(\alpha)}}}

Non-Structural Recursion

Finally, the notion of metrics over measures scales up to account for
more general scenarios where the recursion over the datatypes is not
structural. For example, consider the function
braid
which takes
two lists
x_{1},\ldots
and
y_{1},\ldots
and returns the list
x_{1},y_{1},\ldots

⬇

val

braid

:

xs
:
list
(’
a
)

=>

ys
:
list
(’
a
)

=>

list
(’
a
)

/

len
(
xs
)

+

len
(
ys
)

let

rec

braid

=

(
xs
,

ys
)

=>

{

switch

(
xs
)

{

|

Nil

=>

ys

|

Cons

(
x
,

xs
’)

=>

Cons
(
x
,

braid
(
ys
,

xs
’))

}

}

The recursion in
braid
is not structural: the recursive call flips the order
of the lists to ensure the values alternate in the output.
However, in this case, the
sum
of the lengths of the two input lists
shrinks. We specify this via the metric
/

len
(
xs
)

+

len
(
ys
)
which tells
the type checker to check the body of
braid
under the following environment

{xs,\ ys}\!:\!{\texttt{list}{(\alpha)}},\ {\texttt{braid}}\!:\!{{{xs^{\prime}}\!:\!{\texttt{list}{(\alpha)}}}\rightarrow{{{ys^{\prime}}\!:\!{{\texttt{list}{(\alpha)}}{{\{{p}\}}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{list}{(\alpha)}}}}

where the refinement

p\ \doteq\ 0\leq\texttt{len}(xs^{\prime})+\texttt{len}(ys^{\prime})<\texttt{len}(xs)+\texttt{len}(ys)

limits recursive calls to parameters the sum of whose lengths
are decreasing, to verify that
braid
terminates.

9.2
Types and Terms

Metric

m

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

p

decreasing expression

\ \mid\

p
,
m

lexicographic metric

Terms

e

\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=

\ldots

from Fig.
8.1

\ \mid\

\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{t/m}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}

recursive binder

Figure 9.1:
\mathsf{\lambda_{\tau}}
: Syntax of Types and Terms

As demonstrated by the examples,
\mathsf{\lambda_{\tau}}
requires two small extensions
to its syntax: a way to specify termination metrics, and a means of specifying
metrics in type signatures, as summarized in
fig.

9.1
.

Metrics

A
termination metric
(or just metric in brief)
is either a single
decreasing expression

p

which is an
int
-sorted term from the refinement logic
(
fig.

2.1
), or a
lexicographic metric

comprising a sequence of decreasing expressions.

Recursive Signatures

In
\mathsf{\lambda_{\tau}}
we require that recursive
rec

binders be annotated with signatures that also specify
a termination metric
m
.
For example, we would type
ack
as

\texttt{{rec}}\;\texttt{ack}={\lambda{m,n}.\ {e_{\texttt{ack}}}}\!:\!{t_{\texttt{ack}}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;\ldots

where the signature
t_{\texttt{ack}}
specifies
the lexicographic metric with the sequence
of decreasing expressions
m,n

t_{\texttt{ack}}\ \doteq\ {{m}\!:\!{\texttt{{nat}}}}\rightarrow{{{n}\!:\!{\texttt{{nat}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{{nat}}}}\penalty\ /\penalty\ m,n

(9.2)

9.3
Declarative Typing

\textrm{{Metric Well-formedness}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash m$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash p:\texttt{{int}}}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash p}{{{\textsc{Wfm-Base}}}}\par\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash p\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash m}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash p,m}{{{\textsc{Wfm-Lex}}}}

Figure 9.2:
Rules for Checking Metric Well-formedness

As we saw with the examples in
section

9.1
termination
checking reduces quite directly to plain refinement checking after

limiting
recursive applications within the implementation to
types that are strengthened with special refinements that ensure
that the recursion is
well-founded
.

Metric Well-formedness

The judgment
\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash m
says that
a termination metric
m
is
well-formed

in an environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}
.
The judgment is established by the rules
Wfm-Base

and
Wfm-Lex
which, in concert, check that each decreasing
expression in the metric can be typed as an
int
-valued term under
\mathrm{\Gamma}
.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{wfr}&:&(M\times M)\rightarrow P\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{wfr}(p^{*},p^{\prime})&\doteq&0\leq p^{\prime}\wedge p^{\prime}<p^{*}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{wfr}(p^{*};m^{*},p^{\prime};m^{\prime})&\doteq&0\leq p^{\prime}\wedge(p^{\prime}<p^{*}\vee r)\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad r&=&p^{\prime}=p^{*}\wedge\mathsf{wfr}(m^{*},m^{\prime})\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 9.3:
Computing Well-foundedness Refinements

Well-foundedness Refinements

The procedure
\mathsf{wfr}(m^{*},m^{\prime})
shown in
fig.

9.3

takes as input two termination metrics, and returns as output a predicate
that guarantees the metric demonstrates well-founded (terminating) recursion.
The inputs
m^{*}
and
m^{\prime}
respectively
denote the values of a function’s termination metric over the

original
and
recursive
call parameters.
The output is a
well-foundedness refinement
corresponding
to the precondition that must hold at each recursive call in order
for the metric to demonstrate the function terminating.

For
range
, we would use the termination metric
/\penalty\ j-i

to compute the well-foundedness refinement

\mathsf{wfr}(j^{\prime}-i^{\prime},j-i)\doteq 0\leq j^{\prime}-i^{\prime}<j-i

(9.3)

Similarly, for
ack
, we would use the termination metric
/\penalty\ m,n

to compute a well-foundedness refinement

\mathsf{wfr}((m^{\prime},n^{\prime}),(m,n))\doteq 0\leq m^{\prime}\wedge(m^{\prime}<m\vee(m^{\prime}=m\wedge 0\leq n^{\prime}<n))

(9.4)

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{lim}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times M\times T)\rightarrow T\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{lim}(\mathrm{\Gamma},m,t)&\doteq&\mathsf{lim^{*}}(\mathrm{\Gamma},m,m,t)\\[7.22743pt]
\hline\cr\mathsf{lim^{*}}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times M\times M\times T)\rightarrow T\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{lim^{*}}(\mathrm{\Gamma},m^{*},m,{{x}\!:\!{{b}{{\{{p}\}}}}}\rightarrow{t})&&\\
\quad\mid\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{b}}\vdash m&\doteq&{{x^{\prime}}\!:\!{{b}{{\{{p^{\prime}}\}}}}}\rightarrow{t^{\prime}}\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad p^{\prime}&=&{p}[x:=x^{\prime}]\wedge\mathsf{wfr}(m^{*},m^{\prime})\\
\quad\quad m^{\prime}&=&{m}[x:=x^{\prime}]\\
\quad\quad t^{\prime}&=&{t}[x:=x^{\prime}]\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{lim^{*}}(\mathrm{\Gamma},m^{*},m,{{x}\!:\!{s}}\rightarrow{t})&\doteq&{{x^{\prime}}\!:\!{s^{\prime}}}\rightarrow{t^{\prime\prime}}\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad t^{\prime\prime}&=&\mathsf{lim^{*}}(\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{s}},m^{*},m^{\prime},t^{\prime})\\
\quad\quad m^{\prime}&=&{m}[x:=x^{\prime}]\\
\quad\quad s^{\prime}&=&{s}[x:=x^{\prime}]\\
\quad\quad t^{\prime}&=&{t}[x:=x^{\prime}]\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{lim^{*}}(\mathrm{\Gamma},m^{*},m,\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{t})&\doteq&\forall{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}}.{\mathsf{lim^{*}}(\mathrm{\Gamma},m^{*},m,t)}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 9.4:
Checking termination by type limiting

Type Limiting

The procedure
\mathsf{lim}(\mathrm{\Gamma},m,t)
shown in
fig.

9.4

computes a type
t^{\prime}
which strengthens the input types (preconditions)
to require that all (recursive) calls be
limited
to values that are
allowed by the metric
m
.
The real work is done by the helper
\mathsf{lim^{*}}(\mathrm{\Gamma},m^{*},m,t)

which takes the
original
metric
m^{*}
and the
recursive

metric
m
where all the input binders (
x
) are replaced with “primed”
versions (
x^{\prime}
) and returns a version of
t
where
(1) the inputs are renamed with their primed variants and
(2) the well-foundedness refinement is used to strengthen
the first input refinement where it is well-formed,

i.e.
where all the binders appearing in the refinement
are in scope.
To this end, the procedure recurses over the structure of the
(function) type
t
, adding the binders to
\mathrm{\Gamma}
and
renaming the inputs
m
with their primed versions,

until
it has added enough binders to
\mathrm{\Gamma}
for
m^{*}

to be well-formed, at which point, it strengthens the current
parameter’s refinement
p
with the well-foundedness
refinement
\mathsf{wfr}(m^{*},m^{\prime})
.

For simplicity, the definition of
\mathsf{lim^{*}}
and the rule

Chk-Term
do not support refinement polymorphism.
It is straightforward to remove this restriction by extending
the definitions to allow for types abstracted over refinements.

Example: Type of
range

Recall that
range
has type

t_{\texttt{range}}\doteq{{i}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{{j}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}}}\penalty\ /\penalty\ j-i

(9.5)

We will check its body with the metric limited type

\displaystyle\ \mathsf{lim}(\emptyset,\ j-i,\ {{i}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{{j}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}}})

which is defined as

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathsf{lim^{*}}(\emptyset,\ j-i,\ j-i,\ {{i}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{{j}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}}})

as the metric
j-i
is not well-formed, we rename
i
to
i^{\prime}
and recurse

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ {{i^{\prime}}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{lim^{*}}({i}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}},\ j-i,\ j-i^{\prime},\ {{j}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}})}

now, since
{i}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}},{j}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}\vdash j-i
the above is

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ {{i^{\prime}}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{{j^{\prime}}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{\mathsf{wfr}(j-i,j^{\prime}-i^{\prime})}\}}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}}}

which, after substituting the well-foundedness refinement (
eq.

9.3
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ {{i^{\prime}}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{{j^{\prime}}\!:\!{{\texttt{{int}}}{{\{{0\leq j^{\prime}-i^{\prime}<j-i}\}}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}}}

\square

\textrm{{Termination Checking}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash x=e:s/m\triangleright t$}

\frac{t=\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ s)=\forall{{\overline{\alpha}}\!:\!{k}}.{{\overline{{y}\!:\!{s}}}\rightarrow{t^{*}}}\quad e=\char 12\relax{\overline{\alpha}}{\lambda{\overline{y}}.\ {e^{*}}}\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};{\overline{{\alpha}\!:\!{k}};\overline{{y}\!:\!{s}};{x}\!:\!{\mathsf{lim}(\mathrm{\Gamma},m,t)}}\vdash e^{*}\Leftarrow t^{*}}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash x=e:s/m\triangleright t}{{{\textsc{Chk-Term}}}}

\textrm{{Type Checking}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t$}

\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash x=e_{1}:s_{1}/m\triangleright t_{1}\and\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{t_{1}}}\vdash e_{2}\Leftarrow t_{2}}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}/m}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}\Leftarrow t_{2}}{{{\textsc{Chk-Rec}}}}

Figure 9.5:
Bidirectional Checking: Other rules from
\mathsf{\lambda_{\rho}}
(Fig.
8.4
)

Termination Checking

We introduce a new
termination checking

judgment
\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash x=e:s/m\triangleright t

which
guarantees
that in an environment
\mathrm{\Gamma}
,
the (recursive) definition
x=e
is terminating
with the metric
m
, and that downstream definitions
can
assume
that
x
behaves as
t
.
The rule
Chk-Term
shown in
fig.

9.5

establishes this judgment by

1.

Instantiating
the
holes
in
s
to obtain
the complete signature
t
,

2.

Splitting
the definition
e
into its
input
binders

\overline{{y}\!:\!{s}}

and
body

e^{*}
,

3.

Checking
the body
e^{*}
in an environment containing
the input binders
\overline{{y}\!:\!{s}}
which
name the
current
input,
and
where
x

is bound to its
metric limited type

\mathsf{lim}(\mathrm{\Gamma},m,t)
.

That is, the key change is to check the body of the recursive
binder in an environment that limits the recursion using the
specified metric.

Checking Recursive Definitions

We can now use the termination checking judgment
in the updated rule
Chk-Rec
shown in

fig.

9.5
.
To check that
\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}/m}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}

has type
t_{2}
, the new rule requires that the binder

x
terminates with
m
, and then, (as before),
checks
e_{2}
against
t_{2}
in the environment
extended with
x
.

Example: Checking
range

Lets see how
Chk-Term
checks the term

\displaystyle e_{\texttt{r}}

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ \texttt{{rec}}\;\texttt{range}={(\lambda{i,j}.\ {e_{\texttt{range}}})}\!:\!{\ t_{\texttt{range}}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;\ldots

(9.6)

where

\displaystyle e_{\texttt{range}}

\displaystyle\ \doteq\ \texttt{{if}}\;i<j\;\texttt{{then}}\;{\texttt{Cons}[\texttt{{int}}]\ i\ (\texttt{range}\ (i+i)\ j)}\;\texttt{{else}}\;\texttt{Nil}[\texttt{{int}}]

\displaystyle t_{\texttt{range}}

\displaystyle\ \doteq{{i}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{{j}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}}}\penalty\ /\penalty\ j-i

Chk-Term
stipulates that we use the metric
to limit the type and check the body
e_{\texttt{range}}

against the specified output
\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}
in environment

\mathrm{\Gamma}_{\texttt{range}}
from
eq.

9.1

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{\texttt{range}}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ e_{\texttt{range}}\Leftarrow\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}

Rule
Chk-If
reduces the above to checks on each branch. Eliding the trivial
else
case, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{\texttt{range}};i<j

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ {\texttt{Cons}[\texttt{{int}}]\ i\ (\texttt{range}\ (i+i)\ j)}\Leftarrow\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}

which
Syn-App
splits into a check that

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{\texttt{range}};i<j

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ \texttt{range}\ (i+i)\ j\Leftarrow\texttt{list}{(\texttt{{int}})}

Again,
Syn-App
splits the above into checks that ensure each
input
to
range
satisfies the
environment’s (limited) input type

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{\texttt{range}};i<j

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{i^{\prime}}\!:\!{i^{\prime}=i+1}}\}}}}\ \prec:\ \texttt{{int}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Gamma}_{\texttt{range}};i<j;{i^{\prime}}\!:\!{{\{{i^{\prime}=i+1}\}}}

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{j^{\prime}}\!:\!{j^{\prime}=j}}\}}}}\ \prec:\ {\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{j^{\prime}}\!:\!{0\leq j^{\prime}-i^{\prime}<j-i}}\}}}}

the first of those is trivial; the second reduces to the entailment

\displaystyle i<j;i^{\prime}=i+1;j^{\prime}=j

\displaystyle\ \vdash\ 0\leq j^{\prime}-i^{\prime}<j-i

that is readily verified by the SMT solver.

\square

9.4
Verification Conditions

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{checkT}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times x\times E\times T\times M)\rightarrow(T\times C)\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{checkT}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ x,\ e,\ s,\ m)&\doteq&(t,\ ({\overline{{y::s}}})\Rightarrow({{x::t^{\prime}}})\Rightarrow c)\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad c&=&\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma};{\overline{\alpha};\overline{{y}\!:\!{s}};{x}\!:\!{t^{\prime}}},\ e^{*},\ t^{*})\\
\quad\quad t^{\prime}&=&\mathsf{lim}(\mathrm{\Gamma},m,t)\\
\quad\quad\forall{{\overline{\alpha}}\!:\!{k}}.{{\overline{{y}\!:\!{s}}}\rightarrow{t^{*}}}\ \mbox{as}\ t&=&\mathsf{fresh}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ s)\\
\quad\quad\char 12\relax{\overline{\alpha}}{\lambda{\overline{y}}.\ {e^{*}}}&=&e\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 9.6:
Algorithmic Termination Checking

The algorithmic VC generation procedure for
\mathsf{\lambda_{\tau}}

extends how
\mathsf{check}
generates Horn constraints for

\texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}
,
as summarized in
fig.

9.7
.
The implementation mirrors the declarative formulation

Chk-Rec
, where we first check that the recursive
definition
e_{1}
is terminating, and then use its type
t_{1}

to check
e_{2}
.

Algorithmic Termination Checking

We implement the termination checking judgment with a procedure

\mathsf{checkT}
summarized in
fig.

9.6
, which takes
as input a recursive definition
x=e
and the type

s
and metric
m
ascribed to the definition and
returns as output a pair comprising the Horn VC
c

whose satisfiability indicates that the definition is
well-typed and terminating, and the type
t
that
subsequent binders can assume for
x
.
First, (as in
fig.

5.5
) we use
\mathsf{fresh}

to instantiate the holes in the specification
s

with new Horn variables.
Second, we obtain the body
e^{*}
and input binders

\overline{{y}\!:\!{s}}
.
Finally, we invoke
\mathsf{check}
to compute the VC
c

for the body in an environment containing the input
binders and the metric-limited type
t^{\prime}
.
The following states the correspondence between the algorithmic
and declarative versions of termination checking:

Proposition 9.4.1
.

If
\mathsf{checkT}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ x,\ e,\ s,\ m)=(t,c)

and
c
is Horn satisfiable then
\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash x=e:s/m\triangleright t
.

Following the same reasoning as the declarative checking,

i.e.
generating a VC for the body in the metric-limited environment,

\mathsf{check}(\emptyset,\ e_{\texttt{r}},\ \ldots)
generates the
following VC for the term from
eq.

9.6
:

\forall{i,j,i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}.\ i<j\Rightarrow i^{\prime}=i+1\Rightarrow j^{\prime}=j\Rightarrow 0\leq j^{\prime}-i^{\prime}<j-i

The above VC is valid, which proves that
range
terminates.

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{check}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E\times T)\rightarrow C\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ \texttt{{rec}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}/m}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2},\ t_{2})&\doteq&c_{1}\wedge({{x::t_{1}}})\Rightarrow c_{2}\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad(t_{1},c_{1})&=&\mathsf{checkT}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ x,\ e_{1},\ s_{1},\ m)\\
\quad\quad c_{2}&=&\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{t_{1}}},\ e_{2},\ t_{2})\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 9.7:
Algorithmic Checking for
\mathsf{\lambda_{\tau}}
, extends cases of
fig.

8.8

9.5
Discussion

To recap, in
\mathsf{\lambda_{\tau}}
we saw how to extend the type system
to also check that (recursive) functions terminate.
We started with functions like
sum
and
sumT
that recurse
on some natural number
n
that directly demonstrates termination.
Then, we saw how to generalize the above idea to decreasing expressions
like the one that we used to demonstrate
range
terminates.
We saw how the idea of expressions can be generalized to sequences
to yield termination metrics which demonstrate termination via
well-founded lexicographic ordering.
Finally, we saw how the notion of measures generalizes the above
to functions that work on datatypes.
The key idea in all of the above, is simply to check the body of
the recursive call with a strengthened type that limits (recursive)
inputs to be well-founded, thereby enforcing termination.

Incompleteness

Of course, thanks to the Halting problem there are terminating functions
that cannot be proven terminating via the approach shown above, because
there is no algorithmic procedure to find termination metrics, and because
the metrics themselves may, in general, fall outside the SMT solver’s
decidable theories.
For example, it would be a major result to find a suitable terminating
metric for the
collatz
function
[
collatzWiki
]
.

Termination in Practice

Nevertheless, there is evidence that the mechanisms shown here
with simple extensions,
e.g.
to account for mutually recursive
functions, suffice to verify termination in the vast majority
of programs that arise in practice.
For example, the metric-based approach extended with simple heuristics
to generate default metrics associated with particular datatypes, suffices
to verify 96% of recursive functions on a corpus of more than 10,000 lines
of widely used Haskell libraries, whilst requiring only about 1.7 metrics per
100 lines of code
[
Vazou14
]
.
Other SMT-based verifiers like
F*

[
fstar
]
and
Dafny

[
dafny
]

use similar strategies to check termination very effectively.

Other Strategies for Proving Termination

There is a vast literature on techniques for proving termination
all of which ultimately find their roots in the notion of well-founded
metrics, introduced by
[
Turing49
]
.

[
JonesB04
]
and
[
Sereni05
]
embody this idea via the
“size-change principle” that they use to verify
termination of recursive functions, and which, can
be rephrased as a
contract
to enable
dynamic

termination checking
[
Nguyen19
]
.
Proof assistants like
Coq

[
coq-book
]

and
Isabelle

[
IsabelleManual
]
employ

structural
termination checks wherein
recursive calls can only be made on strict
sub-structures of the inputs (
e.g.
the tail of
an input list.)

[
HughesParetoSabry96
]
and
[
BartheTermination
]

show how to generalize this idea via
sized types

wherein the bodies of recursive functions are checked
under metric limited environments.
An alternative approach formulated by
[
Giesl11
]
is to
reduce termination for functional programs
to termination of term rewriting systems.

[
Rybalchenko04
]
show how to generalize and unify
the notion of termination metrics and program invariants, via
the notion of
transition invariants
, which also allow
the use of abstract interpretation based methods to automatically
synthesize suitable metrics (or “ranking functions”), which
was the basis of the
Terminator
tool
[
CookPR11
]

which verifies the termination of device drivers written in
C
.

Our formulation for
\mathsf{\lambda_{\tau}}
is inspired by the method
introduced by
[
XiTerminationLICS01
]
to encode
sized types in a refinement setting.
Refinements provide the advantage of
unifying

reasoning about invariants of data with reasoning about
termination.
This unification is crucial for large real-world code bases,
where termination requires functions only be called under
certain pre-conditions (
e.g.

int
valued inputs are non-negative),
or require specific post-conditions (
e.g.
the
split
function
in a merge-sort routine returns output lists that are strictly smaller
than the input), or let us specify arithmetic metrics like those in

range
where termination depends crucially on the
path-sensitive reasoning performed by the rest of the type checking.

Chapter 10
Programs as Proofs

We have been rather timid in what we allow
specifications to
say
, limiting them
mostly to facts about integers or sets or
ordering extended with uninterpreted measures
that describe properties of algebraic data,
to ensure that the VCs are SMT decidable.
Next, lets see how to break out of this shell,
to allow users to write specifications over
user defined functions and then prove theorems
about those functions by supplying proofs
structured as programs.
We will do so by
reflecting
the implementation
of the user-defined function into its output type,
thereby converting its type signature into a precise
description of the function’s behavior.
Reflection has a profound consequence: at
uses

of the function, the standard rule
Syn-App
for function
application turns into a means of explicating how the function
behaves at the given input, which lets us encode the function’s
behavior at the (refinement) type level.
The above idea, coupled with a small set of combinators,
lets us write sophisticated proofs simply as programs.

10.1
Examples

Lets start with an overview of how refinement reflection works
by seeing how it lets us write paper-and-pencil-style proofs
as programs.

10.1.1
Propositions as Types

Refinements let us encode propositions as types.
For example,
a unit type can be refined with a logical proposition
that encodes that
1

+

1

=

2

⬇

type

one_plus_one_eq_two

=

()[
v
|1

+

1

=

2]

As the
v
and
()
are unimportant, we will elide them and just write

⬇

type

one_plus_one_eq_two

=

[1

+

1

=

2]

As another example, here is the proposition
that
int
addition is commutative,
i.e.

\forall{x,y}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}.x+y=y+x
:

⬇

type

plus_comm

=

x
:
int

=>

y
:
int

=>

[
x

+

y

=

y

+

x
]

Programs as Proofs

Notice that we can represent universal quantification as a function type,
following the Curry-Howard correspondence
[
CHIso
,
Wadler15
]
.
Thus, following the correspondence, any term
e
whose type corresponds
to a proposition
P
can be viewed as a
proof
of that proposition.
Here is a trivial “proof” of the proposition
one_plus_one_eq_two

⬇

val

thm_one_plus_one_eq_two

:

one_plus_one_eq_two

let

thm_one_plus_one_eq_two

=

()

Note that the VC generation procedure we outlined in
chapter

3

would verify the above program by checking the validity of the VC

1+1=2

which is validated by the SMT solver. Here is a proof for
plus_comm

⬇

val

thm_plus_comm

:

plus_comm

let

thm_plus_comm

=

(
x
,

y
)

=>

()

The VC generation procedure from
chapter

3
generates the VC

\forall x,y.\ x+y=y+x

which the SMT solver validates via the theory of linear
arithmetic, giving us our “theorem”.
These two propositions fell squarely within decidable theories
and hence had trivial proofs, with the SMT solver doing all
the work.

10.1.2
Refinement Reflection

Next, lets extend the language of refinements
with user-defined functions, and write propositions
and proofs over those functions.

Step 1: Propositions over User-defined Functions

First,
\mathsf{\lambda_{\pi}}
introduces a way to define functions,

e.g.
to
sum
numbers from
0
to
n

⬇

val

sum

:

n
:
nat

=>

nat

/

n

def

sum

=

(
n
)

=>

{

if

(
n

==

0)

{

0

}

else

{

n

+

sum
(
n
-1)

}

}

The
def
-bound functions are just like the usual
rec
binders
— the metric
/

n
ensures they terminate — except
that we can refer to them in refinements as the

uninterpreted
function
{sum}
.
Consequently, we can now specify the proposition

\forall i,j.\ i=j\Rightarrow{sum}(i)={sum}(j)

(10.1)

and verify it via a trivial proof

⬇

val

sum_eq

:

i
:
nat

=>

j
:
nat
[
i
=
j
]

=>

[
sum
(
i
)

=

sum
(
j
)]

let

sum_eq

=

(
i
,

j
)

=>

()

The above program generates the VC corresponding directly
to the proposition
eq.

10.1
which the SMT solver
automatically validates via congruence closure
[
Nelson81
]
.

Step 2: Refinement Reflection

\mathsf{\lambda_{\pi}}
imbues
def
binders with a second
crucial property: we strengthen their user-specified
types with a refinement that exactly

reflects the function’s implementation
.

\par Writing proofs like \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 2\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 3}}}}}} can be
tedious: how are we to divine \emph{which}

\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Equational Proofs}}\
We can solve this problem by structuring
proofs to follow the style of calculational
or equational reasoning \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Bird89, Dijkstra76}{}{}]}
and implemented in {Agda}\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{agdaequational}{}{}]},
and {Dafny} \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{LeinoPolikarpova16}{}{}]},
via an \emph{equality-chaining} combinator
{\@@listings@block{78}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}(===){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}:\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{y}}}:\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{y}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}]{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{v}}}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\&\&{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{v}}}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{y}}}]\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}(===){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{y}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{y}}}\@lst@endline}}{}}}The combinator's type specification says that
$e_{1}\ \texttt{===}\ e_{2}$ is
a \emph{proof} that $e_{1}$ and $e_{2}$
are equal, and further, a term that equals
$e_{1}$ and $e_{2}$.
We can use equality-chaining to
rewrite \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 2\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 3}}}}}} in a way that
might mirror a pencil-and-paper proof
{\@@listings@block{79}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 2\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 3}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{\textunderscore}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}-\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}3{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}6]{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{@}}}-\textbraceright\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 2\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 3}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}(){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}(2)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}+{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}(1)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}+{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}1{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}+{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}(0)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}3\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}The precondition of \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small(===)}}} checks that each
\emph{intermediate} equality holds, simply by
using the \emph{applied} instance of \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}}}} at
the respective call.
The postcondition of \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small(===)}}} lets us chain the
equalities together to prove the goal.
Thus, if we skip a step, {e.g.}{} if we write
{\@@listings@block{80}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 2\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 3}}}\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}(){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}(2)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}+{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}1{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}+{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}(0)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}3\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}the precondition for the first equality-chain
will fail, and so type checking pinpoints where
information is needed to complete the proof.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Functions as Lemmas}}\
Suppose we want to verify the proposition \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}(3){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}6}}}.
We could, of course, repeat all the calculations we did
to prove \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 2\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 3}}}}}} but instead, it would be nice to
\emph{reuse} the proposition that we have already proved
as a \emph{lemma} to prove the new goal.
We do so by introducing a \emph{because} combinator that
conjoins propositions
{\@@listings@block{81}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}(?){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}:\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{p}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}]{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{y}}}:\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{b}}}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{q}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{y}}}]{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{v}}}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{p}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{v}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\&\&{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{q}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{y}}}]\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}(?){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{\textunderscore}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}\@lst@endline}}{}}}We can now reuse \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 2\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 3}}}}}} as a lemma, simply by
applying it as
{\@@listings@block{82}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 3\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 6}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{\textunderscore}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}(3){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}6]\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 3\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 6}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}(){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}(3)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}3{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}+{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}(2)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}?{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 2\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 3}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}()\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}6\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}The types of our combinators ensure that the above yields a VC like
$${sum}(3)=\defn{sum}(3) \Rightarrow${sum}$ (2) = 3 \Rightarrow${sum}$ (3) = 6$$wherethefactestablishingthevalueof{sum}(2)isestablishedbyapplying,andhence,obtainingtheoutput(post-condition)ofthefunction\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 2\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 3}}}}}}.\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Proofs as Programs}
\par The above proofs are quite unremarkable: they merely
confirm what a computation evaluates to.
However, they introduce the building blocks of more
interesting examples that illustrate the
correspondence between proofs and programs summarized
in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:proofs_as_programs}.
\par\begin{figure}\begin{center}\begin{tabular}[]{*{2}{c}}\hline\cr\hline\cr{Proof}&{Program}\\
\hline\cr Theorem&Function\\
Apply Theorem&Call Function\\
Case Split&Branch\\
Induction&Recursion\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{tabular}\end{center}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{10.1}}{Correspondence between Proofs and Programs.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 10.1}}{Correspondence between Proofs and Programs.}}}
\end{figure}\par\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Induction on Numbers}}\
Lets write and prove the proposition $$\forall n.\ \sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}i=\frac{n\times(n+1)}{2}$$We can specify the proposition as a type and then
provide a proof as:
{\@@listings@block{83}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{thm\textunderscore sum}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{nat}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}[2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}+1)]\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{thm\textunderscore sum}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{switch}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}0{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}(0)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}0{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}(0+1)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}+{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}-1))\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}+{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}-1)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}?{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{thm\textunderscore sum}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}-1)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}+1)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}The above proof mirrors the classic proof \emph{by induction}.
We split cases on \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}}}} via a \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{switch}}}}}} that branches on the value of \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}}}}.
In the \emph{base} case we prove the equality via a calculation on \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}(0)}}}.
In the \emph{inductive} case we prove the equality by \emph{recursively}
applying \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{thm\textunderscore sum}}}}}} at \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}-1)}}} which effectively allows us to use the
\emph{induction hypothesis} for a smaller value of \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}}}}.
The \emph{termination} check --- made possible by the metric \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small/{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}}}} ---
crucially ensures that the recursion ({i.e.}{} induction) is well-founded,
and hence, that the proof is not circular.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Induction on Data}}\
We will see how \emph{measures} let us define \emph{selectors} that
let us reflect functions on data types like lists into the refinement
logic.
This lets us write and prove theorems over datatypes, such as
this example that involves the usual function for concatenating lists
{\@@listings@block{84}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{list}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{list}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{list}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{def}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{switch}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Nil}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}))\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}Lets verify that \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}}}} is \emph{associative}, {i.e.}{} $$\forall{xs},{ys},{zs}.\ \texttt{app}(\texttt{app}({xs},\ {ys}),\ {zs})\ =\ \texttt{app}({xs},\ \texttt{app}({ys},\ {zs}))$$by writing a recursive proof:
{\@@listings@block{85}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{
