}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{zs}}}))\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright),{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}),{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{zs}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}})),{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{zs}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}),{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{zs}}}))\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}?{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app\textunderscore assoc}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{zs}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{zs}}})))\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}==={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright),{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{zs}}}))\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}This time, the induction is on \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}}}} and is
shown well-founded due to the metric \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{len}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}})}}}.
As before, we split on the base case where \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}}}} is \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Nil}}}}}},
and the inductive case where \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}}}} is \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright)}}}.
In either case, we prove the respective goal via
a calculation. In the inductive case, we get to
invoke the induction hypothesis by \emph{calling}
the theorem \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app\textunderscore assoc}}}}}} on the smaller input \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright}}}.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Types and Terms}
\par\begin{figure}\begin{tabular}[]{rrcll}{\emph{Terms}}&$e$&$\vcentcolon\vcentcolon=$&$\ldots$&\emph{from Fig.\penalty\ \ref{fig:seven:syntax}}\\
&&$\ \mid\ $&$\texttt{{def}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{t/m}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}$&\emph{reflected binder}\\[3.61371pt]
\end{tabular}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{10.2}}{$\mathsf{\lambda_{\pi}}$: Syntax of Types and Terms}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 10.2}}{$\mathsf{\lambda_{\pi}}$: Syntax of Types and Terms}}}
\end{figure}\par\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:eight:syntax} summarizes the extensions in $\mathsf{\lambda_{\pi}}$
needed to support reflection and proofs.
The syntax of types remains unchanged.
The terms are extended with a form $\texttt{{def}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{t/m}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}$
which are just like \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{rec}}}}}}-binders except that we will strengthen their
output types using reflection.
\par\begin{figure}$$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{reflect}&:&(X\times E\times T)\rightarrow T\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{reflect}(f,e,s)&\doteq&\forall{{\overline{\alpha}}\!:\!{k}}.{{\overline{{y}\!:\!{s}}}\rightarrow{{b}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{p\wedge p^{\prime}}}\}}}}}}\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad\forall{{\overline{\alpha}}\!:\!{k}}.{{\overline{{y}\!:\!{s}}}\rightarrow{{b}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{p}}\}}}}}}&=&s\\
\quad\quad\char 12\relax{\overline{\alpha}}{\lambda{\overline{y}}.\ {e^{*}}}&=&e\\
\quad\quad p^{\prime}&=&(\nu={f}(\overline{y})\wedge\nu=\mathsf{embed}(e^{*}))\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}$$\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{10.3}}{Reflecting Terms into Types}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 10.3}}{Reflecting Terms into Types}}}
\end{figure}\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Reflection}}\
The workhorse of $\mathsf{\lambda_{\pi}}$ is the procedure $\mathsf{reflect}(f,e,s)$
shown in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:eight:reflect}, which takes as input a binder $f$,
the binder's definition $e$ and the binder's type $s$, and returns
a variant of $s$ where the \emph{output} type is strengthened with a
refinement that says that the returned value \emph{equals} the implementation
of the function at the given inputs.
The procedure works by first obtaining the parameters $\overline{y}$
and \emph{body} $e^{*}$ of the definition $e$. Next, it translates
the body $e^{*}$ into the refinement logic via the procedure $\mathsf{embed}$.
Finally, it strengthens the output type with the postcondition $p^{\prime}$ that
says that the output value $\nu$ equals the function body.
For example, $\mathsf{reflect}({add},e,s)$ where
$e\doteq\lambda{x_{1},x_{2}}.\ {x_{1}+x_{2}}$ and
$s\doteq{\texttt{{int}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}\rightarrow{\texttt{{int}}}}$
returns as output the type
$${{x_{1}}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{{x_{2}}\!:\!{\texttt{{int}}}}\rightarrow{{\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{\nu={{add}}(x_{1},x2)\wedge\nu=x_{1}+x_{2}}}\}}}}}}$$\par\par\begin{figure}$$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{embed}&:&E\rightarrow P\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\mathsf{embed}(n)&\doteq&n\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{embed}(\mathit{true})&\doteq&\mathit{true}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{embed}(\mathit{false})&\doteq&\mathit{false}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{embed}(\mathsf{c}\ x_{1}\ x_{2})&\doteq&x_{1}\bowtie_{\mathsf{c}}x_{2}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{embed}({({f}\;{e_{1}})\ldots}\;{e_{n}})&\doteq&{f}(\mathsf{embed}(e_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{embed}(e_{n}))\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{embed}(\texttt{{let}}\;x=e_{1}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2})&\doteq&{\mathsf{embed}(e_{2})}[x:=\mathsf{embed}(e_{1})]\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{embed}(\texttt{{if}}\;x\;\texttt{{then}}\;{x_{1}}\;\texttt{{else}}\;x_{2})&\doteq&\textit{if}\;{x}\;\textit{then}\;{\mathsf{embed}(e_{1})}\;\textit{else}\;{\mathsf{embed}(e_{2})}\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{embed}(\texttt{{switch}}\;x\;\{\overline{a}\})&\doteq&\mathsf{embAlts}(x,\overline{a})\\[7.22743pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}$$\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{10.4}}{Embedding Terms into the Refinement Logic}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 10.4}}{Embedding Terms into the Refinement Logic}}}
\end{figure}\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Embedding}}\
The hard work in $\mathsf{reflect}$ is done by the procedure
$\mathsf{embed}$, summarized in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:eight:embed} which
recursively translates \emph{implementation} terms into
\emph{logical} expressions.
The procedure translates literals like \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small 2}}} and \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{false}}}}}}
into the corresponding values in the logic
({i.e.}{} $2$ and $\mathit{false}$ respectively);
primitive function applications like \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}+{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{y}}}}}} or \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textless={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{b}}}}}}
into the corresponding terms or relations in the logic
({i.e.}{} $x+y$ and $a\leq b$ respectively); and
translates all other function calls into uninterpreted
function applications.
Let-binders can be translated by substitution as type
checking ensures that all reflected terms are terminating,
and hence, well defined.
Branches are translated into ternary choices.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{{Example:\penalty\ Reflection of $\texttt{sum}$}}\
Suppose that $e_{sum}$ is the implementation
of the \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}}}} function from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:eight:refl:ex}. Then
\begin{equation}\mathsf{embed}(e_{sum})\doteq\textit{if}\;{n=0}\;\textit{then}\;{0}\;\textit{else}\;{n+{{sum}}(n-1)}\end{equation}where the recursive call is translated into an uninterpreted
function application ${{sum}}(n-1)$.
\hfill$\square$
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Embedding Case Alternatives}}\
Pattern match terms $\texttt{{switch}}\;x\;\{a_{1};\ldots\}$
are embedded using $\mathsf{embAlts}(x,a_{1};\ldots)$
which translates them as nested ternary branches of
the form $\textit{if}\;{c_{1}}\;\textit{then}\;{e_{1}}\;\textit{else}\;{\ldots}$ where $c_{1}$ is
a logical predicate that is true when $x$ matches
the first constructor and $e_{1}$ the embedding of
the corresponding result.
We translate case alternatives using
two operators from the SMT decidable
theory of Algebraic Datatypes \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Nelson81}{}{}]}.
First, the \emph{test} predicate
$\mathit{is}_{D}(x)$ determines
whether $x$ equals to a term
$D(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})$.
Second, if $x$ equals $D(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})$
then the \emph{projection} function
$\mathit{proj}_{D}^{i}(x)$ equals $c_{i}$.
Thus, given the scrutinee $x$, the procedure $\mathsf{embAlts}$
translates each alternative, by using the test predicate
to determine if that alternative matches: if so, translating
its body with the binders replaced by the respective projections,
and otherwise, recursing on the remaining alternatives.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{{Example:\penalty\ Reflecting $\texttt{app}$}}\
Recall the definition of the list \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}}}}end
function from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:eight:proofs:ex}. Let $e_{app}$
denote the body of the implementation, {i.e.}{} \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{switch}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}...\textbraceright}}}.
Then
$$\mathsf{embed}(e_{app})\doteq\textit{if}\;{\mathit{is}_{{Nil}}({ys})}\;\textit{then}\;{{Nil}}\;\textit{else}\;{{Cons}({head}({xs}),{app}({tail}({xs}),{ys}))}$$where ${head}$ and ${tail}$ are the projections for the \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}}}}
constructor.
\hfill$\square$
\par\begin{figure}$$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{embAlts}&:&(X\times\overline{A})\rightarrow P\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\mathsf{embAlts}(x,a;\overline{a})&\doteq&\textit{if}\;{\mathit{is}_{D}(x)}\;\textit{then}\;{\mathsf{embAlt}(x,a)}\;\textit{else}\;{\mathsf{embAlts}(x,\overline{a})}\\[7.22743pt]
\mathsf{embAlts}(x,a)&\doteq&\mathsf{embAlt}(x,a)\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\mathsf{embAlt}&:&(X\times A)\rightarrow P\\[3.61371pt]
\mathsf{embAlt}(x,{D}({\overline{y}})\rightarrow{e})&\doteq&{\mathsf{embed}(e)}[\overline{y_{i}}:=\overline{\mathit{proj}_{D}^{i}(x)}]\\[7.22743pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}$$\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{10.5}}{Embedding Switch Alternatives into the Refinement Logic}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 10.5}}{Embedding Switch Alternatives into the Refinement Logic}}}
\end{figure}\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Declarative Checking}
\par\begin{figure}$\textrm{{Type Checking}}\hfill\framebox{$\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash e\Leftarrow t$}$
\begin{equation*}\frac{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash x=e_{1}:s_{1}/m\triangleright t_{1}\quad t_{1}^{\prime}=\mathsf{reflect}(x,e_{1},t_{1})\quad\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{t_{1}^{\prime}}}\vdash e_{2}\Leftarrow t_{2}}{\mathrm{\Gamma}\vdash\texttt{{def}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}/m}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}\Leftarrow t_{2}}{{{\textsc{Chk-Refl}}}}\end{equation*}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{10.6}}{Bidirectional Checking: Other rules from $\mathsf{\lambda_{\tau}}$ (Fig.\penalty\ \ref{fig:seven:check})}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 10.6}}{Bidirectional Checking: Other rules from $\mathsf{\lambda_{\tau}}$ (Fig.\penalty\ \ref{fig:seven:check})}}}
\end{figure}\par The rule {{{Chk-Refl}}} shown in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:eight:check} shows how we use
$\mathsf{reflect}$ to check reflect-binders $\texttt{{def}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}/m}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2}$.
First, we check that the definition $x=e_{1}$ is terminating with metric $m$.
Next, we reflect the definition of $e_{1}$ to strengthen the type $t_{1}$ to $t_{1}^{\prime}$
which is bound to $x$ in the environment used to check $e_{2}$.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{{Example:\penalty\ Using reflected $\texttt{sum}$}}\
Suppose that we wanted to check

where $\mathsf{embed}(e_{sum})$ is the embedding of ${sum}$'s body \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:embed:sum}.
We can then check $e$ in the environment extended by binding ${sum}$ to its reflected type:
$\mathrm{\Gamma};{{{sum}}\!:\!{t_{sum}}}\ \vdash e\Leftarrow t$.
\hfill$\square$
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Verification Conditions}
\par\begin{figure}$$\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{check}&:&(\mathrm{\Gamma}\times E\times T)\rightarrow C\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ \texttt{{def}}\;x={e_{1}}\!:\!{s_{1}/m}\;\texttt{{in}}\;e_{2},\ t_{2})&\doteq&c_{1}\wedge\forall{{x}\!:\!{t_{1}^{\prime}}}.\ {c_{2}}\\
\quad\mbox{where}&&\\
\quad\quad(t_{1},c_{1})&=&\mathsf{checkT}(\mathrm{\Gamma},\ x,\ e_{1},\ s_{1},\ m)\\
\quad\quad t_{1}^{\prime}&=&\mathsf{reflect}(x,e_{1},t_{1})\\[3.61371pt]
\quad\quad c_{2}&=&\mathsf{check}(\mathrm{\Gamma};{{x}\!:\!{t_{1}^{\prime}}},\ e_{2},\ t_{2})\\[3.61371pt]
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}$$\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{10.7}}{Algorithmic Checking for $\mathsf{\lambda_{\pi}}$, extends cases of\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:seven:algo}}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 10.7}}{Algorithmic Checking for $\mathsf{\lambda_{\pi}}$, extends cases of\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:seven:algo}}}}
\end{figure}\par Finally, we extend algorithmic VC generation function to account for reflected-binders,
as summarized in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:eight:algo}. Following the declarative rule, we first invoke
$\mathsf{checkT}$ to obtain a constraint $c_{1}$ that checks that the reflected definition
$x_{1}=e_{1}$ terminates with metric $m$. We then embed the definition
into the returned type to obtain the reflected type $t_{1}^{\prime}$ that is used to compute
the VC $c_{2}$ for $e_{2}$.

\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Discussion}
\par With $\mathsf{\lambda_{\pi}}$ we saw how to extend specifications with arbitrary
user-defined functions whose definitions are reflected into the
function's output type. This lets us prove propositions over those
functions, simply by writing programs where each \emph{use} of a
function instantiates, via the reflected output, the defintion
of the function at the given input.
Reflection dramatically expands the range of what refinements can
be used for, from enforcing invariants of values or data types,
to proving the functional correctness of {e.g.}{} various parallelism
constructs \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Vazou17}{}{}]}, dynamic information flow enforcement
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{lweb}{}{}]}, and laws governing replicated data types \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{vazou20}{}{}]}.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Axioms}}\
Other SMT based verifiers, notably {Dafny} and {F*} support
specifications over user defined functions by encoding their semantics
with universally-quantified \emph{axioms}.
Modern SMT solvers have sophisticated heuristics for instantiating these
axioms automatically using user specified \emph{triggers} \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Simplify}{}{}]}
yielding proofs where the user need not spell out all the intermediate
computations.
One drawback of the axiomatic approach is that reckless triggering
can cause the SMT engine to diverge \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Leino16}{}{}]}.
{Dafny} and {F*} use a notion of \emph{fuel} \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Amin2014ComputingWA}{}{}]}
to limit the instantiation to some fixed depth. While fuel can be quite effective
in practice, it lacks semantic completeness guarantees which are useful to characterize
what kinds of proofs can be successfully automated.
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Suter2011}{}{}]}, show completeness guarantees for a class
of \emph{sufficiently surjective} recursive functions, which,
informally, correspond to catamorphisms over algebraic
datatypes, {e.g.}{} functions like the \emph{measures} from $\mathsf{\lambda_{\delta}}$ that compute the length of a list or height of a tree.
Unfortunately, this result does not extend to arbitrary (terminating)
user-defined functions.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Proof by Logical Evaluation}}\
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Vazou18}{}{}]} observe that much of the verbosity in proofs
arises from spelling out long \emph{chains} of computations,
for example, the intermediate equalities in the proofs \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 2\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 3}}}}}}
(\lx@sectionsign\penalty\ \ref{sec:eight:refl:ex}) and \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{thm\textunderscore sum}}}}}} and \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app\textunderscore assoc}}}}}}
(\lx@sectionsign\penalty\ \ref{sec:eight:proofs:ex}).
Based on this observation, the paper introduces an the notion
of \emph{proof by logical evaluation} (PLE), an algorithm for
strengthening the antecedents in the VCs by automatically unfolding
the definition of recursive functions in way that is both terminating
and complete for equational chains, effectively enabling a form
of \emph{refinement-level computation}.
With PLE, we can prove \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 3\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 6}}}}}} simply as
{\@@listings@block{86}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 3\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 6}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}(){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}3{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}6]\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum\textunderscore 3\textunderscore eq\textunderscore 6}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}(){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}()\@lst@endline}}{}}}as the PLE algorithm unfolds the definitions of \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}}}} three times.
PLE greatly simplifies inductive proofs by eliminating the tedious
internal steps, allowing the proof to focus on the important
case-splitting and induction (recursion).
That, is the proof of \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{thm\textunderscore sum}}}}}} is reduced to
{\@@listings@block{87}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{thm\textunderscore sum}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{nat}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}[2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}+1)]\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{thm\textunderscore sum}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{switch}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}0{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}()\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{thm\textunderscore sum}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{n}}}-1)\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}which simply spells out the inductive skeleton, yielding a VC:
$$\begin{array}[]{llll}\lx@intercol\forall n.0\leq n\Rightarrow\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\lx@intercol n=0\Rightarrow\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\lx@intercol\quad 2\times{sum}(n)=n\times(n+1)\hfil\lx@intercol&\quad\quad\mbox{(base case)}\\
\wedge&\lx@intercol n\not=0\Rightarrow\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&&0\leq n-1\wedge n-1<n&\quad\quad\mbox{(metric)}\\
&\wedge&2\times{sum}(n-1)=(n-1)\times n\Rightarrow&\quad\quad\mbox{(ind hyp)}\\
&&\quad 2\times{sum}(n-1)=n\times(n+1)&\quad\quad\mbox{(ind case)}\end{array}$$\par Similarly, \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app\textunderscore assoc}}}}}} reduces to the below, where the proof need only
include the recursive skeleton, and the recursive call that establishes
the induction hypothesis for \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright}}}:
{\@@listings@block{88}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app\textunderscore assoc}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{list}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{list}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}),{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{zs}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{zs}}}))]\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}/{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{len}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app\textunderscore assoc}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{zs}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{switch}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Nil}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}()\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{app\textunderscore assoc}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{zs}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}Note that it is essential that the proof function
\emph{terminates}: otherwise we could simply write
circular ``proofs'' for \emph{any} proposition.
While the increased automation of PLE or axioms makes the
proofs much more concise, much work remains in devising
interfaces that can help the programmer structure their
proofs, and understand why particular proofs are rejected
by the type checker.
\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{chapter}{toc}{Conclusion}
\par In this article we saw a progression of languages that
incrementally implement a refinement type checker capable
of enforcing a full spectrum of correctness requirements
at compile time.
We conclude with some remarks on our experience developing
and using refinement type checkers over the past decade, and
point the way to some interesting and important directions
for future work.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{The Good: Types Enable Compositional Reasoning}
\par The great advantage of refinement types is that they
\emph{align the abstractions} that the analysis uses
with those that the programmer uses.
Consequently, they provide a simple syntax-directed
way to \emph{decompose} reasoning about complex values
like collections and higher-order functions or collections
into VCs over simple values like integers.
\par For example, consider the goal of verifying array-access
safety in the following function that sums the squares of
an array \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}}}}
{\@@listings@block{89}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sumSquares}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{array}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{int}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{int}}}\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sumSquares}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{get}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{i}}})\textasciicircum 2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{for}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{i}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{in}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}0{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}..{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{length}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}-{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}1]\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}The programmer might write the function using for-comprehension
syntax, but internally, the function would be translated into
{\@@listings@block{90}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sumSquares}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{array}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{int}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{int}}}\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sumSquares}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{is}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{range}}}(0,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{length}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}-{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}1);\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{body}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{i}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{get}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{i}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasciicircum{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}2{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright;\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{map}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{body}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{is}}});\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{sum}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}which makes use collections, higher-order functions and polymorphism
and collections. Each of these features are problematic for classical
program logics or program analysis, but are decomposed away by types.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@{\emph{Collections}}
First, we need a way to represent and establish
the fact that \emph{every} element of the collection \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{is}}}}}} was
a valid index for \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}}}}.
With program logics, this would require universally quantified
invariants which make for brittle SMT solving.
With program analysis, this would require tailoring sophisticated
abstract domains capable of performing shape analysis \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Gopan05}{}{}]}.
In contrast, refinements represent this fact
as $$\texttt{list}{({\texttt{{int}}}{{{\{{{\nu}\!:\!{0\leq\nu<{length}(x)}}\}}}})}$$the \emph{refinement} expressing the
constraint on a single \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{int}}}}}} and the
\emph{type constructor} \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{list}}}}}}
generalizing the constraint over
the collection.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@{\emph{Closures}}
Next, we need to represent the fact that the closure
\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{body}}}}}} accesses the array \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}}}} at various indices
\emph{supplied by} \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{map}}}}}}.
Classical ({e.g.}{} Floyd-Hoare) program logics do not
account for closures.
Modern logics like {e.g.}{} Hoare-Type Theory \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Nanevski}{}{}]}
or {Iris} \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{iris}{}{}]} do handle them, and tools like
{Dafny} permit reasoning about closures, but with
significantly more overhead.
In the program analysis world, this problem is a variant
of \emph{Control-flow Analysis} \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Shivers88}{}{}]} which is
complicated by reasoning about properties of free variables
({e.g.}{} \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}}}}) \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Might07}{}{}]}, which has resisted a robust solution
for several decades.
Types make the problem practically disappear: we ascribe
the type \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{i}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{nat}}}[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{i}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textless{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{length}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}})]{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{int}}}}}} to \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{body}}}}}} and then
(contra-variant) function subtyping naturally ensures that
only valid indices are in fact passed into the closure.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@{\emph{Polymorphism}}
Finally, functions like \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{map}}}}}} are ubiquitous: they are reused
in many different sites, and verification requires a way to
specify a contract that is both \emph{general} enough to account
for all the use-cases, yet \emph{precise} enough to facilitate
verification at each site.
In the program logic setting, this requires quantification
over the possible invariants \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Nanevski}{}{}]} which
makes verification less ergonomic, as the programmer must
spell out where the quantifier is added ({i.e.}{} generalized) and
removed ({i.e.}{} instantiated).
In the program analysis setting this the problem of
\emph{context sensitivity} which remains a notorious
source of imprecision \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Smaragdakis20}{}{}]}.
In contrast, type- and refinement- polymorphism provides
a natural solution: we need only (automatically) instantiate
the type variables $\alpha$ and $\beta$ in \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{map}}}}}}'s type
$${({\alpha}\rightarrow{\beta})}\rightarrow{{\texttt{list}{(\alpha)}}\rightarrow{\texttt{list}{(\beta)}}}$$with suitable refined types to enable context-sensitive verification.
\end{itemize}\par\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{The Bad: Reasoning about State}
\par A reader who has made it this far is clearly aware of the
elephant in the room: this article has hewed closely to
\emph{pure} programs, and entirely shied away from
discussing the topic of \emph{state}.
This is partly for exposition, partly because there
is already a substantial literature on the topic that
merits its own separate survey and partly because
\emph{precise} reasoning about imperative features
remains quite difficult.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Invariant References}}\
The simplest way to account for state is by introducing
a type
{\@@listings@block{91}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{type}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ref}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}/\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{is}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{co}}}-{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{and}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{contra}}}-{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{variant}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered/\@lst@endline}}{}}}denoting pointers to values of type \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}})}}} and then
use the standard API for accessing pointers:
{\@@listings@block{92}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{new}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ref}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}/\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{allocate}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered/\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{get}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ref}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}/\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{read}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered/\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{set}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ref}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}(){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}/\textasteriskcentered{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{read}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textasteriskcentered/\@lst@endline}}{}}}The machinery described in \lx@sectionsign\penalty\ \ref{sec:lang:three} scales up
to account for such references, but suffers from two problems.
First, it is \emph{flow-insensitive}: we end up assigning
a \emph{single} type to a reference throughout its lifetime
({e.g.}{} \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{nat}}}}}}) as opposed to different types at different points
as the reference is \emph{updated}.
Second, there is no way to use references in refinements,
as the values referred to might change.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Alias and Ownership Types}}\
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{AliasTypes}{}{}]} introduces a mechanism called \emph{Alias Types}
for reasoning about references and aliasing within a type system,
essentially by typing each pointer with a singleton \emph{location},
and tracking a separate \emph{store} that holds the values of each
location.
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{LiquidPOPL10}{}{}]} describes a way to combine logical predicates
with alias types to obtain a refinement type checker for C programs.
Similarly, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Chugh2012}{}{}]} shows how to combine alias types
with refinements to obtain a refinement system for JavaScript,
and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Bakst16}{}{}]} shows how to extend the approach to recursive
alias types thereby allowing refinements to specify and verify
complex invariants of linked data structures.
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Vekris16}{}{}]} shows how \emph{ownership types} \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Noble}{}{}]}
used to enforce reference immutability for Java \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{zibin}{}{}]}
can provide a more lightweight mechanism wherein immutable
refinements can be embedded within an imperative language
like TypeScript.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Monads}}\
None of the above methods scale up to handle
the combination of higher-order functions
\emph{and} state.
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Filliatre98}{}{}]} introduced a method for verifying
higher order programs with references, and \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{ynot}{}{}]}
introduce \emph{Hoare Type Theory} which encapsulates
that reasoning within \emph{Hoare Monads} which are
the usual state-transformers indexed by pre- and
post-conditions. This approach, while expressive,
is tricky to use as it lacks a way to algorithmically generate
verification conditions whose validity implies correctness.
{F*} elegantly solves this problem via the notion
of \emph{Dijkstra Monads} where the monad is indexed
by a single \emph{predicate transformer}: a function
that computes the (most general) heap pre-conditions
under which some desired post-condition will hold.
Crucially, the composition of the transformers yields
a mechanism for computing VCs. This method, combined
with SMT solvers' native support for McCarthy's axioms
for reasoning about arrays via the \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{select}}}}}} and \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{store}}}}}}
operations \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Mccarthy}{}{}]}, yields a powerful way to
verify higher-order stateful programs.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Separation Logic}}\
Finally, over the last two decades, Separation Logic
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{BI01, Reynolds02}{}{}]} has transformed how we think
about the verification of pointer-manipulating programs,
and is the basis for modern mechanized program logics
like {Iris} \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{iris}{}{}]} which has been used to
verify a range of sophisticated concurrent, pointer
manipulating algorithms using the {Coq} proof
assistant.
In future work it would be interesting to investigate
how refinements can be combined with the monadic approach
perhaps in combination with separation logic \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{kloos}{}{}]}
to yield simpler and easier to use tools for verifying
stateful programs.
\par\par\lx@frontmatter@fallback\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{The Ugly: Explaining Verification Failures}
\par The more sophisticated a static type system,
analysis or program logic, the more difficult
it is to \emph{explain} failures.
In our experience, the most challenging aspect
of using refinement types is that the high degree
of automation makes it difficult for beginners to
understand verification \emph{failures}, which can
arise in several modes.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Problem: The Implementation is Wrong}}\
The most common case is when the implementation
does not respect the specification. For example,
suppose \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{noDups}}}}}} is a measure (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:lang:five})
such that \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{noDups}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}})}}} holds when the list \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}}}}
contains no duplicates. Hence we can define a type
of \emph{unique lists} {i.e.}{} without any duplicates as
{\@@listings@block{93}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{type}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ulist}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{list}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}})[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{v}}}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{noDups}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{v}}})]\@lst@endline}}{}}}The following code fails to verify
{\@@listings@block{94}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{append}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ulist}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ulist}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ulist}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{let}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{append}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{switch}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Nil}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{append}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}))\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}Unfortunately the error message will simply say that
the result of \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},...)}}} is not a unique list and
the programmer may be quite puzzled as to why.
\par These failures are the easiest to explain, as one can
augment the type checker with some form of symbolic execution
to produce \emph{counterexamples} that describe why the property
does not hold \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{hallahan19}{}{}]}.
For example, in this case, the programmer could be given
a counterexample of the form
{\@@listings@block{95}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}(1,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Nil}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}(1,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Nil}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{append}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}(1,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}(1,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Nil}}}))\@lst@endline}}{}}}which would demonstrate a situation where the
output refinement fails to hold even though the
input requirements are met, and hopefully this
will provide a hint as to how to modify
the specification or the code.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\emph{Problem: The Specification is Weak}}\
A more vexing situation arises when the code
\emph{does} satisfy the specification, in that
there are no counterexamples, but where verification
fails because the specifications where not enough
to \emph{prove} the property.
Continuing with the \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ulist}}}}}} example from
above, suppose that from the counterexample,
the programmer has realized that the output
is unique only when the input lists have no
common elements. They will specify this extra
requirement as:
{\@@listings@block{96}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph3}{\color[rgb]{0.41,0.2,0.04}{val}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{append}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ulist}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}})\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ulist}}}(\textquoteright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}})[{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{intersect}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{empty}}}]\@lst@endline\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ulist}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}\textquoteright)\@lst@endline}}{}}}But now, imagine their dismay when the code is \emph{again}
rejected by the type checker.
Unfortunately, this time, we cannot find a counterexample
as indeed the function \emph{does} correctly implement
the given specification: the concatatenation of two
unique lists with no common elements always yields
a unique list.
\par In this case, verification fails because typechecking
is \emph{modular}. The only information that the type
checker has about the output of a function application,
is whatever was specified in the function's type.
Thus, in the above example consider the case
{\@@listings@block{97}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{append}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}}))\@lst@endline}}{}}}The signature for \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{append}}}}}} says that the (recursive)
call \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{append}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{xs}}}\textquoteright,{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{ys}}})}}} can return \emph{any} unique list,
including one that may possibly contain \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}}}}}, and so
the \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph5}{\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0.8}{Cons}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{x}}},...)}}} need not be a duplicate-free list.
Of course, this cannot happen \emph{in reality}
because the list \emph{output from} \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{append}}}}}} can
only contain elements from the lists \emph{passed into}
\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\small{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{append}}}}}}, but this information is absent from
the type signature, preventing verification.
\par This classic failure mode --- widely known
in the verification community as the difference
between invariants and \emph{inductive} invariants
--- is a significant stumbling block for programmers
as it is difficult to pinpoint exactly where the extra
information is needed, and what that information should be.
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{hallahan19}{}{}]} demonstrate an algorithm for generating
\emph{counterfactual counterexamples} that can pinpoint
the functions whose types need to be strengthened.
In future work it would interesting to see if ideas
from the synthesis literature \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{gulwani_synthesis}{}{}]}
can be used to suggest hints on how to strengthen
the specifications or code to facilitate proof,
or more broadly, help the programmer rapidly
build a robust mental model of the requirements
for formal verification.
This would go a long way towards flattening the
steep learning curve that remains the most daunting
hurdle limiting the broader adoption of formal
verification in software development.
